# Ora — Technical Documentation

*The deepest layer of Ora's documentation: the installed-system source of truth for engineers, architects, and evaluators. Two accepted companion documents — an accessible overview and a how-to guide — derive from this one.*

> **Composite implementation pin.** Chapters 1–17 retain their established audited pins except where a later currency note says otherwise. Chapter 18 describes the current standalone Programming implementation and supersedes the removed governed-process runtime, automatic entry router, process automation, triggers, inspectors, and lifecycle surfaces. This document is the vault canonical; its body-only repository mirror is `docs/technical-documentation.md`.

---

## What Ora Is

Ora is a multi-model orchestrator: a system that routes a single analytical task across several large language models — some running locally on the operator's machine, some reached over cloud APIs — and drives them through a fixed eight-step adversarial pipeline. The pipeline's job is reliability. A large language model asked to analyze, then asked to check its own analysis, tends to ratify what it already produced; Ora replaces that self-review with a structured contest between models and against persistent external state.

The reframe this whole system rests on is assisted human intelligence. Ora is not an autonomous analyst and does not present itself as one. It is an apparatus that makes a human operator's analytical work more reliable — the models supply generation and adversarial pressure; the human supplies the problem, the judgment about what matters, and the authority to act. I state this once and do not soften it later in the document: every "AI" component named here is a mechanism for assisting human intelligence, not a substitute for it.

Two distinctions run through the entire architecture and are worth fixing at the outset.

The first is **explicit Programming versus ordinary Inquiry**. Inquiry remains the foreground analytical pipeline and is never intercepted. Programming begins from its dedicated control, inspects a real Git repository, waits for one plan approval, executes on a task branch, and uses a fresh reviewer before committing accepted slices.

The second is an **explicit framework suite**. PEF, PIF, PFF, CFF, OFF, and DCA remain important named frameworks selected for analytical work:

- **PEF** — Problem Evolution Framework
- **PIF** — Process Inference Framework
- **PFF** — Process Formalization Framework
- **CFF** — Corpus Formalization Framework
- **OFF** — Output Formalization Framework
- **DCA** — Decision Clarity Analysis

Each is defined in its own canonical. Frameworks provide instructions and do not create Programming Runs, capability registrations, automatic triggers, or background execution.

## Why This Document Leads With Reliability

Reliability is the problem this architecture exists to demonstrate, so it is the lens the document leads with. The failure it targets is specific and well-documented: a single language model, asked to review or verify its own output, is an unreliable critic of itself. It re-derives the same reasoning, re-commits the same errors, and reports confidence it has not earned. Self-review is a race condition where the checker and the thing being checked share the same state — the check passes because it was never independent.

Ora's response is to make the checking independent, and to make it survive across turns. Four subsystems do the reliability-restoration work, and I frame each one throughout this document by the failure mode it prevents rather than by any performance figure:

- **The eight-step adversarial pipeline** prevents single-model self-ratification. It stages generation, cross-model challenge, consolidation, and formalization as distinct steps run by distinct models, so the critic is not the author.
- **Persistent vault state** prevents the amnesia failure — a model reasoning only from its context window, with no durable memory of prior conclusions, contradictions, or provenance. The vault is external, versioned state the pipeline reads and writes across sessions.
- **The capability gate** prevents unearned action. Before the system reaches for a tool or an external effect, a gate decides whether the request is well-formed enough to proceed; a malformed request is refused rather than run.
- **Oversight and governance** prevent silent drift — the slow divergence between what the system claims to do and what it actually does, unnoticed because nothing was watching.

One honesty constraint governs every reliability claim in this document. The framing is **qualitative**. I name the failure mode a component prevents; I do not attach a success rate, a per-step reliability budget, or a percentage, because the installed system does not instrument those numbers. Where such a budget would genuinely help an evaluator, I say plainly that it is not measured. A reliability claim you cannot trace to an instrumented metric in the code is a claim I do not make.

## How To Read This Document

This is the deepest layer of Ora's documentation — the source of truth for engineers, architects, and evaluators. Every subsystem chapter follows the same five-part depth model, in this order, so you can navigate any chapter by the question you are asking:

1. **Problem** — what fails without this subsystem; the concrete user or system risk it addresses.
2. **Design Decision & Tradeoffs** — the architectural move, the main alternative I rejected and why, the tradeoff accepted, and the open problems that remain.
3. **Implementation** — runtime entrypoints, data flow, key modules, config surfaces, and behavior under both normal operation and failure. This section braids *why* into *how* rather than separating them.
4. **Evidence & Verification** — the tests, parity checks, and packets that confirm the subsystem works, and — explicitly — the surfaces that are *not* covered.
5. **Expert Reading Notes** — what to inspect first, and where a future change is most likely to introduce drift.

Each chapter then closes with a sixth, mandatory subsection: **Platform compatibility**, exposing the per-subsystem platform checks (see the Platform-Support Statement below for why this is non-negotiable).

The chapters are grouped into six parts:

- **Part I — Install & Run** (chapters 1–3): getting Ora onto a machine and starting it.
- **Part II — Analytical Core** (chapters 4–7): the pipeline, the framework suite, and the model-orchestration machinery.
- **Part III — Knowledge, Tools & Media** (chapters 8–11): the vault, capability dispatch, and the media/visual subsystems.
- **Part IV — Supervision & Governance** (chapters 12–15): oversight, execution review, and the governance apparatus.
- **Part V — Operate & Extend** (chapters 16–17): running the installed system and extending it without forking core.
- **Part VI — Standalone Programming** (chapter 18): explicit entry, inspect-first planning, approval, real repository execution, fresh independent review, accepted-slice commits, evidence tools, and Git-native recovery.

Five appendices (A–E) follow the chapters. They are consolidated into this one document rather than split out, so that the source of truth stays in a single place.

**User-facing nomenclature (2026-07-11).** The interface concepts this document describes carry new user-facing names, decided after the pin: a working session is a **Dialogue** (formerly a conversation); the text-input pane is the **Inquiry** pane; the text-output pane is the **Findings** pane; the visual/canvas pane, when discussed as part of a Dialogue, is the **Exhibits** pane (in pure image-rendering contexts "image pane" or "visual pane" remains acceptable); the small side-model chat panes in the upper right are the **Aside**; the conversation/engram/file browse modal, with its sidebar access point, is the **Library**; and the default project — the container work lands in when no project is selected — is the **Commons** (formerly General). This document is code-level and pinned to commit `7a5e8f40`, so it deliberately keeps the internal identifiers and the UI strings as they exist at that commit — `conversation.json`, the `conversations` ChromaDB collection, `conversation_memory.py`, the `sidebar` model slot, `sidebar.js`, `visual-panel.js`, endpoint names, and quoted UI labels are all unchanged and correct as written. Read "conversation" in code-adjacent prose as the mechanism behind a Dialogue, and the nexus id `general` as the internal identifier of the Commons at this document's pinned commit. **Currency note (2026-07-11, post-pin):** the code-level rename has since landed on `main` at commit `062b67a7` — the internal nexus id is now `commons`, with the legacy id `general` still recognized everywhere, permanently. This document's chapter bodies remain pinned to `7a5e8f40` and are not re-audited against `062b67a7`; this parenthetical exists only so a reader comparing this document to a current checkout isn't misled by a claim that was true at the pin and has since changed. The user-facing documents ([[Guide — Using Ora]], [[Reference — Ora Accessible Overview]]) use the new names throughout.

## Platform-Support Statement

The primary tested target is **macOS on Apple Silicon**. That is the platform the installed system at commit 7a5e8f40 was developed and run on, and it is the only platform for which I will say a surface "works" on the strength of evidence. Every other platform — Windows-native, WSL, generic Linux — is somewhere between untested and partial, and each chapter's Platform compatibility subsection labels the specific surfaces accordingly. I do not label a non-mac surface "works" without evidence; where a surface was not actually run off-Mac, it is labeled "untested."

There is a real portability apparatus, but it is partial, not complete. It consists of `orchestrator/runtime_paths.py` (path resolution that avoids hardcoded home directories), `orchestrator/platform_check.py` (runtime platform detection), `scripts/check-portability.py` (a static scan for portability hazards), and `orchestrator/tests/test_portability.py` (the portability test suite). This apparatus is the mechanism by which Ora is being made cross-platform; at 7a5e8f40 it does not yet certify any non-mac platform as fully supported.

Three concrete hazards currently block a clean non-mac release, and I name them here because they recur in individual chapters:

1. **Hardcoded interpreter path in the server.** `server/app.py` shells out to `/opt/homebrew/bin/python3` — the Apple Silicon Homebrew path — at lines 14130 and 14163 (the direct-API and OpenRouter model-refresh subprocesses). On any machine without Homebrew at that exact path, those subprocesses fail.
2. **Tracked `/Users/oracle` roots in config.** Committed configuration files carry absolute paths under `/Users/oracle` — specifically `config/routing-config.json` and `config/capabilities.json`, which are git-tracked. (`config/models.json` carries the same absolute roots but is gitignored, so it does not ship; the tracked hazard is confined to those two files.) Those roots are baked into version-controlled state, so a fresh checkout on another machine or under another username inherits paths that do not exist.
3. **Flag-incomplete `start.bat`.** The Windows start script (`start.bat`) is a genuine mirror of `start.sh` — it locates a Python interpreter via the `py` launcher and falls back to `python` — but it is incomplete relative to the flag and startup surface the shell script exercises. It starts a server; it does not yet reach parity with the Unix launcher.

## How To Read the Status Labels

Status honesty is load-bearing in this document. Every feature is labeled with one of these exact words, and the word means precisely what it says:

- **implemented** — built, landed in `~/ora` main at 7a5e8f40, and operating.
- **partial** — built but not fully live: incomplete, or gated behind a feature flag that defaults OFF. A flag that defaults OFF is *partial*, never *implemented*, no matter how complete the code behind it is.
- **planned** — designed and intended, not yet built.
- **deferred** — consciously postponed; a decision was made not to build it now.
- **unmerged-code-review** — implemented in an unmerged worktree and sitting at the code-review gate; not merged, not installed, not landed.
- **stale** — present in the tree or the docs but superseded, orphaned, or contradicted by current code. Where a docstring or CLAUDE.md contradicts the code, I trust the code and, only where it is load-bearing, note the drift.

The load-bearing application of these labels: **Execution-Review Phases 1–4 are landed** in `~/ora` main at 7a5e8f40 (the commit itself is the Phase 1–4 portability retrofit, PR #181). **Execution-Review Phase 5 is unmerged-code-review** — it is implemented, but it lives in a separate worktree (`~/ora-worktrees/phase5-impl`, branch `execution-review-phase5-impl`, at 82553162) sitting at the code-review gate. Phase 5 is not merged to main, not installed, and not landed at 7a5e8f40 (the worktree path/branch/SHA are out-of-pin metadata; Phase 5 has since merged post-pin as PR #185). The installed system as described at 7a5e8f40 has Execution-Review coverage ending at Phase 4. I never call Phase 5 "installed," "landed," or "design-only"; it is code that exists and is waiting on merge.

## Core vs. Project (the generic-prototype boundary)

Ora is a generic prototype, and its core stays generic on purpose. Project-specific code — anything that encodes the assumptions of one particular publication, client, or use case — lives in that project's own `ora-project/` plugin directory and is registered to core through a pointer file, never inlined into `~/ora/orchestrator/` or the core config. This boundary is what keeps the generic prototype reusable across projects instead of accreting one project's special cases into the shared substrate; the mechanics of the plugin dir, the pointer-file registry, and the extension seams are covered in the **Project Plugin Convention** chapter.


---

# Part I — Install & Run

## 1. Installation & Runtime Profiles

### Problem

A `git clone` of Ora is not a running system. The checkout gives you source, a set of seed configuration files under `config/`, and a pile of `.md` frameworks. It does not give you a populated model-routing configuration, an inference-engine choice, API credentials, a launcher, or any evidence that a single model call will complete. Six concrete failures sit between the checkout and a working install, and the install/runtime-profile subsystem exists to prevent each one.

First, **empty routing**. The pipeline reads `config/routing-config.json` for endpoints and slot assignments; a fresh clone's slots are not populated against the live provider catalog, so without a populate step every slot resolves to nothing and the first prompt dies with no model to call.

Second, **wrong engine**. Ora runs local models through MLX (Apple's Metal-backed inference runtime) on Apple Silicon and through Ollama (a Go-based local-model server) everywhere else. A clone ships `engine: "auto"` sentinels that are correct on no machine until something resolves them to the real engine for that host. Ship an unresolved config and the server tries to load MLX weights on a Linux box with no Metal.

Third, **silent cost misconfiguration**. The desktop auto-populate step and the server rewrite each make a deliberate cheapest-defensible choice per slot. Get this wrong — let a paid model sit silently at the head of a fallback chain — and a background publication loop bills real tokens on every call. This is not hypothetical; the server-side free-chain design is documented in-code as a response to a runaway-process incident.

Fourth, **no launcher**. `server.py` needs `ORA_HOME`, a resolvable Python interpreter, a set of runtime feature-flag environment variables, and a stale-process kill before it can bind a port. A user typing `python3 server/app.py` gets none of that.

Fifth, **non-idempotent, non-resumable installs**. A source install makes several network round-trips (catalog refresh, registry sync, smoke round-trip). Any of them can fail transiently. Without recorded state, a failure halfway through forces a full restart from a partially-mutated tree.

Sixth, **the porting trap**. Ora was built on and for one machine (Apple M4 Max, macOS). Every hardcoded `~/ora`, every `pkill`, every `/opt/homebrew/bin/python3` shebang is a latent failure on any other platform. Without a single place that resolves roots and a standing linter that flags platform-specific constructs, portability rots silently, and you only discover it when someone runs the clone on Windows.

The subsystem's job is to carry a checkout across these six gaps to a server that binds a port and answers a prompt — and to do so on more than one class of machine without pretending the non-Mac paths are as tested as the Mac one.

### Design Decision & Tradeoffs

**The decision: three purpose-built install surfaces, not one universal installer.** Ora ships (1) a nine-step Python desktop installer, `scripts/install.py`, for the macOS Solo profile; (2) a Bash headless installer, `scripts/install-server.sh`, for a Linux API-only server; and (3) macOS `.app` bundles plus shell launchers (`start.sh`, `Ora.app`) for runtime launch. These are three code paths with different languages, different assumptions, and no shared install-orchestration module.

The rejected alternative is the obvious one: a single cross-platform installer that detects the environment and branches. It loses here, and the repo carried the fossil of the alternative until that fossil was retired: `installer/`'s 21-file natural-language layered installer spec, deleted because it gave executable-looking instructions for a system that no longer exists. Git history keeps it; the only thing left under `installer/` is `macos/`, the launchd plist template and app-launcher shim `scripts/ora-launchd.sh` actually reads. The reason a universal installer fails is that the three targets do not share a spine. The desktop path is interactive, keychain-backed, and installs local MLX weights; the server path is non-interactive, env-file-backed, and forbids local models entirely; the `.app` path is not an installer at all but a double-click runtime launch. A universal installer would be a tree of platform conditionals whose branches are each exercised by exactly one environment — the worst possible test-surface-to-code ratio. Three focused tools each do one job and can each be read end-to-end.

**The tradeoff is drift.** Three independent paths with no shared module guarantee that a change in one does not propagate to the others, and the installed system demonstrates the cost. The desktop installer auto-populates the **`budget`** preset (`scripts/install.py:1560,1572`) and then bakes all four preset cards through the runtime's own baker; `docs/install-guide.md` said "Optimum preset" for months, a name `runtime_paths.py:865` has never carried — it defines `PRESET_NAMES = ("free", "budget", "speed", "premium")`, with no "optimum" at all. That one is now corrected on both sides. The server installer's own docstring (`install_server_config.py:11-16`) claims it points slots at `anthropic-api-haiku` and `anthropic-api-sonnet-4-5`, while the code directly below it (`SERVER_SLOT_ASSIGNMENTS`, lines 102-114) writes **free OpenRouter chains**. `install-server.sh` and `docs/cloud-ora-install.md` name `qwen3.6-plus`/`qwen3.6-35b-a3b`. Three descriptions of one behavior, all wrong, none load-bearing at runtime because the code is authoritative — but each a trap for a reader who trusts the prose. Trust the code.

**Second decision: Solo is the only supported profile; Hybrid and Organization are reserved names that hard-refuse.** `DEPLOYMENT_PROFILES` (`install.py:222-243`) declares all three, but only Solo carries `supported_now: True`. Selecting either other profile does not degrade or approximate — it refuses with an explicit "reserved for future Ora installs, not supported today" message (`install.py:1017-1021`). This is the same explicit-not-implemented-over-silent-wrong-answer discipline the system applies elsewhere, here at the install boundary. The alternative — quietly treating Hybrid as Solo — was rejected because the profiles differ in `api_pool_size` and concurrency assumptions; a silent alias would produce a subtly mis-configured multi-machine deployment that fails later and further from the cause. The open problem here is load-bearing: Hybrid and Organization appear in user-facing framing ("permanently free local AI") but the concurrency architecture that would unblock them (gated on "G1.27 network discovery and concurrency validation") is unbuilt. The names are promises the installed system cannot yet keep, and it says so at the refusal point rather than pretending.

**Third decision: centralize path resolution in one module as the porting strategy.** `orchestrator/runtime_paths.py` is the single source for every Ora root — `ORA_HOME`, `VAULT`, `CONVERSATIONS`, the runtime overlay dirs — each built from `os.path.expanduser("~")` and each overridable by an environment variable (lines 14-38). It also owns `norm_key`/`within_base` (case- and separator-normalized path comparison with a directory boundary, lines 49-74) and a cross-platform advisory file lock (`fcntl` on POSIX, `msvcrt` on Windows, guarded imports, lines 87-134). The rejected alternative — hardcode `~/ora` at each call site — is exactly the state the codebase is climbing out of, and the climb is incomplete: `orchestrator/platform_check.py:19-20` still hardcodes `WORKSPACE = os.path.expanduser("~/ora/")` and reads `routing-config.json` from that literal path rather than through `runtime_paths.routing_config_path()`. That is a real, in-tree instance of the drift the module was built to eliminate, and it is a reading-notes hotspot below.

**Fourth decision: the server writes an all-free fallback chain with no paid safety net.** `install_server_config.py` rewrites every slot to a chain of `:free` OpenRouter models (`PREMIUM_BUCKET_CHAIN` for writing slots, `FAST_BUCKET_CHAIN` for utility, lines 50-113) and states plainly (lines 61-66): "No paid safety net on the writing-slot chain. If every free option exhausts simultaneously, the call fails … but no token charges. This is a deliberate cost-protection choice after a runaway-process incident." Note that the file's own header comment (lines 42-44) contradicts this, describing paid models sitting "at the bottom of the chain as a safety net" — stale narration the code below overrides. The failure mode the code actually prevents is a runaway background loop silently rolling over to paid models. The tradeoff is availability: a simultaneous free-tier exhaustion drops the task rather than paying to complete it. For an async, non-interactive publication cadence that is the correct trade; for an interactive desktop it would not be, which is one more reason the two install paths are separate.

### Implementation

**Desktop install — `scripts/install.py`.** The entrypoint is `main()` (lines 1839-1901). A `models` subcommand short-circuits to `scripts/local_models.py` for post-install model management (lines 1843-1844, 1790-1801); a `converters` subcommand re-runs the Pandoc/Typst provisioning alone; otherwise the run drives a fixed nine-step pipeline (lines 1877-1885): preflight → dependencies → converters → profile select → catalog refresh → registry sync → auto-populate → smoke test → external-API orientation. Each step is a function returning `bool`; the loop halts on the first `False` with `INSTALL_HALTED at step '<name>'` and exits non-zero (lines 1888-1894). On success it emits the deterministic `COMPLETION_MARKER` — `"INSTALL_COMPLETE: 0 warnings, 0 errors"` (line 125, emitted 1896) — which the test protocol greps for.

Idempotence and resumability run through `install-state.json`. `save_state` records `steps_completed` and timestamps (`load_state`/`save_state`, lines 479-495); `--resume` skips any step already in that set (lines 1889-1891); `--reset` unlinks the state and log but explicitly leaves vault, conversations, and downloaded models alone (`reset_install`, lines 512-519). `--dry-run` threads through every step as a preview flag.

Step behavior, braided with why: **Preflight** (step 1, lines 722-786) resolves every user-storage root before any install work begins, and its hard gates are Python ≥3.11, ≥5GB free disk, write permission at both the checkout and the resolved Documents directory, and a vault path that already exists or can be created. Two of its checks are deliberately *not* gates. Missing document-conversion dependencies only produce a note, because step 2 installs them from `requirements.txt` and halting here is what once made the published install path unusable on a clean machine. And an unreachable OpenRouter warns and proceeds (lines 756-771), printing the same catalog-outage policy step 5 implements — the prediction and the behavior come from one constant, so pre-flight can never promise a survivable outage that the install then halts on. **Catalog refresh** (step 5) and **registry sync** (step 6) subprocess out to `refresh-catalog.py` and `sync_model_registry.py`, and neither is fatal for the ordinary failure. A refresh that cannot complete — missing script, 120-second timeout, non-zero exit — falls back to the catalog packaged with the checkout and states plainly what is stale about it (`_catalog_refresh_fallback`, lines 1062-1088); the install halts there only when `_catalog_baseline` finds no usable catalog to fall back on, because every later step picks its models out of that file. Registry sync is non-fatal in every case (lines 1155-1209), because `boot.py` can fall back to routing-config capability flags if the registry is absent. Step 6 also gates the empirical vision-capability probe behind `ORA_INSTALL_PROBE=1` — default-appending `--no-probe` (lines 1187-1188) to keep installs non-flaky. This is why the probe is **partial (flag-gated-off)**, not implemented-on: the default install does not run it. **Auto-populate** (step 7, lines 1545-1598) writes `config/configurations/user-pipeline.json` from the `budget` preset and bakes the four preset cards the Models pane promises; it halts if a promised preset is missing or holds a model in no slot afterwards, and applies that same standard to `user-pipeline` itself, because that is the configuration Ora actually serves from. **Smoke test** (step 8, lines 1678-1736) populates a `smoke-test-free.json` config, extracts candidate free models (`_extract_smoke_models`), and — only if an OpenRouter key is present in env or keyring — walks up to five of those candidates until one completes a real chat round-trip (`_openrouter_smoke_call`, lines 1618-1675). An auth failure (401/403) stops the install; a mere rate-limit or unavailability does not, because free models flake and that is not the user's misconfiguration. **External-API orientation** (step 9) prints the recommended key set and optionally opens provider pages.

The install-time comment lag is worth flagging inline: `install.py:1107-1121` still narrates an "Artificial Analysis intelligence-index check" in the imperative, while the surrounding docstring correctly states that Chatbot Arena replaced AA as the intelligence source and AA is now optional. The code path is correct; the comment block is stale.

**Runtime launch — `start.sh`.** The Mac launcher (lines 1-104) exports `ORA_HOME` (line 12) — the note at lines 5-10 explains why: a path helper once fell back to a literal `"~"` when `expanduser` could not resolve `HOME`, spawning stray `~/ora/...` directories, so exporting `ORA_HOME` guarantees resolution even if the passwd lookup is transiently unavailable. It then exports seven runtime feature-flag variables (`ORA_RAG_SELECTION`, `ORA_RAG_FIT_GATE_SLOT`, `ORA_WEB_EXTRACTION`, `ORA_RUNTIME_ENGRAM_PROMOTION`, `ORA_RUNTIME_ENGRAM_AUTOCOMMIT`, `ORA_DELIVERABLE_SCRUB`, `ORA_OR_STATS`, lines 18-57), `pkill`s a stale `server/app.py` (line 60), resolves Python (Homebrew then PATH, lines 63-71), starts the server with `--oversight` unless `--no-oversight` is passed (lines 76-81), polls ports 5000-5010 for a `/health` response (lines 83-100), and opens a browser via an `open`/`xdg-open`/`start` cascade (lines 89-95).

At 7a5e8f40 the tracked launcher is `start.sh` — it exports `ORA_HOME` plus seven `ORA_*` runtime flags (`start.sh:12-58`) and launches the server backgrounded (`"$PYTHON" server/app.py --oversight &`), selecting `/opt/homebrew/bin/python3` with a system-`python3` fallback. `stop.sh` is a two-line script: shebang plus `pkill -f 'server/app.py'`. The author's Mac additionally carries two **untracked** launch artifacts that are NOT part of this commit (a fresh clone does not contain them): a `run-ora-server.sh` launchd wrapper and the `Ora.app` / `Ora Models.app` bundles. They diverge from `start.sh` on which flags they export — a real operational drift hazard on that machine — but because they are untracked their exact contents are not verifiable from the pinned commit and are not documented as installed-system fact here.

**Engine resolution — `orchestrator/platform_check.py`.** `detect_platform()` (lines 23-34) returns MLX only when `os == "Darwin"` and `arch == "arm64"`; every other combination gets Ollama. `startup_check()` (lines 62-103) reads `routing-config.json`, rewrites each `engine: "auto"` local endpoint to the resolved engine (adding `http://localhost:11434` for Ollama), and writes the file back. This is the resolve-once-and-persist move: settle the engine at boot rather than re-deriving it on every model call. RAM detection (`get_system_ram_gb`, lines 37-59) branches `sysctl` / `/proc/meminfo` / `wmic`.

**Server install chain — `install-server.sh` → `install_server_config.py` → `install_server_smoke.py`.** The shell script (`set -euo pipefail`) hard-guards on `uname -s == Linux` and tells a Mac user to use `install.py` instead (lines 82-84). It apt-installs the dep set, creates a `.venv`, pip-installs the Linux Python subset (no `mlx-lm`; the `DEPS` array names `flask`/`flask-cors`, `chromadb`, the `anthropic`/`openai`/`google-generativeai` SDKs, and `keyring` — the last only to silence a boot.py import, lines 138-158), installs Ollama and pulls `nomic-embed-text`, runs the config rewrite, prompts for keys into `~/.config/ora-server.env` at `chmod 600`, and runs the smoke test. `install_server_config.py::main()` (lines 160-236) synthesizes any missing free-model endpoints, validates that every needed id is present (fails with exit 2 if not), backs up the original to `routing-config.json.pre-server-install` exactly once, then overwrites `slot_assignments`, `default_endpoint`, `gear4_overrides`, `buckets`, and `operational_context` to API-only. `install_server_smoke.py` runs five assertions (`main()`, lines 92-99): routing loads via `boot.load_routing_config`, every slot resolves to an enabled/active endpoint, no slot references `local-mlx-*`/`local-ollama-*`, `OPENROUTER_API_KEY` is in the environment, and `chromadb` imports — exiting non-zero on any failure.

One implementation smell inside `install_server_config.py`: `NEW_FREE_ENDPOINTS` is defined twice (lines 72-76, then 88-100). The second binding shadows the first, so only the 11-entry list is ever used; the 3-entry list is dead. It is harmless because the second is a superset, but it is a landmine for the next editor who changes the wrong one.

**Config surfaces** the subsystem owns: `install-state.json` / `install.log` (desktop progress); `routing-config.json` (+ `.pre-server-install` backup); `config/configurations/user-pipeline.json` and `smoke-test-free.json`; `config/local-models.json`; `~/.config/ora-server.env`; the `ORA_*` environment variables; and the system keychain (`keyring`, service `ora`) for desktop keys — bypassed entirely on the server, which uses the env file.

### Evidence & Verification

**What is instrumented and passes.** `install_server_smoke.py` is the only automated install verifier: five hard assertions, run as step 6/6 of the server install, non-zero exit on any failure. `check-portability.py` is a standing linter over the whole tree — a RULES table of regexes for `pkill`, hardcoded Homebrew paths, `osascript`, `launchctl`, `/tmp`, `~/Library`, and similar, with a platform-guard downgrade pass (a file that branches on `sys.platform`/`platform.system()` downgrades its own matches) and an allowlist of intentionally Mac-only files (`ALLOWED_MAC_ONLY_FILES`, lines 178-190, which includes `Ora.app/Contents/MacOS/ai`, `Ora Models.app/Contents/MacOS/ai`, `start.sh`, `stop.sh`). `check_missing_files` (lines 275-290) would flag an absent `start.bat`/`stop.bat` as CRITICAL — but at this commit both `start.bat` and `stop.bat` exist and are tracked, so that rule does not fire. A live run at commit 7a5e8f40 reports **CRITICAL: 11, HIGH: 32, MEDIUM: 69, LOW: 59, INFO: 8**, exit 1 on the CRITICALs. The 11 CRITICALs are nine `homebrew-python-hardcode` findings (`uninstall/uninstall.sh:12`, three in `output_runtime.py`, and others) plus two macOS test-string matches in `test_shell_profiles.py` — not a missing Windows launcher. This is the commit whose message is "macOS+Windows portability retrofit," and the Windows launchers landed here.

**What is protocol-only, not automated.** `docs/install-testing.md` is a manual clean-room protocol: a three-environment matrix (two Mac Minis plus a Parallels Windows 11 ARM VM), a Solo-only ship criterion, and a pass checklist keyed on the `COMPLETION_MARKER` grep plus an `invoke_chat` round-trip. **There is no automated end-to-end test for `install.py`, `platform_check.py`, `runtime_paths.py`, or `local_models.py`.** The cross-platform advisory lock in `runtime_paths.locked_file` has guarded POSIX/Windows branches but no visible unit test exercising the `msvcrt` path. No reliability budget or per-step success rate is instrumented anywhere in this subsystem — the smoke checks are pass/fail assertions, not measured rates. Any percentage would be invented; none is claimed.

**Explicitly untested surfaces** (do not read the code's cross-platform branches as evidence they run):
- The Parallels Windows-native install gate has not been passed. Windows-native and WSL desktop installs are documented-but-unverified, even though a Windows launcher (`start.bat`/`stop.bat`) now ships.
- `wmic` RAM detection (`platform_check.py:50-56`, `local_models.py:113-121`) is untested and at risk — `wmic` is deprecated and removed in recent Windows 11 builds.
- The `msvcrt` file-lock branch, `norm_key` Windows normalization, `%USERPROFILE%` root resolution, and the `keyring` Windows/Linux backends are code-present, run-absent.
- `install-server.sh` is exercised on Ubuntu 24.04 (Hetzner CX33) only; its `apt-get` assumption means Fedora/Arch need porting, and WSL-Ubuntu is plausible-but-untested.

**Drift the reader must not trust the docs over the code on:** the server slot picks (free OpenRouter chains in `install_server_config.py` vs the anthropic/qwen names in its own docstring and `install-server.sh`/`cloud-ora-install.md`); the server embedding default (`nomic-embed-text` on the server vs `bge-m3` (1024-dim) on the Mac desktop per CLAUDE.md 2026-05-29 — a genuine divergence that risks a ChromaDB dimension mismatch on any Mac↔server collection round-trip, and one that is *undocumented in the install path*); and `docs/README.md`'s reference to a nonexistent `Local AI.command` desktop launcher.

### Expert Reading Notes

**Read these first, in this order.** (1) `install.py:222-243` and `:996-1028` — the `DEPLOYMENT_PROFILES` table and the profile gate tell you the whole Solo-only policy and its refusal semantics in two screens. (2) `install.py:1877-1885` — the nine-step pipeline list is the spine; every step function hangs off it. (3) `runtime_paths.py:14-46` — the root-resolution block is the contract every other module depends on for pathing; if you are porting Ora, this is the file. (4) `install_server_config.py:50-113` — `PREMIUM_BUCKET_CHAIN`, `FAST_BUCKET_CHAIN`, and `SERVER_SLOT_ASSIGNMENTS` are the actual server routing; read the chains, ignore the docstring and the header comment. (5) `platform_check.py:23-34,62-103` — the engine fork and the `auto`-resolution rewrite.

**Where future changes most likely cause drift.** The three-install-path split is the standing drift generator; the already-diverged descriptions (preset name, server slots, embedding model) are what happens when someone edits one path and not the paired doc. Treat any change to a preset name, a slot-assignment id, a routing-config schema key, or an endpoint id as a multi-file change: grep all three install paths plus `docs/` before you consider it done. Specifically —

- **`platform_check.py:19-20`** hardcodes `~/ora/` instead of calling `runtime_paths.routing_config_path()`. It is the clearest in-tree violation of the centralized-paths decision and the first thing to fix if `ORA_HOME` ever needs to point somewhere non-default; today, engine resolution silently ignores `ORA_HOME`.
- **The Mac launch paths disagree on environment.** Only `start.sh` (7 flags, `--oversight`) is tracked at this commit; the author's machine also has an untracked `run-ora-server.sh` launchd wrapper and an `Ora.app` bundle that export different flag sets. They will drift further with every new feature flag unless the flag set is factored into a shared sourced file, and a feature enabled only in `start.sh` is invisible to anyone who launches via the untracked wrapper or app bundle. (The untracked launchers are not in the commit, so a fresh clone launches only via `start.sh`.)
- **`run-ora-server.sh` is not in the commit.** A clean checkout of 7a5e8f40 does not contain it; it exists only as an untracked file on the author's machine (a 2026-07-02 recreation), so it is machine-local and can differ per machine. The committed, reproducible launcher is `start.sh`.
- **`install_server_config.py`'s duplicated `NEW_FREE_ENDPOINTS`** — edit the second definition (lines 88-100), never the first; the first is dead.
- **`installer/`'s natural-language layer set is gone.** The 21 Markdown files (manifest, appendix, phase1/, phase2/) described an installer that was never the live path and were retired rather than reconciled; git history holds them. `installer/macos/` stays — it holds the launchd plist template and the app-launcher shim `scripts/ora-launchd.sh` installs.

**Blast radius.** A preset-name change touches `install.py`, `runtime_paths.PRESET_NAMES`, `auto-populate-configuration.py`, and the docs. A routing-config schema key change touches all three install paths, `platform_check.startup_check`'s rewrite, and `boot.load_routing_config` (which the server smoke test calls). An endpoint-id change in the free chains touches `install_server_config.py`'s chains *and* the daily `ora-audit-models.py` catalog check that guards them.

### Platform compatibility

Per-subsystem platform checks at commit 7a5e8f40. "Works" appears only where the path was actually run; every non-Mac surface that was not exercised is labeled untested.

- **Desktop installer (`scripts/install.py`):** macOS = works (primary tested path). Windows-native = untested (`py -3 scripts\install.py`; documented as "intended, pending Parallels clean-install test", `install-guide.md`). WSL = untested (documented fallback, not in the formal matrix). Linux-desktop = untested/best-effort (explicitly "not the primary tested desktop path"). Pure-Python 3.11+, no shell dependencies, so the command is portable in principle — but do not claim it works off-Mac.
- **`start.sh` launcher:** macOS = works. Windows-native = has a committed `start.bat` mirror (uses `%USERPROFILE%`, the `py` launcher, and `taskkill`) but that `.bat` is untested — no Parallels run has exercised it. WSL = untested. Linux = untested (`xdg-open` branch present, but `pkill` is POSIX and the whole path was not run).
- **`stop.sh`:** macOS = works. WSL / Linux = works in principle (`pkill -f` is POSIX). Windows-native = committed `stop.bat` mirror present, untested.
- **`run-ora-server.sh` (launchd wrapper):** macOS = works (and untracked in git). Windows-native / WSL / Linux = n/a (`#!/bin/zsh -l`, hardcoded `/opt/homebrew/bin/python3`, launchd job — Mac-only by construction).
- **`Ora.app` / `Ora Models.app` bundles:** macOS = works. Windows-native / WSL / Linux = n/a (macOS `.app` format, hardcoded Homebrew Python shebang, `subprocess open` — no cross-platform equivalent shipped).
- **`install-server.sh` (Linux server):** Linux (Debian/Ubuntu, `apt`) = works (exercised on Hetzner CX33, Ubuntu 24.04). macOS = refuses by design (`uname` guard → use `install.py`). Non-apt Linux (Fedora/Arch) = needs-porting. WSL-Ubuntu = untested. Windows-native = n/a.
- **Python interpreter resolution:** macOS = works (`/opt/homebrew/bin/python3` then PATH in `start.sh`). Linux / WSL = works (PATH `python3`). Windows-native = untested (`start.bat` implements the `py -3` → `python` fallback, but no Parallels run has exercised it; the app bundle and launchd wrapper hardcode the Homebrew path).
- **RAM detection (`platform_check.get_system_ram_gb`, `local_models.get_total_ram_gb`):** macOS (`sysctl`) = works. Linux / WSL (`/proc/meminfo`) = works. Windows-native (`wmic`) = untested and at risk — `wmic` is deprecated/removed in recent Windows 11 builds.
- **Engine resolution (`platform_check.detect_platform`):** macOS/Apple-Silicon → MLX = works. Every non-Apple-Silicon host → Ollama at `localhost:11434` = untested on desktop (the non-Mac engine path was never exercised outside the server install, which forbids local models entirely). MLX is Apple-hardware-bound.
- **Advisory file lock (`runtime_paths.locked_file`):** macOS / Linux / WSL (`fcntl.flock`) = works. Windows-native (`msvcrt.locking`) = untested (branch present, guarded import, no test). Falls to best-effort single-process if neither primitive is available.
- **Path root resolution + `norm_key`/`within_base` (`runtime_paths`):** macOS / Linux / WSL = works (built from `expanduser("~")`, env-overridable). Windows-native = untested (`%USERPROFILE%` resolution and case/separator normalization are coded but not run).
- **Keyring credential storage (desktop):** macOS Keychain = works. Windows Credential Locker / Linux Secret Service = untested (backends exist in the `keyring` lib; not exercised here). The server path bypasses keyring entirely.
- **Server key store `~/.config/ora-server.env` (chmod 600):** Linux / WSL = works (POSIX `chmod`, `read -rs`). macOS / Windows-native = n/a (server path is Linux-only).
- **Browser auto-open post-launch:** macOS (`open`) = works. Linux (`xdg-open`) = works in principle. WSL = untested (browser handoff is documented as flaky). Windows-native = untested.
- **Local model download (`local_models.py`, MLX weights via HuggingFace `snapshot_download`):** macOS/Apple-Silicon = works (recommends `mlx-community/*` MLX-format repos). Windows-native / WSL / Linux = n/a for MLX (Apple-Silicon-only); non-Mac hosts route workhorse slots to cloud, and the server path has no local models by design.
- **Windows launcher (`.bat`):** Windows-native = present-but-untested — `start.bat`, `stop.bat`, and `uninstall/uninstall.ps1` are all committed at this commit (the "macOS+Windows portability retrofit" that this commit landed), but none has been run on Windows. The `check-portability.py` missing-launcher CRITICAL that earlier fired for absent `.bat` files no longer fires.

For the rationale behind the installer's shape and the retired First-Boot flow, see the vault sources *Installer — Ora* and *Paper — Installer*. Engine and slot behavior downstream of this chapter is developed in the routing and pipeline chapters.

## 2. Server & Browser-UI Runtime

The orchestrator described in the pipeline chapters is a library. It has no way to receive a prompt, show you a result, let you switch models, or resolve an oversight pause. This chapter is about the process that turns that library into a running system you can talk to: a single localhost Flask host (`server/app.py`, 15,040 lines) that serves one browser page (`server/index-v3.html`) backed by ~130 static JavaScript modules, and exposes 174 HTTP routes covering chat, clarification, conversation persistence, named configurations, model-routing slots, oversight, canvas/visual rendering, media capability, themes, and the model registry.

I am describing the installed system at commit `7a5e8f40` (PR #181, "Execution Review Phase 1-4: macOS+Windows portability retrofit"). That commit matters for this chapter specifically: it is the change that pulled the server's filesystem roots out of hardcoded `~/ora` / `~/Documents` string literals and behind a single cross-platform resolver. Where older documentation still describes the server as Mac-only string paths, trust the code — the retrofit landed. What the retrofit did not do is boot the server off a Mac; the code paths exist, the observation does not, and the Platform compatibility section holds that line.

### Problem

Without this subsystem there is no product. But "we need a UI" is not an architecture; the interesting failures are narrower.

**Lost work.** The pipeline takes minutes. A user submits a prompt, closes the laptop lid, and walks away — that is the normal case, not the exception. Ora is async-by-design. If the submission lives only in an open HTTP request or a browser tab, then a dropped connection, a tab close, or a server crash mid-run destroys the input with no record that it ever existed. The failure mode is silent data loss: the user believes they asked a question and later finds no answer and no trace.

**The spinner problem.** A synchronous streaming UI — hold the connection open, stream pipeline-stage events, render progress — couples the user's presence to the pipeline's runtime. If runs are sub-second that is fine. When runs are minutes and the user has left, streaming buys nothing and costs a live connection that will drop. The failure mode is a UI that only works if you sit and watch it.

**The empty-response drop.** A specific, real bug. Greetings, acknowledgements, system lookups, and the archived placeholder modes (`simple` / `standard`) route through `load_mode()`, which returns `""` for a mode file that no longer exists. The analytical pipeline then runs to completion and emits nothing — the user's "hi" produces `pipeline produced no response`. This is not hypothetical; the bypass guard described below exists precisely to close it.

**No control surface.** Model routing, named configurations, capability-slot assignment (which provider generates images, which generates video), and oversight resolution all need a place to live. Without HTTP endpoints backing a UI, every one of these is a config-file edit and a restart.

### Design Decision & Tradeoffs

**The decision: file-as-source-of-truth, poll-to-reconcile.** `POST /chat` does not stream. It runs the full pipeline synchronously server-side, writes the result to disk — a processed chunk file plus a `conversation.json` envelope — *before* it replies, and returns one small JSON acknowledgement: `{"status": "ok", "conversation_id": ..., "chunk_id": ...}`. The browser never holds an event stream for the pipeline. Instead it polls list and conversation endpoints on a 12-second cadence and reconciles what it finds against what it last rendered. The disk is the source of truth; the browser is a cache that catches up.

`_invoke_pipeline` states this in its own docstring (`server.py:6018`): "No SSE. No streaming. No pipeline-stage progress events. Per the V3 spec, the user does not watch the pipeline run — they submit and come back later."

**The alternative I rejected: keep the SSE-streamed live pipeline.** The pre-V3 design streamed `pipeline_stage` events over `text/event-stream` so the UI could show a live progress bar. Some of the project documentation still headlines the server as "Flask + SSE" with "SSE streaming with `pipeline_stage` events for UI progress." That description is stale. I abandoned it because it optimizes for a user who watches — and the actual user submits and leaves. A streamed connection to an absent client is a connection waiting to drop, and a dropped connection under the old model lost the run. The reframe is not manual versus agentic; it is foreground-watched versus background-completed execution, and the pipeline is background.

**What this buys and what it costs.** The gain is crash-recoverability and honesty about latency: because the input is on disk before anything else happens and the output is on disk before the reply, no single point in the request can silently drop work. The cost is real and I will not paper over it. There is no live progress — the user gets a pending marker and then, eventually, a result. And there is a reconciliation-latency floor: a result that lands 200 ms after a poll is not seen for up to 12 seconds. That 12s cadence is a deliberate tradeoff between staleness and polling load, not a measured optimum.

**Second decision: the bypass guard.** Inside `_pipeline_stream`, three cases fall through to `_direct_stream` — a path that calls the model with `boot.md` as the system prompt (`load_boot_md()`) and reliably emits a real `response` event — instead of the mode pipeline (`server.py:3524`): (1) the pre-routing stage flagged `bypass_to_direct_response` (a greeting, ack, system command, or lookup); (2) the resolved mode is the archived placeholder `simple` or `standard`; (3) a clarification is pending but the plain-HTTP endpoint cannot render the clarification panel. This guard is named by the failure it prevents: the empty-response drop. Without it, each of those three paths produces `pipeline produced no response` because `load_mode()` returned `""`.

**Third decision: single-user localhost only.** `app.run(host="localhost", ...)` (`server.py:15040`). No auth middleware, no non-localhost bind, no Content-Security-Policy header on the served page. This is a deliberate scope choice for a single-user local tool, and it is honest about its limits: the multi-machine / remote-access goal that appears elsewhere in the project's notes is **not built at the server layer**. Binding beyond localhost without first adding an auth and CSP layer would expose an unauthenticated pipeline that can write files and call paid APIs. That layer is planned, not present.

**Open problems.** The 12s poll cadence is hardcoded independently in two files — `sidebar.js` (`REFRESH_INTERVAL_MS = 12000`, line 26) and `sidebar-oversight.js` (`POLL_MS = 12000`, line 33). Changing the reconciliation interval requires touching both; there is no single source of truth for it. And the durability claim here is qualitative: the design *prevents* silent loss by ordering disk writes before the reply, but nothing measures a loss rate or a recovery rate. There is no instrumented reliability budget, and I will not invent one — the named failure mode is a mid-request crash between the pending-file write and the reply, and the guarantee is that such a crash surfaces the input as a recoverable errored chunk on next boot, not that some percentage of runs recover.

### Implementation

**Startup and self-heal (`server.py:14780`–`15040`).** The `__main__` block is where the process assembles itself, and its ordering is load-bearing. It parses two flags — `--scheduler` (task scheduler, `server.py:14784`) and `--oversight` (meta-layer daemon, `server.py:14785`). It then binds the first free port in the range 5000–5010 by trying `socket.bind` on each in turn (`server.py:14789`); the first success wins and the loser ports are skipped. If all eleven are busy, the loop falls through with `port` still at its initial `5000` and `app.run` will then fail to bind — there is no explicit "all ports busy" error path.

After the bind, a sequence of self-heal hooks runs, each wrapped in its own `try/except` so a failure prints and continues rather than aborting boot: `platform_check.startup_check` (engine-matches-machine validation, `server.py:14797`), the API in-flight pool cap sized from `install-state.json` via `mlx_mutex.configure_api_pool_from_install_state` (`server.py:14808`), an MLX worker heartbeat every 30s for the oversight health surface (`server.py:14822`), an embedding-readiness probe against the Ollama daemon (`server.py:14833`), index/RAG-manifest regeneration (shelling out to `bash generate-indexes.sh` / `compile-rag-manifest.sh` at `server.py:14856` / `14893` when the on-disk indexes drift from the directories), OpenRouter and direct-API catalog refreshes, local-MLX-model discovery that rewrites the `local_models` section of `config/models.json`, and a Lucide icon-set rebuild. Each is best-effort by design: this is a personal tool that should boot degraded rather than refuse to boot.

Two of these hooks are the durability spine. The **orphan pending-submission scan** (`_scan_orphaned_pending_submissions`, defined at `server.py:5417`, called at `server.py:14984`) walks the pending submission directory at boot, surfaces each stuck submission as an errored chunk in the sidebar's Errored group with retry/dismiss controls, and moves the file to `processed/` so it is not re-surfaced next boot. This is the crash-recovery half of the lost-work guarantee. The **SIGINT/SIGTERM shutdown handler** (`_shutdown_handler`, `server.py:14959`, registered at `14975`–`14976`) fires `session_end` hooks, clears sidebar windows, and cleans up bash-execution state before exiting.

**The `/chat` request lifecycle.** This is the path to read first for the whole subsystem.

1. `chat()` (`server.py:6297`) parses the payload and immediately calls `_log_pending_submission` (`server.py:5352`) — *before* validation, before attachment processing, before anything that could fail. It writes the full submission (input, history, mode/framework selections, attachments) to a `pending/<submission_id>.json` record. This write is the recoverable record. If it fails, the handler logs and proceeds anyway — a disk error must not be the thing that drops the user's input.
2. Attachments are partitioned: text is inlined into the prompt, images are passed separately. Multipart image uploads (via `/chat/multipart`, `server.py:6439`) land under `~/ora/sessions/<conversation_id>/uploads/`.
3. `_invoke_pipeline` (`server.py:5997`) drives the pipeline. It iterates `agentic_loop_stream` synchronously (`server.py:6091`) — the generator still yields SSE-framed chunks internally, but the server consumes them (`json.loads(chunk[6:])`, `server.py:6101`) rather than forwarding them, collecting the final `response` event and the last-seen stage/mode/gear for bridge state. `agentic_loop_stream` (`server.py:3828`) itself dispatches to `_pipeline_stream` (analytical, `server.py:3003`) or `_direct_stream` (bypass, `server.py:3666`); the bypass guard lives inside the former (`server.py:3524`). The conversation is marked Pending for the duration and cleared in a `finally`.
4. On a final response, `_save_conversation` (`server.py:5626`) runs synchronously so the `chunk_id` is known before the reply. It performs the persistence contract's three steps: append the prompt/response pair to the per-session raw audit log under `CONVERSATIONS_RAW`; write a processed chunk `.md` (YAML frontmatter + contextual header + exchange body) to the resolved chunk destination (`CONVERSATIONS_DIR`, or a per-request `output_destination` override resolved by `_resolve_chunk_destination`); and index the header+prompt into the ChromaDB `conversations` collection. The `conversation.json` envelope is the source of truth, set at creation and immutable per-turn; the chunk files are a denormalized cache. The `_save_conversation` docstring still names `nomic-embed-text-v1.5` as the embedder; per the project's own migration notes the embedder is now bge-m3 — a docstring drift, harmless to this chapter but worth flagging when you read that function.
5. On success, `_finalize_pending_submission` (`server.py:5379`, called at `6256`) moves the pending file to `processed/`; on failure it is left in `pending/` for the next boot's orphan scan. The reply is `{"status": "ok", ...}` or, on any failure, `{"status": "errored", ..., "failure_summary": ...}` after marking the conversation envelope errored. The failure summary is deliberately informative: when the generator returned without a response and without an error, the code synthesizes a breadcrumb from the last observed stage rather than the bare "no response" string (`server.py:6270`–`6276`).

Side effects that do not gate the reply — per-turn spatial-state persistence, the end-of-session runtime pipeline — fire in daemon threads after the chunk is saved.

**Cross-platform path resolution.** Every filesystem root the server touches now flows through `orchestrator/runtime_paths.py`. `WORKSPACE` honors `$ORA_HOME` before falling back to `expanduser("~/ora")` (`server.py:22`); conversation roots come from `runtime_paths.CONVERSATIONS_STR`, which resolves `$ORA_CONVERSATIONS` or `expanduser("~")/Documents/conversations` (`runtime_paths.py:27`,`38`). Because the defaults are built from `expanduser("~")` rather than literal `/Users/...`, they resolve to `%USERPROFILE%` on Windows. The module also carries the pieces a Windows port actually needs: `norm_key` / `within_base` (`runtime_paths.py:49`,`61`) do case- and separator-normalized, directory-boundary-anchored path comparison — so a protected-prefix check is not fooled by a sibling like `~/ora-project` matching `~/ora` — and `locked_file` (`runtime_paths.py:100`) is a cross-platform advisory lock that uses `fcntl` on POSIX and `msvcrt` on Windows, each imported under a guard so neither platform crashes at import (`runtime_paths.py:88`,`92`). One residual hardcode survives the retrofit inside `server.py`: `VISUAL_UPLOADS_ROOT = os.path.expanduser("~/ora/sessions/")` (`server.py:6376`) does not consult `$ORA_HOME`, so a relocated install writes uploads to the un-relocated root. That is a portability drift, not a crash.

**Config surfaces.** The tracked launcher `start.sh` exports the runtime flags — `ORA_HOME`, `ORA_RAG_SELECTION=1`, `ORA_RAG_FIT_GATE_SLOT=sidebar`, `ORA_WEB_EXTRACTION=1`, `ORA_RUNTIME_ENGRAM_PROMOTION=1`, `ORA_DELIVERABLE_SCRUB=1` and more (`start.sh:12-58`) — then launches the server backgrounded (`"$PYTHON" server/app.py --oversight &`), selecting `/opt/homebrew/bin/python3` with a system-`python3` fallback. Model routing is edited through `GET/POST /config/routing/slots` (`server.py:14042`/`14071`), which merges `{preferred, fallback}` into a single named slot within `routing-config.json` and rejects underscore-prefixed keys from client patches so annotations can't be clobbered. Named configurations live behind the `/api/configurations*` family (`server.py:13395`–`13596`): list, duplicate, new, per-slot edit, per-slot fallback edit, delete, active-get/set, and active-toggles. The active pipeline is chosen through these, not a config-file edit.

**The route surface (174 routes).** Grouped by family: chat (`/chat`, `/chat/multipart`, `/chat/queue-retry`); clarification (`/api/clarification`, `/skip`, `/pending`); conversations (list, load, fork, mark-read, close); configurations and routing slots (above); oversight (`/api/oversight/paused` at `server.py:14683`, `/api/oversight/operating` at `14716`, and per-entry `/name`, `/engagement`, `/discuss` at `14731`–`14761`); canvas/visual (`/api/canvas/save` at `6691`, `/api/canvas/load/<id>` at `6792`, visual-schema serving); media capability — nine wired image/video POST routes (`image_generates` at `server.py:11048`, then `image_edits` `11227`, `image_outpaints` `11403`, `image_upscales` `11615`, `image_styles` `11790`, `image_critique` `11987`, `image_varies` `12375`, `image_to_prompt` `12601`, and `video_generates` `12779`); themes; and the model registry (`/models` at `12932`, `/api/model-registry/picks` at `13652`). The capability image/video routes are live and wired — this contradicts the stale note elsewhere in the project docs that image generation is "locked but not implemented." That note was not superseded in place.

**Residual SSE.** Streaming survives on exactly three route bodies, all returning `text/event-stream`: `/api/document/stream` (`server.py:5084`), `/api/clarification` (`server.py:10714`), and `/api/clarification/skip` (`server.py:10805`); `/api/framework/analyze-inputs` (`server.py:5109`) sits in the same neighborhood. These are the minority path. The pipeline's own progress is not among them.

**The V3 static UI.** `/` and `/v3` both serve `index-v3.html` (`server.py:3859`, `3867`); the classic `/classic` and `/v2` routes were retired (per the `index()` comment, 2026-05-30). The page pulls 132 `<script>` tags. `sidebar.js` runs the conversation-list reconciliation loop (12s, visibility-gated so a hidden tab stops polling; `sidebar.js:1163`–`1169`). `sidebar-oversight.js` drives the three-supergroup accordion (Conversations / Paused / Operating) and polls `/api/oversight/paused` and `/api/oversight/operating` every 12s (`sidebar-oversight.js:474`). `v3-conversation.js` renders assistant turns and, per turn, hands the text to `v3-visual-dispatch.js`, which extracts ` ```ora-visual ` fenced JSON via `FENCE_RE` (`v3-visual-dispatch.js:29`), passes each parseable block to `OraPanels.visual.onBridgeUpdate` (the Konva `canvas_action` state machine, deduped per conversation+turn), and swaps the raw fence for a `*📊 Diagram rendered in the visual pane.*` marker so the transcript stays clean.

**Partial and standalone components.** `/chat/queue-retry` (`server.py:9034`) is **partial**: it persists vision-retry requests both in-memory and to `~/ora/sessions/<conversation_id>/vision-retry-queue.json`, but its own docstring is explicit — "NO automatic retry here — a future daemon or user-triggered action will flush the queue." Persistence is built; the flush is not. `server/review.py` is a **separate** standalone Flask app (the Note Review interface) that binds `127.0.0.1:5005` in its own `__main__` (`review.py:530`); it is not imported by `server.py` and is not part of this process — whether anything still launches it was not traced.

**Failure behavior, summarized.** Every startup hook is best-effort. A pipeline crash mid-run leaves the pending file in place and surfaces as an errored chunk on next boot. A save failure marks the envelope errored and returns `status: errored`. The oversight health surface prepends a warning to the next chat response when the daemon is down or a watcher is stale, so the user sees degradation in the conversation without a separate dashboard.

### Evidence & Verification

**What is tested.** The visual-dispatch fence extraction has `server/static/ora-visual-compiler/tests/cases/test-v3-visual-dispatch.js` (12 assertions, per the project's test docs). The routing-slots endpoint has `orchestrator/tests/test_routing_slots_endpoint.py` (12 Python tests, verified by count in-file) and `server/static/ora-visual-compiler/tests/cases/test-visual-slots-pane.js` (24 JS assertions, per the project's test docs), part of a JS harness cited at 813/813. These are the tests that touch this chapter's surfaces.

**What is not tested — and this is the honest gap.** There is no server-route-level HTTP integration test inside `server/`. A grep of `server.py` for test or pytest references returns nothing in-file. Route correctness is inferred from orchestrator-side unit tests plus live probing, not from an automated suite that boots the app and exercises `POST /chat`. There is no CSP test and no auth test, because there is no CSP header and no auth. The full 174-route surface was mapped by family; the capability/media/theme sub-endpoints are asserted present by their route decorators but not each exercised by an automated request. And whether the three residual SSE endpoints are still exercised by the current V3 JS or are vestigial was not traced through the client.

**How to confirm it works live** (this is a manual path, not CI): launch via `start.sh`; `GET /health` (`server.py:3871`) returns `{"status": "ok", "endpoint": ...}`; `POST /chat` with a real prompt, then confirm a chunk `.md` lands under the conversations directory and the `conversation.json` envelope updates. For the durability guarantee specifically: `POST /chat`, kill the process mid-pipeline before the reply, restart, and confirm the submission reappears as an errored chunk with retry/dismiss (the orphan scan at `server.py:14984`).

### Expert Reading Notes

**Read these four spans first, in order.** (1) The `__main__` block (`server.py:14780`–`15040`) for startup ordering and every self-heal side effect — this is where the process's contract with its environment lives. (2) `_invoke_pipeline` (`server.py:5997`) and (3) the bypass guard inside `_pipeline_stream` (`server.py:3507`–`3541`) for the request lifecycle and the empty-response fix. (4) `_save_conversation` (`server.py:5626`) for the persistence contract — internalize that `conversation.json` is source of truth and chunks are a denormalized cache before you touch either.

**Where future changes most likely cause drift.** First, the doc-vs-code gap on streaming: any documentation that headlines "Flask + SSE" or "SSE streaming with `pipeline_stage` events" is describing the retired model; the installed `/chat` is synchronous JSON with disk persistence and browser polling. Second, the 174-route surface grows without a map, so any route-count or route-family reference goes stale quietly. Third, launcher drift: the tracked `start.sh` sets every runtime `ORA_*` flag (`start.sh:12-58`) and selects `/opt/homebrew/bin/python3` with a system-`python3` fallback, oversight-on by default — this is the committed, reproducible launcher. The author's Mac additionally runs an **untracked** `run-ora-server.sh` launchd wrapper (not in commit 7a5e8f40; a fresh clone won't have it) whose flag set can diverge from `start.sh`. Because the wrapper is untracked and machine-local, anyone reasoning from the commit should treat `start.sh` as authoritative; the divergence is a live hazard only on a machine carrying the untracked wrapper. Fourth, the 12s poll cadence duplicated across `sidebar.js` and `sidebar-oversight.js`. Fifth, the `_save_conversation` embedder docstring naming nomic while the system runs bge-m3.

**One thing to verify before you rely on it.** `sidebar-oversight.js` hedges the Approve/Deny commit path as going through "`/api/oversight/resolve` (or fall back to slash commands)," and its own header comment says the v1 build uses the slash-command path. There is no `/api/oversight/resolve` route in `server.py` — the oversight route family stops at `/discuss` (`server.py:14761`). Approve/Deny commit through the chat resolution-chain / slash-command path, not a dedicated resolve endpoint. Do not build against a resolve endpoint that isn't there.

### Platform compatibility

The macOS+Windows portability retrofit (PR #181) is what this commit *is*, so the platform story here is better than "Mac-only" — but "retrofitted so it should work" is not "run and verified off-Mac," and I label the difference. Nothing below was booted on a non-Mac surface; every non-mac cell that is not a clean-degrade path is "untested."

- **First-free-port bind (5000–5010):** macOS = works | Windows-native = untested | WSL = untested | Linux = untested. Plain `socket.bind` loop on `localhost` (`server.py:14789`); `localhost` resolves cross-platform, but full boot to a bound port was only observed on macOS. If all eleven ports are busy the loop falls through and `app.run` fails to bind — no dedicated error path.
- **Filesystem roots (`$ORA_HOME`, `$ORA_CONVERSATIONS`):** macOS = works | Windows-native = untested | WSL = untested | Linux = untested. Resolved through `runtime_paths.py` from `expanduser("~")` (`runtime_paths.py:27`,`38`), which maps to `%USERPROFILE%` on Windows. The retrofit is the reason this is no longer "needs-porting," but no non-Mac boot was run, so it stays untested rather than "plausibly-works."
- **`VISUAL_UPLOADS_ROOT` hardcode:** macOS = works | Windows-native = wrong-root | WSL = untested | Linux = works-at-default-only. `os.path.expanduser("~/ora/sessions/")` (`server.py:6376`) ignores `$ORA_HOME`; a relocated install writes multipart uploads to the un-relocated path. Drift, not crash.
- **Cross-platform advisory lock:** macOS = works | Windows-native = untested | WSL = works | Linux = works. `fcntl` on POSIX, `msvcrt` on Windows, guarded imports (`runtime_paths.py:88`,`92`). The Windows branch exists but was not exercised here.
- **Untracked launch artifact `run-ora-server.sh` (author's machine only — NOT in commit 7a5e8f40):** a `#!/bin/zsh -l` launchd wrapper hardcoding `/opt/homebrew/bin/python3`; macOS-only by construction and not part of the committed system (a fresh clone does not contain it). The tracked launcher at this commit is `start.sh`.
- **MLX heartbeat + `mlx_mutex` (Apple MLX/Metal):** macOS = works (Apple Silicon) | Windows-native = degrades | WSL = degrades | Linux = degrades. Wrapped in `try/except` (`server.py:14822`); off Apple Silicon the import fails, the hook is skipped, and the local-model path is simply absent — the server still boots.
- **Reveal-in-Finder (`POST /api/fs/reveal` → `open -R`):** macOS = works | Windows-native = clean-501 | WSL = clean-501 | Linux = clean-501. Guarded by `sys.platform != "darwin"` returning HTTP 501 "reveal-in-Finder is macOS-only" (`server.py:4948`) before it shells out to `open -R`. This one *was* ported — it degrades cleanly rather than erroring on a missing `open`.
- **Startup `bash` script calls (`generate-indexes.sh`, `compile-rag-manifest.sh`):** macOS = works | Windows-native = fails-soft | WSL = works | Linux = works. Invoked as `subprocess.run(["bash", ...])` (`server.py:14856`,`14893`); no `bash` on Windows-native means these fail, but they are inside the startup `try/except` and only skip index/manifest self-heal.
- **SIGINT/SIGTERM shutdown + threaded Flask:** macOS = works | Windows-native = untested | WSL = works | Linux = works. `signal.signal` handlers (`server.py:14975`–`14976`); SIGTERM semantics differ on Windows-native.
- **Embedding-readiness probe (Ollama reachability):** macOS = works | Windows-native = untested | WSL = untested | Linux = untested. `assert_embedding_ready` at `server.py:14833`; the module comment claims Win/Linux/Mac but only Mac was observed.
- **`index-v3.html` asset loading (absolute `/static` URLs, deferred scripts):** macOS = works | Windows-native = works | WSL = works | Linux = works. Browser-served absolute URLs are OS-agnostic and carry no filesystem-separator concern client-side.
- **CSP / auth / non-localhost bind:** all platforms = absent. No `Content-Security-Policy` header on the served page (0 matches in `index-v3.html`), no auth middleware, `host="localhost"` only (`server.py:15040`). This is a scope decision, uniform across platforms, and it is the blocker for any remote-access goal.

## 3. Settings, API Keys & Local-Model Handling

### Problem

Three failure modes converge on one subsystem, and each is the kind that corrupts trust in an install rather than merely inconveniencing it.

The first is secret leakage. An orchestrator that talks to two dozen external providers holds a bearer credential for each, and each of those credentials bills the user's card. Store those keys in a JSON config file and they leak into git, into backups, into log lines, into any error message that echoes a request URL. The naive alternative — re-prompt for keys every session — trades leakage for friction so severe the product is unusable. The requirement is keys durable across restarts, absent from every file the user might share, and scrubbed from every error string that can round-trip to a browser.

The second is provider-metadata drift. The Settings UI must know each provider's display label and console URL. The boot sequence must know each provider's env-var name to bridge its key. The key-setup framework must know each provider's key prefix to validate format. The verify probe must know each provider's authentication scheme. If those four surfaces each carry their own copy of the provider table, they drift: a provider added to the UI never gets its key bridged to the env; a renamed env var silently drops a working key. This is the classic single-source-of-truth problem, and the punishment for getting it wrong is a key the user pasted correctly that nonetheless does nothing.

The third is local-model registry staleness. Ora runs MLX models — Apple's array framework for on-device inference on Apple Silicon — from `~/ora/models/`. When the user drops a new model into that directory or deletes an old one, nothing notices until a human hand-edits `config/models.json`. The V3 Models pane then shows models that are gone and hides models that are present. This was a live gap dated 2026-05-26, recorded in `local_model_discovery.py`'s own module notes: nothing in the system noticed the on-disk change until a human edited `models.json`.

A fourth, quieter need rides along: non-secret preferences — Whisper model size, default render preset, output style, capture frame rate — need a durable home that is neither an environment variable (wrong lifetime, wrong scope) nor per-conversation state (wrong persistence).

### Design Decision & Tradeoffs

Two deliberate splits and one automation choice.

**Split one: secrets live in the OS keyring; everything else lives in JSON.** API keys are written only to the operating system's credential store via the `keyring` library — service name `ora`, username `<provider>-api-key`. On macOS that store is the Keychain. Non-secret preferences live in `config/user-settings.json`, which holds no secrets — its module docstring states "Secrets (API keys) are NOT stored here; they live in the system keyring" (`orchestrator/user_settings.py:5-7`). The rejected alternative was a single encrypted config blob. It reinvents a credential store the OS already ships, audits, and integrates with the user's login session; a homegrown blob is a smaller attack surface's illusion, not its reality.

**The single source of truth for provider metadata is `provider_registry.py`.** One list, `PROVIDERS`, declares every provider's id, label, category, keyring username, env var, signup and console URLs, key prefix, activation flags, dispatch style, and verify eligibility (`orchestrator/provider_registry.py:73-328`). Every consumer derives from it: `user_settings.py` builds its keyring-username map and its UI status rows from `keyring_username_map()` and `ui_rows()`; `boot.py` builds its keyring→env bridge from `env_bridge_pairs()`; the prefer-direct router builds its rewrite table from `or_prefix_map()`. The registry's own docstring states the invariant: adding a provider is a one-entry edit here; no consumer needs touching. The rejected alternative — duplicating provider tables across the JavaScript panel, the Python boot path, and the setup docs — is exactly the drift failure mode above. The tradeoff is a single point of failure inverted into a single point of correctness: a provider omitted from `PROVIDERS` silently vanishes from the env-bridge, the verify probe, and the Settings UI at once. That is a real hazard, and it recurs under Expert Reading Notes because it is the most likely way a future edit breaks this subsystem.

**Split two: desktop and server key handling diverge on purpose.** The Mac desktop uses the keyring. The headless Linux server does not. `scripts/install-server.sh` writes `OPENROUTER_API_KEY` (and optionally `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`) to a plaintext file at `~/.config/ora-server.env`, `chmod 600`, sourced into the process environment (`scripts/install-server.sh:190-217, 226-231`). This is a deliberate acceptance of plaintext-on-a-locked-box over the cost of wiring a headless credential daemon — the Secret Service backend `keyring` targets on Linux needs a running keyring daemon and a login session a headless VM does not have. What makes the divergence cheap is the bridge design below: the same `boot.py` code serves both hosts because it reads keys from the environment, and the environment is populated either by the keyring mirror (Mac) or by the sourced env-file (Linux). This is an open problem, not a solved one: there is no OS-backed credential store on the server path. The plaintext env-file is the current answer, and a headless-keyring option remains unbuilt. Nothing in `CLAUDE.md` documents the server plaintext path — its only keyring reference is a note about which Python interpreter ships the `keyring` package — so a reader who knows only the desktop model can wrongly assume keyring everywhere.

**Automation choice: local models are discovered, not curated.** `local_model_discovery.py` scans `~/ora/models/` at server startup and rewrites the `local_models` array in `config/models.json`. The rejected alternative — hand-curated entries — is the staleness failure mode by construction. The tradeoff is that discovery can only read what the model files declare, so a model with a malformed `config.json` is silently skipped rather than surfaced as an error. Against that, a no-shrink guard: `refresh()` refuses any write that would record fewer local models than `models.json` already holds, printing the refusal and the missing ids, so neither an unmounted models directory nor a readable-but-empty one can truncate the registry (`local_model_discovery.py::refresh`). Without that guard, a race between a mount and server startup would erase every local model from the UI; deliberate removal is the exception and passes `allow_shrink=True` (CLI `--write --allow-shrink`).

### Implementation

**Provider registry (`orchestrator/provider_registry.py`).** `PROVIDERS` is a list of 24 dicts. The derived helpers are pure functions over that list: `keyring_username_map()`, `labels_map()`, `env_bridge_pairs()` (every provider declaring an `env_var`), `direct_llm_providers()` (those with a `dispatch` value), `catalog_authoritative_providers()` (direct providers with a real pay-as-you-go path), `or_prefix_map()` (keyed by OpenRouter vendor segment for the prefer-direct rewrite), and `ui_rows()` (the no-secret payload the panel renders). `validate_key_format(provider_id, value)` is lenient by design: it returns a `(looks_ok, message)` tuple, and a key under 12 characters or missing its declared prefix returns `looks_ok=False` with a warning message that the UI surfaces but the store path never enforces (`provider_registry.py:384-399`) — the docstring ties this to the vault's [[Framework — API Key Setup]] rule that a surprising shape warns but never blocks a key the user insists on. Categories drive UI grouping through `GROUP_ORDER`; dispatch metadata (`native` vs `openai_compatible`, `base_url`, `or_prefix`, `native_service`) drives direct-vendor routing.

**Settings persistence (`orchestrator/user_settings.py`).** `load_settings()` deep-merges the on-disk file over `DEFAULTS` and is forward-compatible: keys present on disk but absent from `DEFAULTS` are preserved on round-trip, so an older client cannot truncate a field a newer server wrote (`user_settings.py:158-181`). Writes go through `_write_raw()`, which writes a `.json.tmp` sibling and calls `Path.replace` — an atomic same-filesystem rename that prevents a torn write from a crash mid-save (`user_settings.py:151-155`).
