A `threading.Lock` serializes read-modify-write against concurrent requests. `save_settings()` type-validates known leaves before persisting (`capture.frame_rate` ∈ {24,25,30,50,60}, `whisper.model_size` ∈ {tiny,base,small,medium,large-v3}, render preset against `render.py`'s keys) and raises `SettingsError` rather than writing garbage.

The API-key surface delegates entirely to the keyring, guarded by a provider whitelist derived from the registry so the HTTP endpoints cannot write an arbitrary keyring account (`user_settings.py:274-277`). `set_api_key`, `delete_api_key`, and `api_key_present` import `keyring` lazily — the comment notes this lets tests stub it via `sys.modules`. `delete_api_key` swallows every exception because keyring backends raise different exception types for "not found," and "already absent" is success (`user_settings.py:288-296`). `list_api_key_status()` returns the registry's `ui_rows()` enriched with a live `present` boolean and nothing else — the value never leaves the keyring (`user_settings.py:310-324`).

**The keyring→env bridge (`orchestrator/boot.py:173-200`).** `_export_provider_keys_to_env()` runs unconditionally at module import (`boot.py:200`). It iterates `provider_registry.env_bridge_pairs()` and, for each `(env_var, keyring_username)`, writes the keyring value into `os.environ` only when the env var is not already set — the "pre-set env vars win" rule (comment at `boot.py:170`, enforced at `boot.py:189-190`). This one rule unifies the two hosts: on Mac the keyring populates the env; on Linux the sourced env-file has already populated it, so the bridge sees the var set and no-ops. The function is defensively wrapped: a missing `keyring` backend (`ImportError`) returns early, and a registry import failure falls back to the original web-search trio (Tavily/Brave/Exa) so the search cascade survives even a broken registry (`boot.py:176-188`). Without the bridge, every consumer that reads `os.environ` would see no key until a manual restart-time lookup — a cold-start credential outage.

At dispatch time the read path is env-first, keyring-second. `_provider_key(entry)` returns the env var if set and stripped, else falls back to `_keyring_lookup("ora", entry["keyring_username"])` (`boot.py:13713-13720`). The prefer-direct router (`_resolve_direct_endpoint`, `boot.py:13723`+) uses `or_prefix_map()` to rewrite an OpenRouter `vendor/model` id to the vendor's own API when that vendor's key is present, bypassing OpenRouter's markup. It is gated by `ORA_PREFER_DIRECT`, read at `boot.py:13701`: `_prefer_direct_enabled()` treats the value as on unless it is explicitly `0/false/no/off`. That flag is a kill switch, not a feature gate — it defaults on, so prefer-direct is installed and live, not flag-gated-off.

**Settings HTTP surface (`server/app.py`).** `GET /api/settings` returns settings, the no-secret `api_keys` rows, and provider groups (`server.py:1142-1160`). `POST /api/settings` deep-merges an `updates` dict and returns the merged state (`1163-1177`). `POST /api/settings/api-key` stores a key; `DELETE /api/settings/api-key/<provider>` removes one, validating the provider id against the registry before touching the keyring (`1810-1844`). `POST /api/settings/api-key/verify` runs `_verify_provider_key`, which makes the cheapest authenticated call that proves a key works — a free auth probe, a model-list GET, or a single search — using only stdlib `urllib` (`1847-1927`). Every branch is free or negligible; providers whose only auth proof costs a generation (image, TTS, transcription) are marked `verifiable: false` in the registry and short-circuit to "Verification isn't available" at the endpoint before any network call (`1945-1947`). The scrubbing discipline is the load-bearing detail: FRED authenticates via query string, so a malformed key can embed itself in a URL that an exception stringifies; the catch-all replaces the key with `***` before returning (`1922-1926`). The Gemini probe deliberately passes the key as a header, not a query param, for the same reason (`1884-1887`). This component closes the "secret round-trips to the browser in an error message" failure mode.

**Local-model discovery (`orchestrator/local_model_discovery.py`).** `probe_model_dir()` reads a subdirectory's `config.json` and `*.safetensors`, returning `None` for any directory lacking a config, lacking safetensors, or carrying malformed JSON — so `diffusers`, `whisper`, and `loras` subdirectories are rejected automatically (`local_model_discovery.py:238-284, 287-296`). It estimates total parameters from safetensors byte-size and quantization bits rather than parsing layers, which works across architectures and captures embeddings and vision towers for free (`111-127`). It detects MoE (mixture-of-experts — a model that routes each token through a subset of its parameters) and extracts active-params-per-token, first from the directory-name `-a<N>b` convention, then from the config's expert-routing ratio (`85-97, 129-164`). Vision capability is inferred from three signals: a `vision_config` block, an image-token field, or an architecture ending in `ConditionalGeneration` (`61-82`). `refresh(write=True)` rewrites `models.json`'s `local_models` array and stamps `_local_models_discovered_at`, preserving `commercial_models` and all other top-level keys, and refuses the write outright when discovery would record fewer models than the file already holds, unless `allow_shrink=True` (`local_model_discovery.py::refresh`). `server.py` calls it best-effort at startup, wrapped so a failure logs but does not abort boot (`server.py:15008-15024`). The CLI defaults to dry-run; `--write` persists.

**Install-side selection (`scripts/local_models.py`).** A separate concern: at install time, a RAM-tier classifier maps total system RAM to a deployment tier (`small_only` under 32 GB, `workhorse_choice` under 64, `full_local` under 128, `adversarial` at 128+) and a curated per-tier catalog, then wraps `huggingface_hub.snapshot_download` for the fetch, with `local_dir_use_symlinks=False` to avoid the Windows symlink-permission pitfall, and `HF_TOKEN` guidance for gated repos (`scripts/local_models.py:113-296, 329-362`). RAM detection branches on `sys.platform`: `sysctl` on macOS, `/proc/meminfo` on Linux, `wmic` on Windows (`92-120`). This path writes install state to `config/local-models.json`, distinct from the runtime `models.json`.

**Failure behavior, summarized.** Missing keyring backend → bridge no-ops, verify returns `None`. Malformed model `config.json` → that directory is silently skipped. Zero discovered models → `refresh` declines to write. Malformed `user-settings.json` → `load_settings` raises `SettingsError` rather than returning partial state.

### Evidence & Verification

Four test suites cover the subsystem, 110 tests total, and the counts hold against the files: `test_user_settings.py` (27 — persistence, deep-merge, forward-compat, validation, and keyring CRUD via a stubbed `keyring` in `sys.modules`); `test_provider_registry.py` (22 — derived maps, `env_bridge_pairs`, `validate_key_format`, `ui_rows`); `test_local_model_discovery.py` (37 — probe, scan, refresh, MoE active-param extraction, vision detection, and the zero-entry guard); `test_chunk8_local_models.py` (24 — RAM-tier classification, RAM estimation, and the download wrapper). Adjacent coverage lives in `test_aa_path_resolution.py` and `test_direct_catalog.py`.

Live confirmation: `config/models.json` on this machine carries 6 discovered `local_models` and a `_local_models_discovered_at` float stamp — four Qwen MLX builds (a 4B abliterated variant, a 9B, a 27B, and a 122B `-a10b` MoE), GLM-4.6V, and a 128B Mistral-Medium-3.5, matching the 2026-05-26 vision lineup. The server-path key store is checked by `scripts/install_server_smoke.py`, which asserts `OPENROUTER_API_KEY` is sourced from the env-file.

Explicitly untested surfaces. The `keyring` backend is exercised only on macOS Keychain; the Windows Credential Locker and Linux Secret Service paths are never run — the server path deliberately routes around them with the env-file. The verify probes call live provider endpoints; there is no offline mock, so the most failure-prone branch of `_verify_provider_key` — the network call itself — is uncovered by CI. `scripts/local_models.py`'s `snapshot_download` is not exercised against a real gated repo in CI. None of these paths is instrumented with success-rate or reliability metrics; there is no reliability budget measured here, and none is claimed. The named risks are qualitative failure modes, not measured rates.

### Expert Reading Notes

Read `provider_registry.py`'s `PROVIDERS` list first. It is the spine; every other surface in this chapter derives from it, and the fastest way to understand a bug in the env-bridge, the verify probe, or the Settings UI is to check whether the provider's registry entry declares the field that surface consumes. Then read `boot.py::_export_provider_keys_to_env` — it is the Mac↔server unifier, and the "pre-set env wins" rule (`boot.py:189-190`) is what lets one code path serve two credential models. Then read `server.py::_verify_provider_key` for the key-scrubbing discipline (`1922-1926`) — the pattern to preserve is header-auth-over-query-auth and scrub-on-exception.

Drift will most likely appear in four places. First, `config/user-settings.json` on disk carries `capture.default_system_audio` and `whisper.provider`, both absent from `DEFAULTS` (the `default_system_audio` checkbox was wired to nothing and removed 2026-07-01, per the NOTE at `user_settings.py:64-67`; the disk file predates that). The forward-compatible merge preserves them harmlessly, which is exactly why the drift is invisible — a reader comparing `DEFAULTS` to the live file will find keys in the file that the code no longer knows about. Second, `scripts/local_models.py`'s `TIER_RECOMMENDATIONS` catalog still lists Qwen2.5-7B/27B and Meta-Llama-3.1-70B (`scripts/local_models.py:211-296`) — the Qwen2.5/Llama-3.1 era, not the deployed 2026-05-26 vision lineup in `models.json`. The two local-model code paths — install-side selection and runtime discovery — are not reconciled; there is no step that syncs the curated catalog to what is actually installed. Third, any new provider must land in `PROVIDERS` or it silently drops out of the bridge, the verify probe, and the UI at once. Fourth, `local_model_discovery.refresh` rewrites only `local_models` and the timestamp; the `_vision_capable_defaults`, `overhead_reservation_gb`, and `local_model_directory` keys in `models.json` are managed elsewhere and the module does not document where — a reader debugging vision routing should not assume discovery owns those fields.

### Platform compatibility

- **API-key storage backend (`keyring`, `user_settings.set_api_key`/`api_key_present`):** macOS — works (Keychain, exercised). Windows-native — untested (maps to Credential Locker in principle; never run here). WSL — untested. Linux — needs-porting/unknown-untested: `keyring`'s Secret Service backend requires a running keyring daemon and login session a headless server lacks; the server path deliberately uses the plaintext env-file instead, so libsecret is never exercised.
- **Server-side key file `~/.config/ora-server.env` (`chmod 600`, `source` into env; `install-server.sh`):** Linux — works (the deployment target). WSL — works in principle (bash + POSIX perms + `source` apply); not exercised on this machine. macOS — works (POSIX). Windows-native — needs-porting: no `chmod`/POSIX permission bits and no shell `source`; a different secret mechanism would be required.
- **Config paths `Path.home()/'ora'/'config'` and models dir `~/ora/models/`:** macOS — works. Linux — works. WSL — untested. Windows-native — unknown-untested: `Path.home()` is cross-platform, but the hardcoded `~/ora` layout and the absolute `/Users/oracle` paths baked into `models.json` are Mac-shaped. `models.json` is gitignored and machine-specific, so discovery regenerates the absolute paths per host — the paths do not need to be portable, only re-derivable, which the startup rewrite guarantees.
- **Local MLX model inference (running the `models/` contents):** macOS (Apple Silicon) — mac-only; MLX/Metal is Apple-Silicon-exclusive. Windows-native / WSL / Linux — not-applicable: the discovery code merely reads `config.json` + safetensors (platform-neutral), but the models only *run* on Apple Silicon; the Linux server path routes every model call to OpenRouter or direct vendor APIs and hosts no local models.
- **RAM detection `scripts/local_models.py::get_total_ram_gb`:** macOS — works (`sysctl`). Linux — works (`/proc/meminfo`). Windows-native — untested and at risk: uses `wmic`, deprecated on recent Windows. WSL — untested.
- **HF `snapshot_download(local_dir_use_symlinks=False)`:** macOS — works. Linux — works in principle; not exercised in CI. WSL — untested. Windows-native — untested, though disabling symlinks is the correct portability choice for the Windows symlink-permission pitfall; it is simply not run there.
- **`user-settings.json` atomic write (`.json.tmp` + `Path.replace`):** macOS / Linux / WSL / Windows-native — atomic same-filesystem rename on every platform; no `fcntl` locking is used (in-process `threading.Lock` only). Exercised on macOS; the mechanism is stdlib and platform-neutral, but only the macOS path is actually run here.
- **Verify probes (`_verify_provider_key`, live HTTP via `urllib`):** platform-neutral (stdlib `urllib`), so no OS-specific surface — but network-dependent and unmocked; the branch is uninstrumented and untested offline on every platform including macOS.


---

# Part II — The Analytical Core

## 4. Model / Provider Configuration & Routing

### Problem

A single hardcoded model choice cannot span Ora's install spectrum. At one end sits a free-tier-only user with no local hardware, routing every call through rate-limited OpenRouter free endpoints. At the other end sits a power user on a Mac Studio with 128 GB of RAM, running several local MLX models plus a wallet full of paid API keys. Between them are hybrid installs that mix a couple of local models with a Claude or GPT key. A choice that is correct for one of these is wrong — often unusably wrong — for the others.

The problem is worse than "one size doesn't fit all," because a single request is not homogeneous. Ora's adversarial pipeline runs eight steps, and the steps have opposite cost/capability profiles. Prompt cleanup and classification want a cheap 4B model that answers in two seconds. Depth analysis wants a 60B-plus model that can actually execute an adversarial protocol. The breadth analyst wants a model from a *different* training family than depth, so the two do not share the same blind spots. The consolidator and formatter want something in between. If every step draws from the same model, you either overpay for classification or underpower analysis, and you lose the cross-family diversity that makes the adversarial pass worth running.

Four concrete failure modes follow if you do not solve this:

1. **Every model choice becomes a code edit.** Changing which model authors an article, or which model classifies intent, means editing Python. Non-programmer users cannot tune the system at all.
2. **Adversarial diversity is impossible.** With no way to say "breadth must differ from depth," the pipeline silently pairs a model against itself and reports agreement as if it were independent confirmation.
3. **A rate-limited or down provider stalls the pipeline.** One 429 from OpenRouter, or one MLX model that fails to load, and the request hangs or dies with a raw transport error shipped to the user.
4. **Concurrent MLX calls segfault the process.** The MLX runtime on Apple Silicon crashes with SIGSEGV when two threads load or invoke models on the same machine at once. Gear 4 runs depth and breadth in parallel by design. Without serialization, an all-local Gear 4 run kills the server.

A fifth, subtler failure sits underneath all of these: capability metadata drifts. A model's `vision_capable` flag, hand-entered into a config file, goes stale when a provider changes the model. The proof case was `kimi-k2.6`: a wrong `vision_capable: true` in `routing-config.json` routed image turns to a model that silently dropped the image, and the analyst ran blind with no error. Whatever fills each slot, something authoritative has to decide what that model can actually do.

This chapter describes the subsystem that decides which concrete endpoint fills each named pipeline slot — for a given gear, execution context, and user-selected configuration — and dispatches the call while surviving all five failure modes. The eight-step pipeline that consumes these resolved endpoints is covered in the Gear Pipeline & Mode Dispatch chapter; the concurrency primitives are canonicalized in [[Reference — Ora Concurrency Architecture]]; the per-slot Models pane UI is described in [[Reference — Ora Runtime Configuration]].

### Design Decision & Tradeoffs

**The decision: named slots decouple role from model; a user-selected named configuration maps each slot to an explicit primary-plus-fallback chain.**

A slot is a role in the pipeline — `depth`, `breadth`, `sidebar`, `consolidator`, `evaluator`, `step1_cleanup`, `classification`, `formatter`, `primary`. The mode file and the gear pipeline reference slots, never models. A named configuration (`config/configurations/<name>.json`) maps each slot's cell to an ordered chain: a `primary` endpoint id plus a `fallback` list of endpoint ids. The router walks that chain in order and returns the first endpoint that survives filtering. This is the Unix principle of indirection — the pipeline calls a name, and a lookup table binds the name to an implementation, so you re-point the binding without touching the caller.

The user picks a configuration. Four presets are defined — **Free**, **Budget**, **Speed**, **Premium** — baked from the model catalog on first Models-pane open (`active_configuration.bake_missing_presets`, `active_configuration.py:272`), plus any custom configurations the user builds. The active choice is a pointer file at `~/ora/data/active-configuration.json`, read on every interactive request (`active_configuration.get_active_name`, `orchestrator/active_configuration.py:52`). `PRESET_ORDER = ["free", "budget", "speed", "premium"]` (`active_configuration.py:615`) is the authoritative list.

At the reviewed commit, only three preset files are materialized on disk — `free.json`, `budget.json`, `premium.json`; `speed.json` is not yet baked. All four presets are defined in `config/configuration-presets.json` (keys `free / budget / speed / premium`), so `bake_missing_presets` will materialize `speed` the next time it runs against a present catalog; it is a defined-but-unbaked preset, not a missing one.

> Drift note, load-bearing: `CLAUDE.md` names the presets "Free / Budget / Optimum / Premium" and cites a "358-model" inventory. The code says `free / budget / speed / premium` and the shipped registry carries `model_count = 337` (`config/model-registry.json`). The Preset Speed Redesign renamed Optimum→Budget and old-Budget→Speed; the `CLAUDE.md` Models-pane paragraph was not updated. Trust the code. This chapter uses `speed`.

**The main alternative rejected: the six-bucket tier system.** The original design (`docs/design-model-configuration.md`, now marked Historical/superseded) sorted every endpoint into one of six tiers — `local-fast`, `local-mid`, `local-premium`, `free`, `mid`, `premium` — and resolved a slot by walking an ordered list of buckets. That abstraction was dissolved. Opaque tier ordering did not match how users reason. A user does not think "give me a local-premium model"; they think "use Qwen 122B for depth, and if that's busy fall back to GLM." The bucket walk hid the specific model behind a tier label, so the Models pane could not show, and the user could not choose, the actual endpoint. Named configurations invert this: the chain names concrete ids, the UI renders them, and the user edits them directly.

**The tradeoff.** Explicit chains are verbose — a `gear4.depth` cell can list six fallback ids — and they can reference an id that is absent from `routing-config.json::endpoints[]`. When a registry refresh reshapes endpoint ids, a configuration written against the old ids silently degrades: the router logs the unknown id and continues the cascade to the next fallback (`router.py:1044-1061`). This is config-vs-catalog drift, and it is the live system's sharpest edge (see Open problems). The bucket system had the opposite property — tiers were stable, so a refresh could not orphan a slot — but it paid for that stability by hiding the model. The team judged explicit-and-fragile better than opaque-and-stable, because the failure is loud in the logs and the fix (`sync_endpoints_from_catalog.py`) is mechanical, whereas the opacity was a permanent usability tax.

**Gear downgrade over hard failure.** When no endpoint can fill a required slot at the requested gear, the router does not fail — it drops one gear and retries with a wider eligibility pool (`router.py:500`, the cascade loop in `execute`). Gear 4 → 3 → 2 → 1. A single-model install still gets an answer, just a less adversarial one. The rejected alternative was to refuse the request; that would have made the free-tier install useless the moment its one endpoint hit a rate limit.

**Curated registry authoritative for capability flags.** Rather than trust the hand-entered `vision_capable` in `routing-config.json`, an offline pipeline builds `config/model-registry.json` from OpenRouter, LiteLLM, Chatbot Arena, and Artificial Analysis, and `model_registry.overlay_routing_config` merges its capability values onto the endpoint dicts at load (`boot.py:1375`). The registry wins. This closes the `kimi-k2.6` class: a wrong flag in `routing-config.json` cannot propagate into routing because the overlay overwrites it. The tradeoff is a second source of truth that must be refreshed; when the registry is missing or malformed the overlay is a documented no-op and the routing-config values stand.

**Open problems acknowledged in the design:**

- The legacy bucket-walk code still exists in `router.py` (`resolve_endpoint`'s fallback branch at lines 338-392, and `_get_bucket_order` at 1097-1147). It is dead: `routing-config.json` has no `pipelines` block, so `_resolve_config_name` always resolves a configuration name and the bucket branch is never reached. It is a reader trap and a latent bug surface, pending removal (labelled Chunk 12).
- `vision_capable` lives in three places — `routing-config.json` endpoints, `config/models.json`, and the registry overlay — reconciled by `vision_capable_for_endpoint`. The source-of-truth consolidation is incomplete.
- Non-Mac support is untested end to end (see Platform compatibility).

### Implementation

**Entry points.** Two callers reach the router. Project tools call `model_dispatch.invoke_chat(system_prompt, user_prompt, slot=..., context=...)` (`orchestrator/model_dispatch.py:42`) — the stable, project-agnostic public API; MSI's `article_generator.py` is the reference consumer. The gear pipeline inside `boot.py` calls `get_slot_endpoint(config, slot, context, config_name)` (`boot.py:1512`) and, for the depth/breadth pair, `resolve_gear4_endpoints` (`boot.py:1593`). Both paths converge on the singleton `Router`.

**The singleton and its load.** `boot._get_router()` (`boot.py:1389`) builds one `Router` and caches it; a failed load is marked `False` and not retried until `reload_router` clears the marker. On construction the Router reads `routing-config.json`, then does three things that matter (`router.py:_build_lookup_tables`, 145):

1. Indexes endpoints by id.
2. Merges discovered local models from `config/models.json` into the in-memory endpoint table (`_merge_models_json_local_endpoints`, 165). This is the seam that lets a model you picked in the Models pane actually resolve at dispatch time even when `routing-config.json` has drifted behind the discovered set. It is runtime-only; discovery remains the source of truth for the installed local set. An entry's `status` becomes `active` and `enabled` becomes true only when its `model_path` exists on disk (`router.py:203-204`) — a model listed but not present cannot be dispatched.
3. Builds case-insensitive endpoint aliases (`_build_endpoint_aliases`, 218). The sharp edge is MiniMax: the catalog id is `minimax/minimax-m3` but the direct-provider endpoint registers as `minimax/MiniMax-M3`. Configurations get edited from both sides, so a case-equivalent id resolves instead of falling through the chain.

`load_routing_config` (`boot.py:1342`) is separate from the Router and is what `invoke_chat` calls; it reads the file and applies the registry overlay before handing the config to `get_slot_endpoint`.

**Active-configuration precedence.** `_resolve_config_name(config_name, context)` (`router.py:728`) derives the effective configuration with this precedence, most-specific first:

1. An explicit `config_name` passed by the caller (a per-run override) wins over everything.
2. For `interactive` context: the active project's `default_model_profile`, if set and resolvable, overrides the account-wide default — a project default is "closer" than the global one. Best-effort; a failure never breaks chat.
3. Otherwise the account-wide active pointer, `active_configuration.get_active_name()`.
4. For `agent` / `autonomous` context: the static map `DEFAULT_CONFIG_FOR_CONTEXT` (`router.py:45`) — both resolve to `background-default`. Automated processes are not user-steered through the active pointer.

The operative distinction here is foreground vs. background execution: interactive chat consults the user's live selection; a background pipeline run resolves against a fixed default. This is not "manual vs. agentic" — both run the same resolver; they differ only in whether a human is currently steering the pointer.

**The chain walk.** `_resolve_from_configuration` (`router.py:995`) is the live resolution path. It loads and caches the named configuration, maps the slot to a cell path via `_slot_to_cell_path` (`router.py:811`), and walks `primary` then each `fallback` id. For each candidate it applies, in order:

- **Unknown-id skip.** If the id has no matching endpoint, log and continue to the next fallback (the config-vs-catalog drift path, 1044-1061).
- **Enabled / active filter.** Skip endpoints that are disabled or not `status == "active"`.
- **Exclusion filter.** Skip ids already assigned this gear (used for diversity).
- **Same-machine block.** Skip a local endpoint on a machine already committed to another local model this gear — the MLX parallel constraint.
- **Mutex skip.** If a local endpoint's per-machine MLX mutex is currently held, skip it in favour of the next chain entry. If every remaining entry is ineligible and the only survivors are busy locals, return the first busy local so the caller's `call_model` queues at the mutex — the "no API fallback, so we wait" path (`first_busy_local`, 1041/1093).
- **Circuit-breaker skip.** If `endpoint_health.is_in_cooldown(id)` is true, skip (1088).

The first candidate that survives all filters is returned as a v2 endpoint dict, converted to the v1 shape `call_model` expects by `_to_v1_endpoint` (`router.py:1149`).

**Slot-to-cell mapping.** `_slot_to_cell_path` is the contract between the slot vocabulary and the configuration file layout: `step1_cleanup`/`rag_planner`/`classification` → `cells.utility.<slot>`; `sidebar` aliases to `utility.step1_cleanup` (they are functionally interchangeable, and auto-populated configs carry no `sidebar` cell); `depth`/`breadth` → `cells.analysis.gear{N}.<slot>` with gears 1-2 folding up to gear 3; `consolidator`/`consolidation` → `post_analysis.consolidation`; `evaluator`/`verification` → `post_analysis.verification`; `formatter` → `post_analysis.formatter`; `primary` (Gear 2 single-pass) mirrors `gear3.depth`. This map must stay in sync with `get_slot_endpoint`'s v1-slot dispatch in `boot.py` (1535-1568) — the `formatter` branch there (1541) exists because before it was added, `get_slot_endpoint` fell through to `step1_cleanup` and formatted the deliverable with a 4B model.

**Gear cascade and diversity.** `execute` (`router.py:476`) tries the requested gear, calls `resolve_gear` for every slot the gear needs, and on any unfilled slot drops a gear and retries. `resolve_gear` (394) applies adversarial diversity: for the `breadth` slot it excludes the `depth` id so breadth lands on a different model. When diversity is enforced it deliberately does *not* fall back to the same model — it lets the cascade find a wider pool at a lower gear instead. A per-config `diversity_override: false` disables this, for single-model demo configurations like `qwen-9b-only` where every slot is intentionally the same 9B endpoint (proving Ora's pipeline lifts one small model); without the override, gear 4 would downgrade all the way to gear 2 because breadth could never differ from depth.

**Gear-4 parallel safety is now a UI hint, not a control-flow gate.** Since the 2026-05-19 concurrency overhaul, `run_gear4` always submits both analysts in parallel and the per-machine MLX mutex serializes them when both land on the same local machine. `result.parallel_safe` (`router.py:529`) is computed only so the chat UI can warn "Gear 4 (sequential, ~2x runtime)" when depth and breadth are both local on one machine.

**Dispatch.** `call_model(messages, endpoint, images)` (`boot.py:13043`) routes on `endpoint["type"]`:

- **api** — wrapped in `mlx_mutex.track_api_call(id)`, a non-blocking in-flight counter for observability plus an optional sized concurrency cap. It does not block on contention; API providers handle their own concurrency.
- **local** — wrapped in the blocking `mlx_mutex.acquire(machine_id)` (`boot.py:13077`), so only one local call per machine runs at a time. This is the SIGSEGV guard. `call_local_endpoint` (`boot.py:14178`) then resolves the engine: the endpoint's `engine` field defaults to `ollama`, an explicit `engine` is honoured, and `engine == "auto"` resolves to `mlx` only when `platform.system() == "Darwin"` and `platform.machine() == "arm64"`, otherwise `ollama` (14185-14189). MLX runs in-process via `mlx_lm` with a module-level model cache; Ollama posts to `localhost:11434`.
- **anything else** — returns `"[Error] Unknown endpoint type"`.

**Failure behavior feeds the circuit breaker.** `call_model` reads the return string and records the outcome to `endpoint_health` (`boot.py:13093-13105`). A response starting with `"[Error"` is a failure; crucially, an *empty* response is also a failure (13099) — the `kimi-k2.6` proof case returned `content=None` with no exception, and treating empty as success meant the breaker never tripped and the router kept dispatching to the broken model. `endpoint_health` (`orchestrator/endpoint_health.py`) is a per-endpoint circuit breaker: **3 failures within a 60-second rolling window trips a 300-second cooldown** (`_failure_threshold = 3`, `_failure_window_seconds = 60.0`, `_cooldown_seconds = 300.0`, lines 47-49). During cooldown the router's chain walk skips the endpoint. After cooldown the next call is allowed through as a half-open probe; success clears the breaker, failure re-extends the cooldown (`is_in_cooldown`, 110). These three numbers are the trip mechanism, not a reliability budget — nothing in the code measures achieved success rates; the failure counts and cooldown are the only instrumented health signal, and all three are tunable via `configure()`.

**When resolution fails, the caller refuses honestly.** If the whole chain is exhausted, `get_slot_endpoint` returns `None` and deliberately does *not* substitute "the first active endpoint in the catalog" (`boot.py:1568-1592`). That silent substitution was the 2026-05-22 smoke-test bug: a premium config whose OpenRouter ids were unregistered got a local MLX model swapped in, which then shipped a raw "MLX model not found" string to the user. Now `run_gear3`/`run_gear4` surface a refusal that names the configured chain that failed and each entry's cooldown state (via `get_slot_chain`, `router.py:846`). A legacy `slot_assignments` fallback fires only when `config_name is None`, so it can never override an explicit publisher chain.

**Startup rewrite of the local set.** At server boot (`server/app.py:15009`), `local_model_discovery.refresh(write=True)` scans `~/ora/models/` for subdirectories with a valid MLX `config.json` and `.safetensors` files, derives id / ram_gb / type / vision_capable / recommended_roles, and rewrites `config/models.json`'s `local_models` array — preserving `commercial_models` and every other key. A no-shrink guard refuses to write whenever discovery finds fewer models than the file already records (`local_model_discovery.py::refresh`), protecting against a momentarily unmounted or half-populated models directory; deliberate removal passes `allow_shrink=True`. `active_configuration.bake_missing_presets()` creates any missing preset configs on Models-pane open.

**The offline registry pipeline.** Separate from per-request routing, a refresh pipeline builds the capability data. `scripts/sync_model_registry.py` (the 124KB primary builder) pulls OpenRouter + LiteLLM + Arena + Artificial Analysis and writes `model-registry.json`; `refresh-catalog.py`, `refresh-openrouter.py`, and `refresh-direct-apis.py` refresh the catalog and provider listings; `sync_endpoints_from_catalog.py` keeps `routing-config.json::endpoints[]` in step with the ids configurations reference. These are triggered from the server (`/api/model-registry/refresh`, `/reach/start|status`) or run as CLI. `check-model-refresh-drift.py` is a CI guard that fails if a refresh run dirtied tracked seed files.

**Live config edits reach the singleton without restart.** The V3 Models pane POSTs to `server/app.py` (`/api/configurations/*`, `/config/routing/slots`), which write the configuration/routing files and call `boot.reload_router()` (`boot.py:1406`) plus the `active_configuration` setters. `router.reload()` (`router.py:265`) re-reads the file and rebuilds the lookup tables, clearing the configuration cache so edits take effect on the next call; on a read/parse failure it keeps the prior in-memory config so the running pipeline does not degrade.

**Config surfaces.** `config/routing-config.json` (endpoints, machines, capability slots including `slots.vision_input`, diversity, and the legacy `slot_assignments`/`gear4_overrides`/`buckets` fields); `config/configurations/<name>.json`; `config/models.json` (gitignored, machine-specific; auto-managed `local_models` + hand-kept `commercial_models`); `config/model-registry.json` and `config/model-catalog.json`; `config/configuration-presets.json` (preset algorithm params); `config/family-classification.json` (same-family warning). Environment: `ORA_HOME`, `ORA_API_POOL_SIZE`, and `install-state.json::profile` for API-pool sizing (Solo `0` = uncapped, Hybrid 8, Organization 32; `mlx_mutex.PROFILE_API_POOL_SIZES`, 62).

**Vision fallback.** `resolve_vision_fallback` (`router.py:913`) is the resolution path for an image-bearing turn where the slot must be able to read the image. It checks a global `slots.vision_input` chain (`_vision_input_chain`) first; when that chain is configured it *is* the vision fallback and the method refuses to advance into the slot's generic chain, because a `vision_capable` flag is not sufficient to trust an entry — image *delivery* is per-service, and the 2026-06-01 evaluation surfaced a breadth entry that was flagged vision-capable but whose service path never attached the image. When no global chain is present it falls back to the cell's per-cell `vision_substitute` field (the legacy path, still in the code). The two coexist; the global slot takes precedence but has not fully replaced `vision_substitute`.

### Evidence & Verification

**Unit tests** live under `orchestrator/tests/` and run with:

```
cd ~/ora && /opt/homebrew/bin/python3 -m unittest discover -s orchestrator/tests
```

The routing-relevant suites, all present in the tree: `test_model_router` (core slot resolution + gear cascade), `test_router_config_name` (named-config resolution + precedence), `test_router_chain_walk` (fallback-chain reporting + circuit-breaker state in the refusal path), `test_router_vision_input` (global vision-input slot), `test_router_reload` (reload keeps prior config on failure), `test_slot_inheritance` (cell inheritance), `test_active_configuration` (63 test methods: presets, active pointer, slot setters, lifecycle), `test_model_registry` + `test_runtime_configuration_overlay` (registry overlay + `compute_picks`), `test_model_dispatch` (`invoke_chat` typed errors), `test_mlx_mutex` + `test_call_model_mutex` (per-machine mutex, API pool, `call_model` integration), `test_endpoint_health` (cooldown/probe breaker), `test_local_model_discovery` + `test_chunk8_local_models` (MLX scan + rewrite safety guard), `test_provider_registry`, `test_direct_catalog`, `test_vendor_catalog_registry`, `test_sync_endpoints_vendor_auth`, `test_refresh_catalog`, `test_refresh_openrouter`, `test_visual_routing` + `test_routing_slots_endpoint` (capability-slot routing + Models-pane endpoints).

**How to confirm resolution by hand.** `model_dispatch.get_slot_info(slot, context=...)` (`model_dispatch.py:135`) returns the endpoint a slot would resolve to without spending a call — the fastest way to see what a configuration actually binds. The circuit-breaker state for any endpoint is readable via `endpoint_health`. To confirm the local set, `local_model_discovery.refresh()` runs dry (no `write=True`) and prints what it found.

**Instrumented facts, verified against the installed tree at 7a5e8f40.** `config/model-registry.json`: `model_count = 337`, `generated_at = 2026-06-15`, `last_probe_at = null`. `config/routing-config.json`: 547 endpoints, one machine (`studio-128`), no `pipelines` block (confirming the bucket-walk path is dead), `buckets` key still present (the dead data). `config/configurations/`: 8 files — `free`, `budget`, `premium` (the three baked presets; `speed` was defined in `configuration-presets.json` but not yet baked — since then the installer bakes all four during step 7, so a fresh install has no unbaked preset), plus `user-pipeline`, `background-default`, `background-speed`, `qwen-9b-only`, and one `experiment-optimum-premium-consolidator`. (There are no `campaign-*` configuration files in the committed tree.) `active_configuration.PRESET_ORDER = ["free", "budget", "speed", "premium"]`.

**Explicitly untested / unverified surfaces:**

- **End-to-end resolution off macOS.** The Ollama fallback and the pure-API path have no non-Mac test evidence. Every "works" claim in the Platform matrix that is not macOS is "untested," not "broken."
- **Keyring off-Mac.** `user_settings` stores keys via the `keyring` library under service `"ora"`; only the macOS Keychain backend has been exercised.
- **The empirical reach-probe has not run on the shipped registry.** `last_probe_at = null` means the probe that would populate per-endpoint measured latency/reachability has not been run against this registry; the circuit breaker is the only runtime health signal.
- **`direct_catalog` and `vendor_catalog_registry` gates default ON but their live consumption is unverified.** `direct_catalog._enabled()` reads `ORA_DIRECT_CATALOG` defaulting `"1"` (ON), and `vendor_catalog_registry.enabled()` reads its flag defaulting `"1"` (ON). Both are labelled *partial* here not because a flag defaults off, but because they feed the *offline* registry-build pipeline and their runtime effect on live routing was not traced line-by-line; the AA/Arena/LiteLLM fetch logic inside the 124KB `sync_model_registry.py` was verified only at the wiring/subcommand level.
- **`call_api_endpoint` provider-specific request building** (`boot.py:13404`) was not traced per service; the per-service image-delivery claim in `resolve_vision_fallback`'s docstring is taken at its word.
- **The committed config JSONs are authoritative here; the live working tree drifts from them.** A clean checkout of 7a5e8f40 is clean — the committed `model-registry.json` / `model-catalog.json` / `routing-config.json` are what the figures above are read from. On the author's running machine those same files show as `M` (modified) because the catalog/registry refresh rewrites them at runtime; that auto-managed working-tree drift is machine-local and not part of the commit, so this document reports the committed values, not the dirty working copy.

### Expert Reading Notes

**Read in this order.** Start with `router._resolve_from_configuration` (`router.py:995`) and `_slot_to_cell_path` (811) — this is the entire live resolution path in two functions. Then `_resolve_config_name` (728) for the active-config precedence. Then `boot.get_slot_endpoint` (1512) for the v1-slot → v2 mapping, including the `formatter` branch and the single-slot depth/breadth lookup that tries the gear-4 cell before gear-3. Then `boot.call_model` (13043) and `call_local_endpoint` (14178) for dispatch, the mutex acquisition, and the `engine == "auto"` platform gate.

**Ignore the bucket-walk path.** `router.py` lines 338-392 (`resolve_endpoint`'s fallback branch) and 1097-1147 (`_get_bucket_order`) reference `self._buckets` and `pipelines[context]`, which no longer exist in the config. In production these return empty/None and are never reached, because `_resolve_config_name` always resolves a name. Do not mistake them for live logic. The module docstring still describes the router as "bucket-based" — that docstring is stale; the live engine is named-configuration cell chains.

**Where future changes most likely cause drift:**

1. **Config-vs-catalog id drift.** A configuration primary or fallback that references an id absent from `routing-config.json::endpoints[]` is silently skipped and logged (`router.py:1044-1061`). A registry/endpoint refresh that reshapes ids can quietly degrade a preset's chain. `sync_endpoints_from_catalog.py` is the coupling that must hold; if you change how ids are minted, run it and re-check the presets.
2. **Three `vision_capable` sources.** `routing-config.json` endpoints, `config/models.json`, and the registry overlay all carry it, reconciled by `vision_capable_for_endpoint` (`router.py:688`, `boot.py:1443`). Change one, check the other two. `models.json` is the intended source of truth; the migration is incomplete.
3. **The slot-to-cell contract is duplicated.** `router._slot_to_cell_path` and `boot.get_slot_endpoint`'s slot dispatch must agree on the slot vocabulary. Add a slot in one place only and it silently resolves to the wrong cell.
4. **Single-machine assumption.** `DEFAULT_MACHINE_ID = "studio-128"` is hardcoded in `router.py:41`. Multi-machine Bonjour discovery from the design doc was never built. Any local endpoint without an explicit `machine` is assumed to be on the one Studio.
5. **`CLAUDE.md` is stale on preset names and model count** (Optimum vs Speed; 358 vs 337) and still frames routing as bucket-based ("the classification slot resolves from the local-fast bucket"). Trust `active_configuration.PRESET_ORDER`, `config/model-registry.json::model_count`, and the named-configuration code, not that prose.

### Platform compatibility

Ora is developed and run on a single Apple Silicon Mac Studio. Every "works" below means "exercised on macOS." Non-Mac surfaces are marked from the code's platform gates and the absence of off-Mac test evidence — not from having been run.

| Subsystem | macOS | Windows-native | WSL | Linux |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **MLX local inference** (`call_local_endpoint` engine=`mlx`/`auto`) | works | n/a | n/a | n/a |
| **Ollama local fallback** (`localhost:11434`) | works | untested | untested | untested |
| **`mlx_mutex` SIGSEGV serialization** | works | n/a | n/a | n/a |
| **API-only / cloud routing** (OpenRouter + direct APIs) | works | untested | untested | untested |
| **API key storage** (`keyring`, service `"ora"`) | works | untested | needs-porting | untested |
| **Config/model paths** (`~/ora`, `~/ora/models` scan) | works | needs-porting | untested | untested |
| **Local-model discovery scan** (`.safetensors` glob) | works | untested | untested | untested |
| **Server startup + refresh subprocesses** | works | untested | untested | untested |
| **MLX heartbeat + machine id `studio-128`** | works | n/a | n/a | n/a |

Per-item notes:

- **MLX** runs in-process via `mlx_lm` against Apple Metal and is Apple-Silicon-only. `engine == "auto"` resolves to `mlx` only under `Darwin` + `arm64` (`boot.py:14185-14189`); everywhere else `auto` resolves to `ollama`. Marked **n/a** off-Mac because MLX cannot run there at all.
- **Ollama** is the only local path on non-Apple hardware. It is plain HTTP to `localhost:11434` (`call_local_endpoint`) and cross-platform in principle, but has no off-Mac test evidence. Marked **untested**, not "works."
- **`mlx_mutex`** guards the MLX Metal segfault. It is irrelevant where MLX is not the engine — an API-only install never takes the blocking `acquire`. Marked **n/a** off-Mac because there is no MLX to serialize.
- **API-only routing** is pure HTTP plus keyring — the zero-local-hardware path, and the most portable in principle. It is nonetheless **untested** off macOS.
- **Keyring** uses the `keyring` library: macOS Keychain on Mac; Secret Service (Linux) or Windows Credential Locker elsewhere in principle. WSL headless typically lacks a Secret Service backend, so it is marked **needs-porting**; native Windows and Linux are **untested**.
- **Paths** use POSIX `~` and forward slashes throughout (`Path.home()/'ora'/'models'`, `ORA_HOME` defaulting `~/ora`), with no `%USERPROFILE%` handling. Native Windows is **needs-porting**.
- **Local-model discovery** is a filesystem glob with no Apple calls, so it is portable in principle — but only MLX models are useful output, and MLX is Mac-only, so the scan finding anything usable off-Mac is moot. Marked **untested**.
- **Refresh subprocesses** are documented against `/opt/homebrew/bin/python3`, a Homebrew-specific interpreter path. **Untested** off-Mac.
- **`studio-128`** is `DEFAULT_MACHINE_ID`, a single hardcoded Mac Studio. Multi-machine discovery was not built, so the machine layer is **n/a** off the one supported machine.

## 5. Gear Pipeline & Mode Dispatch

### Problem

A single language model cannot reliably catch its own errors. The model that fabricates a citation, misframes a decision, or quietly answers a different question than the one asked will not flag that mistake on self-review. The blind spots are correlated: the same weights that produced the error also grade it, so "check your work" returns the same wrong answer with more confidence. This is the failure mode the whole subsystem exists to prevent — an analysis that is wrong in a way its own author cannot see.

There is a second, cheaper failure. If every prompt runs the same pipeline, a greeting, a capital-city lookup, and a wicked-problem analysis all pay the same latency and cost. A five-to-fifteen-minute adversarial cascade on "hello" is not thoroughness; it is waste. And routing that guesses wrong in the other direction is worse: an ambiguous prompt either interrogates the user with clarifying questions they already answered, or silently commits to the wrong analytical frame and produces a confident answer to the wrong problem.

So the subsystem has two jobs that pull against each other. It must spend heavily where judgment is required and independent review actually breaks correlated errors, and it must spend nothing where the prompt is trivial or already unambiguous. The routing decision that separates those cases has to be predictable — a routing layer that is itself a non-deterministic model call reintroduces exactly the unreliability it is meant to contain.

### Design Decision & Tradeoffs

Two coupled moves answer those two problems.

**Deterministic mode-and-gear routing.** A four-stage pre-routing pipeline classifies each prompt to one of 64 analytical modes across 21 territories (T1–T21, verified in `architecture/territories.md`), and the selected mode file's `## DEFAULT GEAR` heading — not a model's opinion — fixes the pipeline shape. "Gear" is the pipeline's cost/quality tier: Gear 1 is a direct single-model pass with no retrieval; Gear 2 is a single pass with RAG for factual lookups; Gear 3 is a sequential adversarial cascade on two models; Gear 4 is the parallel adversarial cascade. The distribution across the installed 64 modes is concrete and lopsided: 1 mode declares Gear 1, 2 declare Gear 2, 3 declare Gear 3, and 58 declare Gear 4 (confirmed by grep over `modes/*.md`). Judgment-heavy work is the common case; the cheap gears are the exceptions that must opt out explicitly.

The rejected alternative was the retired Mode Classification Directory, which ran an LLM intent-classification pass over a flat mode list (`CLAUDE.md` records it archived 2026-05-02, Phase 9). It was brittle and non-deterministic: the same prompt could route two ways on two runs, and a misclassification was invisible. The pre-routing pipeline replaces that with substring matching against a hand-maintained signal-vocabulary registry. The tradeoff is honest and named in the spec: substring matching is fast and reproducible, but it needs a vocabulary registry someone has to keep current. A signal the registry does not know about does not route. This buys a predictable, reproducible mechanism at the cost of a maintenance burden — the deliberate trade of a dumb transparent mechanism over a clever opaque one.

**Independent-model adversarial review.** For the 58 Gear-4 modes, two analysts — a depth stream and a breadth stream, ideally on different model families for genuine independence — analyze in parallel, then cross-evaluate, revise against each other's critique, and verify each other's revised drafts before a consolidator and formatter produce the deliverable. The reason is the Problem above: independent review catches what self-review cannot, because a second model does not share the first's correlated blind spot.

The rejected alternative was single-model-plus-self-critique — one model that drafts and then critiques its own draft. It is roughly half the cost. It was rejected because it does not break correlated errors; the self-critique inherits the same blind spot that produced the error. The tradeoff for choosing real adversarial independence is steep and stated plainly in `architecture/pre-routing-pipeline.md`: five to fifteen minutes of wall-clock latency and roughly 2x model cost for a Gear-4 turn.

**Open problems.** The reliability layer described below protects against transient model misbehaviour — refusals, clarification loops, brief stubs, tool-call leaks. It does not protect against API rate limits or upstream provider outages; the docstring in `run_gear4` (`boot.py`, the "Reliability ceiling" note at line 11749) states that raising that ceiling requires cross-provider fallback, circuit breakers, and result caching, none of which is built. The verifier is a gate, not an editor: a correction it computes (a fixed date, a fixed citation) is captured in the trace but never reaches the user, because the consolidator and formatter read the reviser's draft directly — an acknowledged architectural gap, not a bug (detailed below). And the routing layer's determinism is only as good as the signal-vocabulary registry; an unrecognized phrasing falls through to a T0 catch-all rather than the specific mode its author intended.

### Implementation

The runtime entrypoint is `boot.py::run_pipeline` (line 9053). The flow, in order: a risk gate, then slash-command and runtime short-circuits (the meta-layer `/instance`, `/render`, `/framework` commands intercept here before any model call), then Phase A prompt cleanup, then `run_pre_routing_pipeline` (line 5513), then the gear-dispatch switch (lines 9338–9394).

**The four stages.** `run_pre_routing_pipeline` runs Stages 1–3; Stage 4 is the gear execution that follows.

- *Stage 1 — pre-analysis filter* (`stage1_pre_analysis_filter`, line 3760). Substring-matches the prompt against the signal vocabulary and a short bypass list. Greetings and prior-conversation references set `bypass_to_direct_response` and the pipeline returns immediately with no mode (line 5553). A distinct branch handles Gear-2 RAG dispatch: an information request carrying a `GEAR2_RAG_TRIGGERS` substring (the trigger list is defined at line 2428) and no judgment marker skips Stage 2 entirely and dispatches straight to the `factual-lookup` mode (the `gear2_rag_dispatch` branch, line 5572). The mode file's own `## DEFAULT GEAR` supplies Gear 2; the dispatcher honours it.
- *Stage 2 — sufficiency analyzer* (`stage2_sufficiency_analyzer`, line 4241). Classifies signals strong versus weak, AND-composes them, checks cross-territory adjacency and within-territory disambiguation, and either dispatches a `mode_id` or surfaces exactly one plain-language disambiguation question. When Stage 2 finds no specific mode *and* no disambiguation conflict, it does not interrogate the user; it routes to a T0 catch-all — `subjective-inquiry` if `_has_subjective_marker` fires, else `general-inquiry`, both Gear 3 (fallback branch, lines 5599–5624). The universal adversarial scaffolding carries the discipline; the mode-specific layer is deliberately thin.
- *Stage 3 — input completeness check* (`stage3_input_completeness_check`, line 5355). Loads the dispatched mode's input contract, picks expert versus accessible framing, and either elicits a missing required field or degrades to a lighter sibling mode.
- *Stage 4 — mode execution.* `extract_default_gear` (line 5737) runs a regex over the mode file for `## DEFAULT GEAR\n\nGear N` and returns N; a mode missing the heading defaults to **Gear 3**, not Gear 2 — the fallback changed 2026-05-24 so an unlabeled mode lands in the universal adversarial pipeline rather than the retrieval-only path (docstring, lines 5739–5752). The gear switch then dispatches: `gear <= 2` runs inline single-model (Gear 1 via the `classification` slot, Gear 2 via the `fast`/`step1_cleanup`/active fallback chain); `gear == 3` calls `run_gear3` (line 11046); `gear >= 4` calls `run_gear4` (line 11691); the bare `else` (line 9389) is a defensive fallback that exists only because no Gear 5 does (`CLAUDE.md`: the model-switcher UI makes Gear 5 redundant).

**Prompt assembly — the injection model.** Every pipeline step's system prompt is built by `build_system_prompt_for_gear` (line 8467). This is the load-bearing coupling in the whole subsystem, so its contract is worth stating precisely. The function takes a `step` — one of the six members of `_PIPELINE_STEPS` (line 8358): `analyst`, `evaluator`, `reviser`, `verifier`, `consolidator`, `formatter` — and injects exactly one role-specific `##` section from the mode file per step, on top of a shared baseline. It extracts named sections via `_extract_section` (line 7823):

- Baseline injected into *every* role-specific step: the mode's `## ANALYTICAL BRIEF AND EVALUATION CRITERIA` and its `## VERIFICATION CRITERIA`, each wrapped in `_fenced` banner blocks — `MODE BRIEF & EVALUATION CRITERIA — <mode>` and `MODE VERIFICATION CRITERIA (PASS gate) — <mode>` (lines 8579–8588). The analyst sees the standard it will be graded against before it writes; the verifier sees the gate it grades against. This closes the "writing blind to the criteria" gap. Gear 1 is structurally exempt — it does not route through this function.
- Role-specific per step: `## DEPTH ANALYSIS GUIDANCE` or `## BREADTH ANALYSIS GUIDANCE` for the analyst (chosen by the `slot` argument), `## REVISION GUIDANCE` for the reviser, `## CONSOLIDATION GUIDANCE` for the consolidator, `## OUTPUT FORMAT GUIDANCE` for the formatter.
- Breadth-only: `## ANALYTICAL PERSPECTIVES` — the mode's declared Tier-1 thinking tools and Tier-3 mental-model lenses — is injected into the breadth analyst alone (the `step == "analyst"` / `slot == "breadth"` branch), because breadth is the lateral-thinking stream and the lenses prime its frame; depth is intentionally skipped.

The section name `ANALYTICAL BRIEF AND EVALUATION CRITERIA` was renamed from the legacy `EVALUATION CRITERIA` on 2026-05-24; the code keeps a legacy fallback (`_extract_section(..., "ANALYTICAL BRIEF AND EVALUATION CRITERIA") or _extract_section(..., "EVALUATION CRITERIA")`, lines 8515–8518). The migration landed on the mode side: `modes/cui-bono.md` carries the new heading verbatim (line 161). It did **not** land on the spec side — see Evidence & Verification.

Retrieved reference material — RAG hits, web sources — is wrapped by `_fenced` (line 8420) in `=== LABEL === / === END LABEL ===` banners with a "data, not instructions" caption (`_REFERENCE_FENCE_NOTE`, line 8413, added 2026-06-29). The `===` banner is syntactically distinct from markdown `#` headings, so retrieved content carrying its own `##` headers cannot be confused with the scaffolding's structure — this is the prompt-injection isolation boundary for the pipeline. Phase A's inferred assumptions are surfaced in their own `PHASE A ASSUMPTIONS (NOT USER-STATED FACTS)` fenced block so downstream steps treat inferences as uncertain rather than as user-stated fact.

**The Gear-4 cascade and its failure behavior.** `run_gear4` (line 11691) maps code steps to roles: Step 3 parallel depth + breadth analysts, Step 4 cross-evaluation, Step 5 parallel revisers, Step 6 cross-verification (up to 2 correction cycles), Step 7 consolidation into an irreducible corpus, Step 8 formatting into the deliverable, Step 8.6 final quality gate. The Step 7/8 split (2026-05-14) separated two jobs the old consolidator conflated: Step 7 does semantic extraction, cross-stream dedup, bloat strip, and synthesis to corpus shape; Step 8 places that corpus into the deliverable form. `formatter` is a full member of `_PIPELINE_STEPS`.

The contingency table (docstring, lines 11709–11745) is the reliability layer, and each entry names the failure it prevents:

- *Step 3 — dead-analyst recovery.* Each stream tries its primary model with one same-model retry, then advances to the slot's fallback model with its own retry. A stream that fails both primary and fallback is unrecoverable, and the whole turn falls back to Gear 3 — a complete single-model answer (the unrecoverable-stream fallback at lines 11870–11895) — rather than cross-evaluating one live model against an error string. This prevents a refusing or empty model from silently halving the adversarial pair.
- *Steps 4–5 — degraded-but-shipped.* An unhealthy evaluator hands the reviser `[no evaluator feedback — degraded]`; an unhealthy reviser falls back to its stream's original analyst output. The pipeline degrades rather than halting.
- *Step 6 — three-way verdict.* PASS / FAIL / BROKEN. PASS and BROKEN both unblock the cycle (re-revision cannot fix a verifier that itself errored); only FAIL triggers re-revision, capped at 2 cycles. BROKEN never counts as real verification — a `step6-cycleN-<slot>-verifier-BROKEN-not-verified` contingency lands in the health record so trend data reflects how often verification actually happened. This replaced a retired auto-PASS-on-exception path (the docstring labels it "silent failure #9").
- *Step 7 — consolidator floor.* A `_call_with_retry` with a 300-char minimum; on failure it returns the longer of the two revised drafts under a `[degraded — consolidation failed]` header so the user still sees output and knows it is degraded.
- *Step 8 / 8.5 — leak scrub.* A structural-leak gate retries the formatter once on a process-meta leak, then Step 8.5 deterministically strips residual pipeline-leak lines.
- *Step 8.6 (Gear 4) / 6.5 (Gear 3) — final quality gate.* An LLM judge on the dedicated verification slot grades the deliverable against the mode's `## VERIFICATION CRITERIA` and the consolidate/format contracts (`QUALITY_GATE_FRAMEWORK = "f-quality-gate.md"`, line 9997; passed as `framework_name` at 11529 for Gear 3, 12826 for Gear 4). A FAIL fires exactly one bounded redo of the implicated producer — the consolidator for an ANALYSIS verdict then re-format, the formatter for a FORMATTING verdict — then ships regardless. PASS and BROKEN ship as-is.

Any degradation is surfaced, never silent: `run_pipeline` prepends a degradation signal to the response (line 9397). Parallelism uses stdlib `concurrent.futures.ThreadPoolExecutor`; the earlier design gated on a `parallel_safe` flag and fell back to Gear 3 when both analysts resolved to local MLX on one machine, but since the 2026-05-19 concurrency overhaul that flag is only a UI hint — the executor submits both calls and a per-machine MLX mutex serializes them, preserving mode fidelity at roughly 2x wall-clock (lines 11769–11775).

**Config surfaces.** `config/routing-config.json` is the single source of truth for endpoints, capability routing, `slot_assignments`, and gear-4 overrides. The `slot_assignments` block names eight gear-independent model slots — `depth`, `breadth`, `evaluator`, `consolidator`, `classification`, `step1_cleanup`, `sidebar`, and `rag_planner` — and any model can fill any slot. Note that there is no `verifier` slot and no `formatter` slot in `slot_assignments`: the verifier and the quality-gate judge resolve to the post-analysis `verification` slot through the router (`resolve_post_analysis_slot("verification", ...)`, lines 1539–1540), and the formatter/consolidator draw on the analysis slots. Env flags read at runtime: `ORA_DELIVERABLE_SCRUB` (Step 8.5), `ORA_MODEL_TOOL_SELECTION` (the model-driven tool escape hatch), `ORA_RAG_SELECTION` (RAG contamination gate), `ORA_PIPELINE_TRACE` (forensic traces). Both `ORA_DELIVERABLE_SCRUB=1` (line 47) and `ORA_RAG_SELECTION=1` (line 18) are exported by `start.sh`, so those are on in the installed system.

**Status precision on two features.** The **Step 8.5 deliverable scrub** is not flag-gated-off: the scrub is guarded by `_env_flag("ORA_DELIVERABLE_SCRUB")` at its gear-4 call site (line 11648; `_env_flag` is defined at line 6973 and is true when the var is `1`), and `start.sh` exports `ORA_DELIVERABLE_SCRUB=1`, so the scrub runs by default in the installed system and is disabled only by explicitly setting it to `0` for debugging (implemented, default-on). The **model-driven tool escape hatch** is genuinely partial: `_model_tool_selection_enabled` (line 8363) defaults `ORA_MODEL_TOOL_SELECTION` to `"0"`, and `start.sh` does not export it — flag-gated-off, so partial. The **Gear-2 `fast` dispatch slot** is partial in a subtler way. The dispatch code (line 9350) prefers a `fast` slot and falls back through `step1_cleanup` to the active endpoint precisely because the slot is "being wired by the parallel model-selector thread." `routing-config.json` does hold a `"fast"` model-tier list (line 14047), but that is a tier bucket, not a `slot_assignments` entry; `get_slot_endpoint(config, "fast")` resolves against `slot_assignments`, which has no `fast` key — so the fallback chain is what actually fires today. The `fast` slot is scaffolded, not installed.

### Evidence & Verification

**Tests.** `orchestrator/tests/test_pre_routing_pipeline.py` covers all four stages plus the dispatch announcement and the `run_pre_routing_pipeline` composition. Gear-4 reliability has dedicated suites — `test_gear4_analyst_recovery.py`, `test_gear4_degradation.py`, `test_gear4_no_short_circuit.py` — alongside `test_quality_gate.py`, `test_boot_prompt_assembly.py`, `test_perspective_loader.py`, `test_deliverable_scrub.py`, and nine `test_style_*.py` files exercising the Output Style composition path. I confirmed the test files exist by directory listing and confirmed the code paths they name against the source; I did **not** execute the suite in this pass, so pass/fail is asserted from wiring, not from a run.

**Forensic traces.** When `ORA_PIPELINE_TRACE` is active, every step writes a health payload and any fired contingency under `data/pipeline-traces/<conv>/<turn>/`, including a `step-health.json` recording each step's ok/degraded state and the named contingency (for example `step8_6-quality-gate-*`, `step6-cycleN-<slot>-verifier-BROKEN-not-verified`). This is the primary way to confirm a Gear-4 turn actually ran the adversarial pair rather than silently degrading — the trace distinguishes "verified" from "verifier BROKEN" so a turn that shipped without real verification is visible after the fact. Reliability here is qualitative: the traces record *whether* each step degraded, but there is no instrumented success-rate or per-step reliability budget in the code. Any percentage would be invented; none is measured.

**Trace-completeness currency note (2026-07-16, post-pin).** PR #269 (implementation commit `99638ef3`, merge commit `241a31c0`) extends the forensic layer well beyond the state described at this document's `7a5e8f40` pin. This is a scoped current-state note for that landed subsystem, not a re-audit or re-pin of the rest of the chapter.

The current trace boundary covers the real server and CLI entrypoints, explicit direct-entry calls, framework milestones, and the production paths for Gears 1–4. It records pre-routing, context/RAG and web consultation, model-call configuration and usage, claim-evidence assembly, verification, fallback/retry paths, lifecycle outcomes, and the terminal value returned after output routing or server delivery. `step-terminal-output.json` is deliberately written after routing/persistence and includes route, output target, and `persisted`; a failed persistence response is therefore evidence with `persisted=false`, not a falsely successful terminal record. Gear 4→3 fallback markers and the last `step-health.json` correct the manifest to the Gear that actually executed.

`trace-manifest.json` preserves history instead of normalizing it away. `expected_steps` is the normally required path; `actual_steps` is the literal on-disk `step*.json` inventory; `derived_artifacts` holds summaries such as step health and cost; `missing_steps` is restricted to required work absent from a completed turn; `skipped_steps` covers optional non-execution and not-reached work on exceptional exits; `replaced_steps` names normal stages displaced by a fallback; `contingency_steps` names real exceptional stages that ran; and `unexpected_steps` contains real stages belonging to none of those declared sets. `stage_replacements` records which contingency displaced which normal stages. Direct and short-circuit paths have their own truthful expected sets rather than inheriting an impossible full-pipeline template.

Tracing remains observational and fail-open: trace start, step writes, terminal capture, inventory refresh, and manifest finalization catch their own failures and cannot change the production return value. Raw turn directories intentionally contain exact forensic inputs and outputs. The browser and HTML-export trust boundary is different: recursive safe projections replace every raw string/byte sequence with type, length, and SHA-256 metadata, hash unrecognized keys, refuse unsafe paths/symlinks/oversized files, and emit fixed error codes. Private Dialogues retain local evidence but are labeled `redaction_level=private`; browser/export surfaces omit their raw content and diagnostic turns retain the private routing tag. Stealth suppresses trace creation, and protected Delete Forever purges any Ora-managed residue. Trace Walk inspection and export are read-only with respect to production behavior and original evidence; pin/unpin changes only retention metadata, and **Investigate** creates a separate same-Dialogue P-Debug turn rather than replaying or modifying the traced execution.

**Confirmed drift (trust the code).** Three documentation-versus-code divergences are real and load-bearing enough to name:

1. `frameworks/book/f-evaluate.md` and `f-revise.md` still reference the mode section `## EVALUATION CRITERIA` (f-evaluate lines 3, 11, 206, 211). The modes and `boot.py` (lines 8515–8518) renamed it to `## ANALYTICAL BRIEF AND EVALUATION CRITERIA` on 2026-05-24. The code keeps a legacy fallback, so this is functional but doc-stale — a reader following the F-stage spec will look for a section name the mode files no longer use.
2. `architecture/pre-routing-pipeline.md` says "56 deep-analysis modes" (line 23) and its Stage 4 still describes loading runtime config from `runtime-configuration.md` (line 235) with error-on-missing per Decision C. Both are stale: 58 modes declare Gear 4, and `runtime-configuration.md` was retired 2026-05-12 — runtime fields now live in each mode's `## DEFAULT GEAR` / `## RAG PROFILE`.
3. `boot/boot.md` line 59 reads "Seventeen modes, four gears." The installed system has 64 modes. The gear count is right; the mode count is stale.

Additionally, `f-quality-gate.md` is wired (Gear-3 step 6.5, Gear-4 step 8.6) but is absent from `pre-routing-pipeline.md`'s stage description and from the F-stage inventory — an undocumented pipeline step relative to the architecture spec. And `architecture/` holds 9 files (`trusted-web-sources.md` is the ninth), while `CLAUDE.md` Decision K enumerates only 8.

**Explicitly untested surfaces.** The Gear-2 `fast` dispatch slot (fallback path fires; the slot itself is not wired into `slot_assignments`), the model-driven tool escape hatch (`ORA_MODEL_TOOL_SELECTION` default off, unexercised in normal operation), and every non-macOS platform (see below). I did not read `server.py::_pipeline_stream` in this pass, so the `manual_mode_selection` override and the `/chat` bypass guard are asserted from `pre-routing-pipeline.md` prose and `CLAUDE.md`, not re-verified against server code.

### Expert Reading Notes

Read in this order. Start with `architecture/pre-routing-pipeline.md` for the *why* of the four stages (discount its "56 modes" and `runtime-configuration.md` language as stale). Then read `build_system_prompt_for_gear` (`boot.py:8467`) to see exactly what each step's prompt contains — this is where the whole subsystem's behavior is actually decided. Then `run_gear4` (line 11691) for the cascade and its contingency table, and `run_gear3` (line 11046) for the sequential variant and the step-6.5 gate.

The single highest drift risk is the coupling between mode-file `## SECTION` names and the `_extract_section` calls at lines 8509–8523. A renamed section does not error; it silently returns empty and drops that step's guidance. The `EVALUATION CRITERIA` → `ANALYTICAL BRIEF AND EVALUATION CRITERIA` rename already left `f-evaluate.md` and `f-revise.md` stale, and the code only survives it because someone added a legacy fallback. If you rename or add a mode section, grep every `_extract_section` call and every `f-*.md` spec before assuming it took effect. This is the place future changes most likely cause silent regressions.

Second: the F-stage spec files in `frameworks/book/` are documentation that must track `boot.py`'s step wiring, and they have drifted before (`f-verify.md` was corrected 2026-05-22 after the Step 7/8 split left it describing a post-consolidation verifier that no longer exists). Treat the specs as intent and the boot.py step map as truth. Note in particular the verifier's known gap: `f-verify.md` states plainly (lines 7, 26) that the verifier's `### Verified Final Output` is captured in the trace but **not propagated** — the consolidator and formatter read the reviser's `revised_depth` / `revised_breadth` directly. A correction the verifier computes never reaches the user. That is by design, but it surprises readers who assume verification edits the output.

Third: the pipeline's "8 steps" and its 6 named pipeline roles are different countings. `_PIPELINE_STEPS` has six roles (analyst, evaluator, reviser, verifier, consolidator, formatter); the "8 steps" language counts Phase A cleanup as step 1, context assembly as step 2, and the quality gate at 6.5/8.6. Do not try to reconcile them into one sequence.

Fourth: `orchestrator/dispatcher.py` is named like a mode/gear dispatcher but is the *tool* dispatcher (permission gating, path validation, audit logging). The actual gear dispatch is the switch inside `boot.py::run_pipeline` at lines 9338–9394. This mis-maps easily from the file list.

Finally, treat `CLAUDE.md`'s mode and file counts as unreliable: 56, 58, 60, 64, and 65 all appear across the docs. The verified numbers at 7a5e8f40 are 64 modes (65 files in `modes/`, one being `INDEX.md`) with a 1/2/3/58 Gear-1/2/3/4 distribution, and 9 files in `architecture/`.

### Platform compatibility

The installed system runs on macOS on Apple Silicon and is verified only there. Per-subsystem checks:

- **Inference engine (depth/breadth analyst calls, Gears 3/4).** `platform_check.py::detect_platform` routes `Darwin` + `arm64` to MLX and everything else to Ollama (the `recommended_engine` branch at line 31). MLX is Apple-Silicon-only. **macOS: works. Windows-native / WSL / Linux: n/a for MLX** — those platforms are routed to Ollama by design, but that Ollama path for the gear analysts was **not run off-Mac and is untested.**
- **Parallel-analyst execution (`run_gear4`).** Uses stdlib `concurrent.futures.ThreadPoolExecutor` (portable) plus a Mac-specific MLX mutex that serializes same-machine local calls. **macOS: works. Windows-native / WSL / Linux: untested** — the ThreadPoolExecutor is portable in principle; the MLX serialization is the Mac-specific part and does not apply elsewhere.
- **RAM detection (`platform_check.get_system_ram_gb`).** Has explicit branches for Darwin (`sysctl`), Linux (`/proc/meminfo`), and Windows (`wmic`), with a 16 GB fallback (lines 40–59). **macOS: works. Linux / Windows-native: code path exists but untested. WSL: untested** (would take the Linux branch).
- **Root paths (`runtime_paths.py`).** Built from `expanduser('~')`, env-overridable via `ORA_HOME` / `ORA_VAULT`, with `norm_key` using `os.path.normcase` + `realpath` for Windows-safe prefix checks. **macOS: works. Windows-native / WSL / Linux: untested** — cross-platform in construction, POSIX-shaped in its defaults, never actually run off-Mac.
- **Interpreter and launcher.** `CLAUDE.md` and `start.sh` hardcode `/opt/homebrew/bin/python3` (a Homebrew Mac path) and `start.sh` is a bash launcher exporting the pipeline env flags (`ORA_DELIVERABLE_SCRUB`, `ORA_RAG_SELECTION`, and others). A Windows `start.bat` ships but does not export those pipeline flags (flag-incomplete), and there is no `.ps1` equivalent. **macOS: works. Windows-native: untested + flag-incomplete** — `start.bat` launches the server but a Windows run inherits none of the pipeline flags, so the pipeline is silently degraded and it has not been run on Windows. **WSL / Linux: untested** — bash would run, but the Homebrew path and package availability are Mac-specific and unverified elsewhere.
- **Server startup (`server.py` → `boot.py` pipeline, localhost:5000, Flask).** **macOS: works. Windows-native / WSL / Linux: untested** — localhost binding is portable in principle, but the server has not been started off-Mac. (The `/chat` pipeline is synchronous JSON with browser polling, not SSE — see the Server chapter; SSE survives only on three residual endpoints.)
- **Trace writes (`data/pipeline-traces/` via `pipeline_trace`).** Path-based writes through `runtime_paths`. **macOS: works. Windows-native / WSL / Linux: untested** — filesystem case-sensitivity differs on Windows; `normcase` handles the prefix checks but the write path itself was not exercised off-Mac.

No non-macOS surface is claimed to work; every one above is labeled untested or needs-porting where it was not actually run off-Mac.

## 6. Framework Loading & Execution

### Problem

An Ora framework is a long adversarial-analysis specification — Process Formalization Framework v2.1, for one, runs to thousands of words of layered instructions. If I hand that whole document to a model as a single prompt, three failure modes follow. The model truncates: it reads the first layers, produces something plausible, and never reaches the verification criteria at the bottom. The model wanders: with no enforced checkpoints, milestone three's output drifts from what the user actually asked in milestone one, and nothing catches the drift until a human reads the final artifact. And the model conflates: it collapses a five-stage procedure into one undifferentiated pass, losing the reproducibility that made the framework worth writing down. A framework that is only prose is a framework the model is free to ignore.

There is a second, orthogonal risk. The directory `~/ora/frameworks/book/` holds 39 markdown files. Most are not user-facing frameworks. Eight are internal Gear-4 pipeline-stage specs — `f-analysis-depth.md`, `f-evaluate.md`, `f-consolidate.md`, and their siblings — that the gear pipeline loads automatically and that make no sense invoked standalone: running `/framework f-verify "…"` would ask the model to perform a per-stream verification pass against nothing. If file existence in `frameworks/book/` were treated as permission to invoke, every one of those internal stages, plus support specs like `phase-a-prompt-cleanup.md` and `supplemental-rag-protocol.md`, would be exposed through the same `/framework` command that runs Problem Evolution. That is a namespace-hygiene failure: the invocation surface must be a curated subset of the file surface, not its mirror.

This subsystem solves both problems. It parses spec prose into an executable milestone graph, runs that graph as milestone-bounded gear passes with a drift check at each boundary, and gates which files a user may invoke through a hand-maintained allow-list.

### Design Decision & Tradeoffs

**The milestone is the unit of execution.** The parser (`framework_parser.py`) reads a framework's `## MILESTONES DELIVERED` section and extracts each `### Milestone N:` (single-mode) or `#### Milestone N:` (multi-mode) block into a `Milestone` dataclass, binding its properties inline: the endpoint it produces, the verification criterion that defines success, the layers it covers, the gear it runs at, the milestones it requires first, and a drift-check question. The executor (`milestone_executor.py`) then runs the framework as a sequence: for each milestone, assemble a structured handoff packet, run it through one gear pass, persist the deliverable, and check it for drift before advancing.

The decision I want to defend here is reuse over parallel machinery. A framework execution *is* adversarial analysis — depth pass, breadth evaluation, revision, consolidation, verification. Ora already has that machinery in `run_gear4`. So one milestone equals one `run_gear4` call over a structured handoff packet (`_run_through_gear_pipeline`, `milestone_executor.py:565-607`). I rejected the alternative of building a separate framework-specific review path. A parallel path would have duplicated the entire gear pipeline's adversarial logic and then drifted out of sync with it the first time someone tuned the gear-4 consolidation step. Instead the framework layer is thin: it decomposes the spec into milestones and lets the existing pipeline do the analytical work. The tradeoff is that a framework can only be as adversarial as the gear it declares; a milestone marked `gear: 1` gets a single-pass model call with no cross-evaluation (`milestone_executor.py:586-607`).
