**Multi-mode routing is deterministic-first, LLM-last.** Frameworks like Problem Evolution declare several operating modes (PE-Init, PE-Iterate, PE-Review, PE-Spawn). Picking the right one for a given user request is a routing problem, and I did not want to pay a model call for it in the common case. `select_mode` is a four-tier cascade (`milestone_executor.py:734-793`): an explicit mode prefix as the first token of the input (consumed, cheap, exact); an in-input mention of a mode name anywhere in the text; only then a small-model routing classifier on the sidebar slot; and finally a default to the first declared mode. The first two tiers are pure string matching. The classifier — the only tier that costs a model call — fires only when no textual signal is present, and it degrades to the first-mode default when no endpoint is available (`_llm_select_mode` returning `None`). This is the same design principle as a fast-path/slow-path cache: spend the expensive resource only on the inputs that actually need it.

**Invocability is a curated allow-list, not file existence.** `framework_invocability.py` loads `config/framework-invocability.json`, which declares three disjoint sets: `invocable_frameworks` (11 entries, runnable through `/framework`), `pickable_frameworks` (11 entries, the same public set surfaced in the picker UI), and `internal_only_frameworks` (10 entries). The loader unconditionally folds the eight Gear-4 F-* stage IDs into the internal set regardless of the JSON (`framework_invocability.py:86-91`), so even a mis-edited config cannot accidentally expose a pipeline stage. `resolve_user_invocable_framework` maps a user token through aliases (`cff` → `corpus-formalization.md`, plus `deep-research`, `knowledge-artifact-coaching`, `off`, and `pff`) and raises `FrameworkInvocabilityError` with a specific message when the token is internal-only or unregistered (`framework_invocability.py:124-149`). The rejected alternative — glob `frameworks/book/` and expose everything — would have made the F-* internal stages invocable and coupled the user-facing surface to an implementation-detail directory.

**Config binding was designed as opt-in, and remains unwired.** `framework_config.py` implements `${config.<key>}` substitution and `<!-- ora-project-extension: <id> -->` overlay splicing, so a project-bound spec can carry project-specific values (a ChromaDB collection name, say) without hardcoding them. The design decision was to keep this a separate opt-in layer — `compose_framework_spec` — so the default execution path stays project-neutral and the executor keeps parsing raw spec text. That decision is sound. But the honest status is that `compose_framework_spec` has no production call site: grep finds it only in `framework_config.py` itself and its test file, and `framework_config` is imported by nothing in production (verified against `orchestrator/` and `server/` at 7a5e8f40). The module's own docstring says Chunk 4 — the migration of `document-processing.md` — "switches that one framework's call sites over" (`framework_config.py:43-46`); that migration did not land. This is partial: coded, unit-tested, and orphaned. The runtime path in `execute_framework` calls `parse_framework_file` on raw text and never touches the config binder (`milestone_executor.py:254`).

**The strict parser is a deliberate tradeoff that leaves DCA non-executable.** The parser fails loudly: a framework whose `## MILESTONES DELIVERED` section is absent raises `FrameworkParseError` rather than silently producing an empty milestone list (`framework_parser.py:100-101`). I prefer loud failure — a spec that doesn't conform should not run half-formed. The cost is that Decision Clarity Analysis (`decision-clarity-analysis.md`), named as one of the six canonical frameworks, has no `## MILESTONES DELIVERED` section. Parsing it raises `FrameworkParseError: decision-clarity-analysis: no '## MILESTONES DELIVERED' section found` (verified by direct invocation at 7a5e8f40), and it is absent from all three lists in `framework-invocability.json` (verified: `grep -c` returns 0). So the operative status is precise: PEF, PIF, PFF, CFF, and OFF parse and route as multi-mode frameworks (all five verified to yield their declared mode lists), but DCA is an analytical specification only — it cannot run through the milestone executor. The canonical framework suite is closed at six members; five are executable, DCA is not.

**Open problems.** Three are load-bearing. First, `DRIFT_DETECTED` is log-and-continue: when the drift check flags that a milestone wandered, the executor records the reasoning and proceeds anyway (`milestone_executor.py:321-324`); pause-and-surface-to-user is deferred. Second, per-framework default configuration via `framework-routing.json` is coded but inert — the file contains only an underscore-prefixed `_example_pef` stub that the loader ignores, so the lookup always falls through to the Router's auto-derivation (`config/framework-routing.json`; lookup at `_lookup_framework_default_configuration`, `milestone_executor.py:141`). Third, the orphaned config-binding layer above.

### Implementation

**Dispatch chain.** Both `orchestrator/boot.py` and `server/app.py` detect the `/framework ` prefix and route to `run_framework_command` (`boot.py:9158-9194`, `server.py:3253-3290`). Each call site is gated by an Execution Review Phase 2 check before dispatch (the comment reads "Execution Review Phase 2 (condition 4)" at both sites). `run_framework_command` (`milestone_executor.py:994`) is the top-level entrypoint: it calls `parse_framework_command` to split `/framework <name> [--config <ConfigName>] [<query>]` into a resolved filename, a query, and an optional config name. The filename is resolved through `resolve_user_invocable_framework`, so an internal or unregistered token is rejected here with a bracketed error string. Every failure mode in this function is caught and returned as a bracketed string rather than raised (`FileNotFoundError`, `FrameworkParseError`, `NotImplementedError`, catch-all `Exception`), because the chat UI must always receive a renderable response. A framework that fails to parse produces `[Framework parse error: …]` in the transcript, not a stack trace.

An empty query is a deliberate fork, not an error: `run_framework_command` treats it as a usage message, while the chat handlers route empty-query invocations to interactive multi-turn elicitation (documented at `parse_framework_command`, `milestone_executor.py:890`).

**Execution.** `execute_framework` (`milestone_executor.py:205-411`) parses the file, resolves the effective config name (explicit arg → `framework-routing.json` default → `None`, at which point the Router auto-derives from execution context), then branches on `is_multi_mode`. Multi-mode frameworks call `select_mode`; the chosen mode's milestones are pulled from `milestones_by_mode`. If the selected mode is mechanical (C-Instance, C-Validate, O-Render), the executor short-circuits before creating scratch or calling any model and returns a redirect (`milestone_executor.py:272`, `_build_mechanical_redirect` at 693). This prevents a specific failure: a mechanical mode's real job is to create a file on disk via a runtime function; routed through the gear pipeline, a model would instead hallucinate an artifact. The redirect points the user at the matching slash command (`/instance`, `/validate`, `/render`).

For a live mode, the executor creates a `ScratchSession` (`scratch.py`), emits a `FrameworkStarted` oversight event, and iterates the milestones. Each milestone runs through `_run_milestone` (`milestone_executor.py:416-456`), which builds the handoff packet once and retries the gear pass up to `MAX_RETRIES = 3` times with `2**(attempt-1)` backoff (`milestone_executor.py:449-451`). On success it writes the deliverable to scratch and runs the drift check; on the third failure it raises `MilestoneExecutionError`, which `execute_framework` catches to mark the scratch session failed — preserving it for inspection — and emit `MilestoneBlocked` plus a failed `FrameworkComplete` (`milestone_executor.py:369-411`). On overall success, scratch is cleaned up and the last milestone's deliverable becomes the final output.

**Handoff packet.** `_build_handoff_packet` (`milestone_executor.py:461-527`) assembles the user message for the gear pass from: the original user input; prior milestone deliverables read from scratch, keyed on the milestone's `required_prior` list; the current milestone header; the layer instruction bodies for the layers this milestone covers (looked up through `_collect_layer_bodies` at `milestone_executor.py:530`, which tries exact label, punctuation-stripped label, then integer match); the output specification; the verification criterion; and any conditional layers. When a milestone references layers whose `## LAYER` blocks are absent, the packet does not silently proceed — it inlines a visible note recording the gap. This matters because the packet, not the raw spec, is what the model sees; a missing layer body would otherwise be an invisible omission.

**Gear invocation.** `_run_through_gear_pipeline` (`milestone_executor.py:565-607`) wraps the packet in a `context_pkg` and dispatches by declared gear: `run_gear4` at gear ≥ 4 (default), `run_gear3` at gear 3, single-model pass below. It deliberately bypasses Phase A cleanup and mode classification — the handoff is framework-generated structured content, not raw human input, so re-running input hygiene on it would be wrong. The `config_name` threads through to the gear functions so a named configuration routes the whole pass.

**Drift check.** `_run_drift_check` (`milestone_executor.py:627-669`) runs on the sidebar slot (`DRIFT_CHECK_SLOT = "sidebar"`, a small cheap model). It prompts a drift-detection auditor with the user's original input, the milestone deliverable, and the milestone's drift question, demanding a structured `STATUS:` reply parsed by `_parse_drift_response` (`milestone_executor.py:672`). Three outcomes: `IN_SCOPE`, `DRIFT_DETECTED`, or `DRIFT_CHECK_SKIPPED` — the last when no drift question is declared, no endpoint is available, or the model call raises. The check names the failure mode it prevents: silent scope creep across a multi-milestone procedure.

**Oversight events.** Every boundary emits through the in-process bus (`FrameworkStarted`, `MilestoneComplete`, `FrameworkComplete`, `MilestoneBlocked`), each carrying the selected mode. The emit function is lazily imported and stubbed to a no-op when the oversight infrastructure isn't loaded (`milestone_executor.py:243-249`), so the executor runs standalone. Standalone invocations (no `project_nexus`) still emit but are filtered out downstream by the oversight router.

**Config surfaces.** Four: `config/framework-invocability.json` (the allow-list); `config/framework-routing.json` (per-framework default configuration — stub-only today); the `--config <ConfigName>` flag on the command; and `ORA_HOME`, honored only by `framework_config.py:60`. Note the workspace-root divergence: `framework_parser.py:20` hardcodes `~/ora/frameworks/book` and `scratch.py:22` hardcodes `~/ora/scratch`, both via `os.path.expanduser` and both ignoring `ORA_HOME`, while `framework_config.py:60` respects it. A relocated install would find the config binder and the parser disagreeing about where the workspace lives.

**A one-shot self-persistence side effect.** `_maybe_persist_self_mindspec` (`milestone_executor.py:155-202`) fires only when the framework is `mindspec-interview` and the mode is `MSI-Self`: it archives the full self-portrait to `~/ora/mindspec/self-spec.md` and writes a projected assistant-directives block into `~/ora/mind.md` (via `mind_projection`, falling back to the verbatim spec on projection failure). It is best-effort and never raises. This is the one place the executor writes outside scratch, and it is narrowly gated so an agent or character spec can never clobber the user's values.

### Evidence & Verification

**Passing test suites** covering this subsystem: `test_multi_mode_dispatch.py` (28 tests) covers the four-tier priority chain, the LLM-classifier response parsing, catalog construction, end-to-end `execute_framework` with a mocked gear pipeline, mode-tagged oversight events, and the mechanical-mode redirect, plus live-parse smoke tests against the canonical CFF and OFF files. `test_framework_invocability.py` (4 tests) covers the allow-list resolution and alias handling. `test_framework_config.py` (35 tests) covers `parse_config_interface`, `substitute_config_refs`, `splice_extension_overlays`, and `compose_framework_spec` end-to-end against synthetic frameworks. `test_chunk3_per_process_config.py` (10 tests) and `test_router_config_name.py` (26 tests) cover named-configuration threading. These counts are the `def test_` totals in each file at 7a5e8f40; the subsystem carries no instrumented aggregate suite number.

**Live parser proof.** Running `parse_framework_file` against each canonical framework at 7a5e8f40 confirms: `problem-evolution` → `['PE-Init', 'PE-Iterate', 'PE-Review', 'PE-Spawn']`; `process-inference` → `['P-Infer', 'P-Debug', 'P-Decompose', 'P-Formalize', 'P-Feasibility']`; `process-formalization` → `['F-Design', 'F-Convert', 'F-Render', 'F-Audit']`; `corpus-formalization` → `['C-Design', 'C-Modify', 'C-Instance', 'C-Validate']`; `output-formalization` → `['O-Design', 'O-Modify', 'O-Render', 'O-Audit']`. The negative case is equally confirmed: `decision-clarity-analysis.md` raises `FrameworkParseError: decision-clarity-analysis: no '## MILESTONES DELIVERED' section found`, and `grep -c decision-clarity config/framework-invocability.json` returns 0.

**How to confirm end-to-end.** Invoke `/framework problem-evolution PE-Init walk through the new project` in chat: the explicit prefix consumes `PE-Init`, the remaining text becomes the effective input, and `format_execution_result` surfaces the selected mode with a mode-selection line in the header. A `/framework f-verify …` invocation returns a bracketed `FrameworkInvocabilityError` message — proof the internal-stage boundary holds. A `/framework cff "create instance for May"` invocation returns the mechanical-mode redirect rather than running the gear pipeline.

**Explicitly untested surfaces.** `framework_input_gap.py` has no dedicated unit-test file in `orchestrator/tests/`; its two analysis paths (structured `## Setup Questions` and LLM-fallback `## INPUT CONTRACT`) are verified only indirectly through its `server/app.py` call site at `/api/framework/analyze-inputs`. `compose_framework_spec` is unit-tested but has zero production call sites — its behavior in situ is unverified because nothing in production invokes it. The `framework-routing.json` per-framework default path is exercised by no real mapping — only the ignored `_example_pef` stub exists, so the non-trivial branch of `_lookup_framework_default_configuration` never fires in production. There is no instrumented reliability metric on milestone execution: `MAX_RETRIES = 3` is a fixed retry count, and the fraction of milestones that pass drift, retry, or fail is not measured. Non-macOS platforms are entirely untested.

### Expert Reading Notes

Read `framework_parser._parse_properties` and `_extract_milestones_section` first. The entire executor depends on that regex schema — properties are parsed from `- **PropertyName:** value` bullets, and a spec that deviates from that exact shape silently loses the property (the value defaults to empty string, and the milestone runs with a missing endpoint or verification criterion). If you are debugging a milestone that runs but produces the wrong deliverable, suspect a malformed property bullet before anything else. Then read `milestone_executor.select_mode` (734-793) and `_build_handoff_packet` (461-527) — those two functions determine which milestones run and what the model actually sees.

Four drift hotspots, where future changes are most likely to cause silent breakage:

1. **Stale docstrings.** `milestone_executor.py:19-24` still claims single-mode is "the MVP target" and multi-mode routing is "not yet wired in (an executor TODO)." That is false at 7a5e8f40 — `select_mode` and full multi-mode dispatch are implemented and tested. Trust the code, not the docstring.

2. **Orphaned config binding.** `framework_config.py`'s docstring describes a Chunk-4 migration of `document-processing.md` that never landed. `compose_framework_spec` is dead in the runtime path. If a new framework adds a `## CONFIGURATION INTERFACE` section expecting `${config.*}` substitution, the executor will not perform it — it parses raw text. This is the single most likely place for a future contributor to build on an assumption the runtime doesn't honor.

3. **DCA in the canonical six.** Anyone reasoning about "the six frameworks" as a uniform executable set is wrong: DCA is a spec-only analytical framework. Check the invocability JSON before assuming a framework runs.

4. **Workspace-root divergence.** `framework_parser.py:20` and `scratch.py:22` hardcode `~/ora` paths; `framework_config.py:60` honors `ORA_HOME`. Any relocation work must reconcile these three, or the parser and the (currently dormant) config binder will read from different roots.

### Platform compatibility

- **`FRAMEWORKS_DIR` (`framework_parser.py:20`, `os.path.expanduser("~/ora/frameworks/book")`)** — macOS: works. Linux: untested; `expanduser` resolves `~` on POSIX, but the hardcoded `~/ora` layout has not been run off Mac. WSL: untested, same basis. Windows-native: needs-porting — the hardcoded `~/ora` layout and forward-slash path have not been exercised off POSIX.
- **`framework_config.FRAMEWORKS_DIR` / `ORA_HOME` override (`framework_config.py:60-61`)** — macOS: works. Linux / WSL: untested. Windows-native: untested. Note the intra-subsystem inconsistency: this module honors `ORA_HOME`; `framework_parser.py` and `scratch.py` do not. On any platform where the install is relocated via `ORA_HOME`, the parser and config binder resolve different roots — though since `compose_framework_spec` is unwired in production, this is latent, not active, today.
- **`framework_invocability.CONFIG_PATH` (`framework_invocability.py:16-20`, `Path(__file__).resolve().parent.parent / "config" / …`)** — macOS: works. `pathlib` relative resolution is cross-platform in principle, but not actually run off Mac. Linux / WSL / Windows-native: unknown-untested.
- **Scratch writes (`scratch.py:22`, `SCRATCH_ROOT = ~/ora/scratch/<execution_id>/`) and the failure-message path (`milestone_executor.py:968`, hardcoded `~/ora/scratch/…`)** — macOS: works. Linux / WSL: untested. Windows-native: needs-porting — same hardcoded POSIX layout as the parser.
- **Local model dispatch for drift check and LLM mode-select (sidebar slot)** — macOS: works. Linux / WSL / Windows-native: the *local* slots are Apple-silicon-only. Ora's local models run on MLX/Metal. Off-Mac, only cloud slots are available; when no endpoint resolves, the drift check returns `DRIFT_CHECK_SKIPPED` and `_llm_select_mode` returns `None` (the cascade then defaults to the first declared mode). Framework execution itself does not require a local model — it degrades to reduced routing precision and skipped drift checks, not failure. This degradation path is coded but has not been exercised off Mac.
- **`compose_framework_spec` overlay reads (`framework_config.py`, utf-8, `OSError`-guarded)** — macOS: works in unit tests. Linux / WSL / Windows-native: untested. Effectively n/a in production today: the entire config-binding path has no production caller, so no platform actually exercises it at runtime.
- **Untested off-Mac overall:** Windows-native, WSL, and Linux have not been run for this subsystem end-to-end. Any "works" above is scoped to macOS; POSIX `expanduser`/`pathlib` semantics are the *reason to expect* Linux/WSL portability, not evidence of an executed run.

## 7. Output Styles & Personal Values

### Problem

An analytical pipeline that produces the right answer in the wrong voice has still failed the user. But voice is not one knob. A user wants two independent controls: *how* the assistant talks — tone, arrangement, diction, the genre it writes in — and *what values* it holds — its directness, its willingness to disagree, its ethical red lines. Bolt these together into a single "personality" and you get a system where changing the writing style silently changes the ethics, and where a request for a warmer tone leaks into a request for softer verdicts.

Two failure modes must be prevented specifically.

The first is granularity collapse: injecting the same voice instruction into a two-line conversational reply and a 3,000-word deliverable. A fast reply at gear 1 needs a disposition — is this assistant blunt or diplomatic, warm or cool. A produced artifact at gear 4 needs that *plus* an arrangement schema, a craft floor, and a diction glossary. One undifferentiated blob is either too heavy for the quick reply or too thin for the deliverable.

The second, and the more dangerous, is style contaminating substance. If the same voice layer that shapes prose also reaches the evaluator or the verifier — the adversarial steps whose entire job is to catch errors — then a style mismatch can lower a verdict. A user who asked for a "challenging" demeanor could, through that alone, get findings marked down. Style is secondary to substance. The system has to encode that ordering mechanically, not merely hope for it.

The values layer has its own failure mode: clobbering. A user might spend two minutes in a guided wizard, then months later run a multi-hour structured interview, then hand-edit the result. If those three authoring paths write the same file without knowing about each other, each overwrites the last and the user loses work silently.

This chapter covers the subsystem that answers all four: the prompt-injection voice/values layer, distinct from and secondary to the gear pipeline described in the pipeline-architecture chapters. It is authoritative for what the vault documents as the Output Styles pane; see [[Reference — Ora Output Styles Pane]] and [[Specification — Output Style Registry]].

### Design Decision & Tradeoffs

The concern splits into two orthogonal, independently-toggled layers with an explicit precedence chain baked into every injected block. In the module's own words (`orchestrator/style_assembly.py:13`) the ordering is:

```
values floor > completeness floor > craft floor > substance/findings > style
```

That ordering is not decorative — a condensed form of it ships verbatim inside the injected text, so the model reads its own precedence constraint. The gear-2 demeanor block emits `PRECEDENCE: values floor > completeness > craft > substance > style` (`style_assembly.py:164`); the gear-3/4 style block emits `PRECEDENCE: values floor > completeness > craft > findings > style` (`:175`). The precedence is *instructed*, not mechanically gated — a prompt line, not a runtime guard — and that is a weaker guarantee than a hard gate. What *is* mechanical is where the block gets injected and which steps see it.

Output Styles are gear-conditional. At gears 1-2 (fast conversational replies) the system injects a compact `## DEMEANOR` block, framed *high* in the prompt, before substance — because at that granularity the disposition *is* the instruction. At gears 3-4 (produced output) it injects the full `## STYLE` block, appended *after* the step's substance, and only on the producing/shaping steps: analyst, reviser, consolidator, formatter. The evaluator and verifier are excluded outright (`orchestrator/boot.py:8711`). This is the mechanical answer to the substance-contamination failure mode: a style mismatch physically cannot reach the step that renders a verdict, because the injection code never runs there.

Personal values live in a single user-authored `mind.md`, appended by the boot loader as an authoritative block, gated on one setting. It is one file with one save destination that equals its load source — no parallel copies to drift.

The main alternative rejected was a single unified persona blob injected uniformly across all steps. It is simpler to build and simpler to reason about, and it was rejected for three concrete reasons. It cannot express gear-granularity — the same text hits the two-line reply and the long deliverable. It cannot express substance-first ordering — a uniform injection is by definition not "after substance, producing steps only." And it entangles configurable tone with non-configurable values, so a user editing their writing genre is one careless edit away from editing their ethical floor. The split costs more surface area; it buys the two guarantees the blob cannot make.

The tradeoff on the values side is three authoring paths for one file: a two-minute guided wizard, a raw editor, and a projection from a multi-hour structured interview. Three writers on one file is exactly the clobbering hazard. It is paid for with a provenance-marker state machine: a composed `mind.md` carries an HTML-comment marker declaring who wrote it — `<!-- ora-mind-guided: … -->` (`orchestrator/mind_guided.py:36`) or `<!-- ora-mind-projected: … -->` (`orchestrator/mind_projection.py:55`) — while the stock default file carries only a plain text marker line (`*Default configuration…*`, `mind.md:3`) that reads as un-customized. The write endpoints read the marker before overwriting and refuse to clobber a hand-edited or interview-authored file without explicit `confirm_overwrite` (`server/app.py:1339`). This lets a 2-minute wizard and a multi-hour interview coexist on the same file without either silently eating the other.

**Open problems.** Two are load-bearing and are flagged plainly here.

First, the situational-delta and register-switching chain is designed and coded but unwired at runtime — status *partial*. `style_assembly.py` fully implements one-rung demeanor swaps (`resolve_demeanor`, `:98`) and a conversational-register overlay (inside `compose`, `:218`), and both are unit-tested in isolation. But the runtime context package hardcodes `style_register: "written"` and `style_deltas: None` (`orchestrator/boot.py:7767-7768`), and no code path sets them otherwise. The register-derivation is explicitly deferred in the module's own docstring ("once the resolution chain is wired", `style_assembly.py:206`). In production this surface is dead: every injected style resolves as written-register with no deltas. It is not a bug; it is an unbuilt half of a designed feature, and the documentation must not present it as working.

Second, the values floor is instructed, not enforced. The precedence line tells the model to hold values above style, but nothing measures whether it did. There is no instrumentation on this — no counter, no per-step budget. A guarantee stronger than "the prompt says so" does not exist yet.

### Implementation

The subsystem has a clean seam: a standalone, side-effect-free assembly core (`style_assembly.py`, `style_store.py`) that only reads files and returns strings, and the boot-layer wiring that decides *when* and *where* those strings get injected. This mirrors the Unix discipline of a pure function plus a caller that owns the side effects.

**Style assembly (the pure core).** `orchestrator/style_assembly.py` parses four read-only markdown source files under `frameworks/book/`:

- `style-registry.md` — 12 built-in genre profiles (explainer, technical, how-to, journalism, business, white-paper, academic, legal, marketing, reference, narrative, conversational), each a `## id` heading plus a `yaml` block (verified: `grep -c '^## '` = 12).
- `style-demeanor-axes.md` — 7 demeanor axes, each with 3 rungs low-to-high: warmth (cool/even/warm), force (gentle/measured/forceful), energy (calm/steady/lively), outlook (skeptical/balanced/affirming), playfulness (straight/dry/playful), directness (diplomatic/plain/blunt), agreeableness (accommodating/candid/challenging) — plus a device-overlays section (sarcasm, irony, hyperbole, understatement) toggled independently. The file carries 8 `## ` headings: the 7 axes plus the device-overlays block.
- `style-arrangement-schemas.md` — 12 ordered slot-list schemas (answer-first, motivation-first, inverted-pyramid, and so on).
- `style-craft-floor.md` — 5 craft-floor bullet lines that outrank style inside the full block.

The parsing uses PyYAML to read the registry's yaml blocks. The import is guarded: if PyYAML is absent, `load_registry` raises `RuntimeError` (`:57`) — and the callers swallow that (below), so a PyYAML-less environment silently *disables* Output Styles rather than crashing the pipeline. That is a deliberate degradation, and it is exactly the failure mode "missing dependency takes down the whole assistant" that the guard prevents.

`compose(style_id, register, gear, deltas, base, custom_entries)` (`:201`) is the entrypoint. It merges any custom profiles over the built-in registry, then branches on gear: `gear <= 2` returns `compose_demeanor_block` (disposition lines + the values-floor line + the completeness line, no arrangement); `gear >= 3` returns `compose_style_block` (demeanor + craft floor + arrangement schema + diction glossary + completeness). The gear-2 branch is where the conversational-register overlay *would* apply — it reads `entry["conversational"]` and merges the alternate demeanor per-axis — but since no caller sets a non-written register at runtime, the overlay is inert today.

**Style store (custom forks).** `orchestrator/style_store.py` persists user-forked profiles to `data/custom-styles.json` (STORE_PATH at style_store.py:25; the file is created on first custom-profile write and is absent on a fresh checkout). It mirrors the model-configuration persistence idiom deliberately: read-only framework files stay canonical, user edits live in the data dir. Writes are atomic — write to `STORE_PATH + ".tmp"`, then `os.replace` (`:95-98`) — so a crashed write never leaves a half-written store. Every reader degrades to an empty dict on a missing or corrupt file (`load_custom_profiles`, `:74`): a bad custom profile can never break the picker or the pipeline. A `PATCHABLE` whitelist (`:48`) means an endpoint can never inject an arbitrary key into a stored entry; a patch to `demeanor`/`devices`/`glossary`/`format` deep-merges key-by-key so one axis can be changed without resending the block.

**The two injection sites (the wiring).** `orchestrator/boot.py::_compose_output_style` (`:8432`) is the single funnel. It reads `style_id` off the context package, merges custom profiles, and calls `style_assembly.compose`. Critically, the *whole function is wrapped in a bare except that returns `""`* (`:8464`) — style is secondary to substance and must never break the pipeline, so any failure (missing file, malformed yaml, absent PyYAML) yields a no-op, not an exception.

It is called from exactly two places:

1. Gears 1-2, framed high (`:8539`): `if 0 < _osf_gear <= 2:` — the compact demeanor block goes near the top of the prompt.
2. Gears 3-4, after substance (`:8711`): `if _osf_gear >= 3 and step in ("analyst", "reviser", "consolidator", "formatter"):` — the full style block is appended after the step's substantive content. The step tuple is the mechanical exclusion of evaluator and verifier.

**Resolution chain.** `_resolve_effective_style_id(config)` (`:7088`) computes the turn's default style before any one-off, in precedence order: active project's `default_style_id` → project container-record `output_style` (read via `project_meta` for a Projects-feature project with no plugin manifest) → the `styles.default_id` account setting → legacy engine-config default → `None`. It is best-effort throughout — every lookup is in a try/except that falls through, so a project-lookup failure never breaks step-2 assembly.

**Config surfaces.** `orchestrator/user_settings.py` defines two account-wide controls (`:105-115`): `styles.default_id` (string; `""` = no style) and `styles.use_custom_values` (bool). Against the upstream inventory this is worth stating exactly: `default_id` ships defaulting to `"explainer"`, not empty — Output Styles are on out of the box, styled with the explainer genre — and `use_custom_values` ships `False`, so values injection is off by default. The settings docstring also states the register is determined by the pipeline path, not a stored toggle; that intent is consistent with the unwired-register open problem above, and the runtime hardcode is why the pipeline-path derivation does not yet happen.

**Per-turn overrides.** `/style <id>` and `/style off` are parsed in `parse_user_command` (`:14509`). `_is_known_style_id` (`:14485`) validates the id against the registry and custom store, and — matching the degradation philosophy — *accepts* (returns `True`) when the registry can't be read, so a one-off isn't silently eaten in a PyYAML-less environment. An unknown id leaves the input intact, so a `/style` typo passes through as ordinary text rather than being consumed.

**Honne/tatemae audience toggle.** `server/app.py::_apply_style_audience` (`:5970`) implements a separate axis: an input-toolbar toggle (`server/static/js/style-audience-toggle.js`) that marks a turn `internal`, folding the active project's `interaction_style` onto `style_id` for that turn (how Ora talks *to* you, versus the default output style). An explicit `/style` one-off already on the context wins over it. This swaps the *whole* style_id; it is **not** the written-vs-conversational register axis — two different knobs a reader could easily conflate.

**Personal values injection.** `load_boot_md` (`orchestrator/boot.py:1316-1324`) appends `mind.md` as an authoritative block only when `styles.use_custom_values` is on and the file exists. Before appending, `_filter_private_values` (`:95`) strips any `## Private Context` section unless the conversation is tagged `private` or `stealth` — so private life-context directives never reach an ordinary conversation. The heading constant `PRIVATE_VALUES_HEADING` (`:87`) is pinned to the projection module's whitelist by a unit test (`test_mind_projection.py:164-165`), so the two can't drift apart. One implementation seam worth flagging: `boot.py` derives its `WORKSPACE` from `os.path.expanduser("~/ora/")` (`:110`) and builds `MIND_MD` from it (`:112`), while `style_store.py` and `style_assembly.py` derive their `WORKSPACE` from `__file__` (`style_store.py:24`, `style_assembly.py:25`). The `__file__`-derived paths let a git worktree's tests write the worktree's own `data/`; the `expanduser` path in boot always points at the live install regardless of checkout.

**Three authoring paths for `mind.md`.**

- Guided wizard — `orchestrator/mind_guided.py` (595 lines). A deterministic, no-model question registry: `QUESTIONS` (`:51`) holds 12 questions across 5 sections (Communication Preferences, Intellectual Posture, Ethical Boundaries, Work Patterns, Standing Principles). `compose(answers, free_text)` (`:530`) builds a complete `mind.md`, splicing in fixed non-configurable lines from `_FIXED` (`:411`) — anti-sycophancy pushback calibration and source discipline — that the user cannot toggle off. The design constraint is explicit in the docstring: the wizard covers only the value dimensions that measurably change model output when injected; the MindSpec library's psychometric variables are prompt-inert and deliberately excluded. Backs `GET/POST /api/mind/guided`. 31 tests.
- Raw editor + create-from-template — `POST /api/mind` (`server.py:1237`), 128 KB cap (`_MIND_MAX_BYTES`, `:1200`), atomic tmp+`os.replace` write. Create-from-template refuses to overwrite a customized file (`:1251`).
- Interview projection — `orchestrator/mind_projection.py` (263 lines). A self-spec is a first-person portrait of how the user operates; the assistant needs second-person imperative directives. `project_self_spec` (`:178`) makes one model call (`model_dispatch.invoke_chat`, breadth slot) driven by `PROJECTION_SYSTEM_PROMPT` (`:73`), which transforms the portrait into whitelisted `## `-sections: What to Challenge Me On, How to Deliver Pushback, My Red Lines, Mission Alignment, Off Limits, Private Context (`ALLOWED_SECTIONS`, `:60`). `validate_directives` (`:159`) enforces the whitelist and 200–8000-char bounds (`MIN_CHARS`/`MAX_CHARS`, `:70-71`). `compose_projected_mind_md` (`:229`) assembles the final file as guided-base plus a projected block delimited by the projected marker. Backs `POST /api/mind/project`. 29 tests.

The projection's source resolution (`server.py:1383`) reads `mindspec/self-spec.md` when present, otherwise back-fills it from a raw-interview `mind.md` (one that exists, is customized, and carries neither guided nor projected marker) — archiving that content to `self-spec.md` first. On this checkout `self-spec.md` does **not exist** (status *planned*): the mindspec directory holds only `default-mindspec.md` (50 KB canonical library) and `mind-template.md`. The projection is fully coded and tested, but its live trigger requires first running `/framework mindspec-interview` to write the self-spec. The endpoint docstring claims the interview writes it "since 2026-07-01" (`server.py:1373`); that claim could not be verified from the files alone.

**Where `mind.md` sits relative to its neighbors.** `mind.md` is the user's *values*, injected conditionally and authoritatively over the default Mind Seeds in `boot/boot.md`. It is not the interview library (`mindspec/`) and not the boot system prompt. The guided wizard and the projection both write `mind.md`; the interview library is read-only reference material that feeds the projection.

**Normal operation vs. failure.** Normal path: a turn resolves a style_id, `_compose_output_style` renders the gear-appropriate block, it splices into the step prompt at the correct site; if values are enabled, `mind.md` (privacy-filtered) appends to boot content. Failure paths all degrade to empty: missing PyYAML → styles disabled, pipeline unaffected; corrupt `custom-styles.json` → empty custom store; malformed profile → `compose` raises, `_compose_output_style` returns `""`; unknown `/style` id → passes as text; missing `mind.md` or values-off → no values block. No single failure in this subsystem can break the analytical pipeline. That is the whole reliability posture, and it is qualitative — none of these degradations is instrumented with counters or success-rate metrics; the named failure mode (styles silently going dark) is caught by the guards, not measured.

### Evidence & Verification

The subsystem carries 123 unit tests across 12 files, all present and verified at this checkout:

| File | Tests | Surface |
|---|---|---|
| `test_style_assembly.py` | 9 | registry/axes/schema parse; gear branch; delta math |
| `test_style_store.py` | 8 | CRUD; atomic write; corrupt-file degradation; PATCHABLE whitelist |
| `test_style_injection.py` | 7 | the two boot injection sites + step gating |
| `test_style_resolution.py` | 7 | the effective-style-id precedence chain |
| `test_style_oneoff.py` | 6 | `/style <id>` and `/style off` parsing |
| `test_style_cards.py` | 4 | picker card labels |
| `test_style_trace.py` | 2 | trace capture |
| `test_style_audience.py` | 7 | honne/tatemae folding |
| `test_style_mindmd.py` | 3 | values-block injection + private filter |
| `test_mind_guided.py` | 31 | question registry, composer, marker parse, overwrite guard |
| `test_mind_projection.py` | 29 | prompt, whitelist validation, projected-marker preservation, private-heading pin |
| `test_mind_endpoints.py` | 10 | the six HTTP endpoints, provenance derivation |

To confirm the subsystem works, run the style and mind suites (`python3 -m unittest orchestrator.tests.test_style_assembly …`). To confirm the substance-first exclusion mechanically, read `boot.py:8711` — the step tuple is the proof that evaluator and verifier never receive a style block. To confirm degradation, corrupt `data/custom-styles.json` and confirm the picker still loads (empty store).

**Explicitly untested / unverified surfaces:**

- The situational-delta / register chain is unit-tested in isolation but has no runtime driver (`style_register` hardcoded `"written"`, `style_deltas` `None`). Its end-to-end behavior is dead in production. The 9 assembly tests exercise `resolve_demeanor` and the conversational overlay directly; nothing exercises them through a live turn, because nothing can.
- The `mind_projection` end-to-end path depends on a real `self-spec.md` that is absent on a fresh install. The 29 tests mock the model call; the live trigger (a completed MindSpec interview writing the self-spec) is not verifiable from this checkout, and the "since 2026-07-01" claim in the endpoint docstring is unconfirmed.
- All file I/O and path derivation are exercised only on macOS (M4 Max). See platform compatibility below.

### Expert Reading Notes

Read in this order. First `style_assembly.compose()` (`:201`) for the gear branch — this is the entire style-shape logic in one function. Then the two `boot.py` injection sites, `:8539` (gears 1-2, high) and `:8711` (gears 3-4, after substance, producing steps only) — this is where the substance-first ordering physically lives, and the step tuple at `:8711` is the load-bearing exclusion. Then `_resolve_effective_style_id` (`:7088`) for the precedence chain. For values, read `load_boot_md:1316-1324` and `_filter_private_values:95`.

Where future changes most likely cause drift:

- The register/delta chain. If someone wires `style_register` or `style_deltas` into the context package (`boot.py:7767-7768`), the currently-dead conversational overlay and delta math go live at once, and the demeanor a user sees at gears 1-2 will change. Any change there needs the assembly tests re-run against a live turn, not just in isolation.
- Two `WORKSPACE` derivations. `boot.py` uses `expanduser("~/ora/")` (`:110`) for `mind.md`; `style_store`/`style_assembly` use `__file__` (`:24`, `:25`). A refactor that "unifies" them could break either the worktree-isolation the `__file__` form gives tests, or the live-install targeting the `expanduser` form gives boot. Verify both before consolidating.
- The `PRIVATE_VALUES_HEADING` ↔ `ALLOWED_SECTIONS` pin. `boot.py:87` and `mind_projection.py:66` must agree on `"Private Context"`; the unit test at `test_mind_projection.py:164-165` enforces it. Rename one without the other and private directives could leak into ordinary conversations.

Known documentation drift, so you don't chase ghosts:

- `style_assembly.py`'s module docstring is stale. Lines 8-10 claim "Nothing in the pipeline imports it yet … [the wiring] sit[s] on top of this later." This is false at 7a5e8f40: `boot.py::_compose_output_style` imports and calls `compose()`. Trust the code; the wiring exists.
- `config/interface.json` does not hold the Output Styles tab. It is layout + theme only. The tab is a `settings-panel.js` / `styles-pane.js` construct. Do not repeat the interface.json premise.
- The `styles.default_id` default is `"explainer"`, not empty. Styles ship on.
- The full-word precedence line (`values floor > completeness floor > craft floor > substance/findings > style`) lives only in the module docstring (`:13`). The lines actually injected into prompts are condensed variants (`:164`, `:175`) and differ between the gear-2 and gear-3/4 blocks (`substance` vs `findings`). Quote the injected form, not the docstring form, if the exact string matters.

Treat the vault's [[Reference — Ora Output Styles Pane]] (2026-07-01) as canonical over both code comments and this chapter for the pane's UI; the `Fiction register` section it lists is a *deferred* placeholder, not built.

### Platform compatibility

All verification for this subsystem was performed on macOS (Apple M4 Max, macOS 26.3). Every non-macOS surface below is labeled from code inspection, not from an off-Mac run; where it was not actually executed elsewhere, it is marked untested.

- `mind.md` / `custom-styles.json` / `self-spec.md` path resolution (WORKSPACE derived from `__file__` in `style_assembly`/`style_store`; from `os.path.expanduser("~/ora/")` in `boot.py`; server paths via `os.path.join`). **macOS: works. Windows-native: untested. WSL: untested. Linux: untested.** All paths are built with `os.path.join` and are separator-agnostic, so they are likely portable, but only exercised on macOS. The two distinct WORKSPACE derivations (noted above) are a portability seam to check off-Mac.
- Atomic writes (tmp + `os.replace` in `style_store._save_all`, the mind endpoints, and `mind.md` writes). **macOS: works. Windows-native: untested. WSL: untested. Linux: untested.** `os.replace` is atomic within a filesystem on both POSIX and Windows, so behavior should hold, but it was not run off-Mac. No `fcntl` or symlink use in this subsystem.
- PyYAML dependency (`style_assembly.load_registry`). **macOS: works. Windows-native: untested. WSL: untested. Linux: untested.** Pure-Python, no native deps. The import is guarded (`:57` raises if absent); a missing-PyYAML environment silently *disables* Output Styles via the exception-swallowing in `_compose_output_style` and the accept-on-failure in `_is_known_style_id`, rather than crashing. Confirm PyYAML is present in any non-Mac deploy env, or the whole style layer goes dark without an error.
- `mind_projection` model call (`model_dispatch.invoke_chat`, breadth slot). **macOS: works. Windows-native: untested. WSL: untested. Linux: untested.** The projection transform is provider-agnostic (any breadth-slot model). Only the *local-model* backing inherits the MLX / Apple-Silicon constraint; a cloud breadth slot removes even that dependency. The Mac-specificity here is the local-inference layer, not the projection code.
- Server endpoints (Flask, localhost:5000 — the six `/api/mind*` and `/api/styles*` routes). **macOS: works. Windows-native: untested. WSL: untested. Linux: untested.** Standard Flask with a portable localhost binding; not specifically exercised off macOS for this subsystem.

# Part III — Knowledge, Tools & Media

## 8. Vault, RAG, Conversations & Persistent State

### Problem

A stateless model cannot supply continuity by itself. Ora must reconstruct the exact history a Dialogue branch may see, add explicit and discovered sources without violating privacy, fit complete evidence units inside the selected endpoint's real request limit, and deliver the same server-authoritative context to every processing path.

The hard cases are structural. A fork must see the ancestor prefixes that existed when each branch was made, never later ancestor turns. Explicit contributors must stay ordered, cutoff-safe, fairly represented, and visibly accounted for when missing, withheld, or deferred. Global retrieval must not rediscover the current Dialogue, its ancestry, a contributor, or a contributor's ancestry through another row. The stored retrieval document must preserve the full exchange even though the vector is intentionally oriented around context plus the user's prompt.

Lifecycle language must also match consequences. Standard and Private Close is reversible hiding, Exit Stealth is navigation, and Stealth Delete Forever is an Ora-managed purge that leaves surviving descendants detached. Explicit exports and copies already held by providers, Git, backups, or other external systems are outside that purge boundary.

### Design Decision & Tradeoffs

Four decisions carry the subsystem.

**The server owns effective history.** Each envelope stores local messages only. A true fork starts with `messages=[]`, local numbering at 1, the direct parent, and an immutable `fork_point_message_count` in that parent's local coordinates. Read-time recursion applies every ancestry cutoff before appending the child's local turns. Later ancestor turns cannot leak, the parent remains unchanged, and `fork_point_chunk_id` is legacy metadata only. Raw exchanges are authoritative; there is no accepted-decision store or inference pass.

**Context is packed as complete units against the endpoint, not a nominal character cap.** The Dialogue maximum is 200,000 tokens, but the safe request can be smaller after required payload, output allowance, retry headroom, images, provider constraints, and safety reserves. An unknown endpoint fails small. Recent local turns and the fork frontier lead, then promoted contributor units, older history, and global RAG. Initial 50/40/10 planning shares for history/contributors/global are soft; unused space returns to a common pool. No turn or indexed note chunk is truncated mid-unit.

**Explicit and global sources remain different lanes.** Contributors are ordered, identity-deduplicated, and uncapped. Dialogue contributors resolve recursively and cutoff-safely; atomic-note contributors use indexed hierarchical-content-provenance chunks. All references remain inventoried as available, missing, withheld, or deferred, and source-fair scheduling prevents one large contributor from crowding out the rest. Global RAG is lower priority and excludes the current Dialogue, all current ancestors, all contributors, and all contributor ancestors source-wide.

**Storage and semantic orientation are separate.** A conversation row's stored document is Context + User + Assistant. Its separately supplied vector input is bounded Context + User. The assistant is therefore returned with the retrieved evidence but does not steer the embedding. Live writes and historical replay target the same contract. Existing rows still require validated replay before this split is fully materialized across the historical collection; this chapter does not claim that production cutover has occurred.

Privacy gates every lane before ranking: Standard may use Standard sources, Private may use Standard + Private, and Stealth may use all three. An explicitly named archived Dialogue remains eligible as a read-only contributor when its entire required ancestry is permitted. Archived atomic-note contributors and archived rows from global retrieval are excluded; every contributed or globally retrieved Dialogue must pass the ancestry privacy test.

### Implementation

**Envelope and effective-history resolution.** `orchestrator/conversation_memory.py` owns `conversation.json`, local-turn append, ancestry fields, contributor references, closure state, and effective-history reconstruction. New forks persist no copied parent messages. Recursive reads enforce each direct parent's immutable message-count cutoff. On Stealth purge, direct children are detached; a legacy copied prefix is removed only when it exactly matches the deleted parent's complete current message list, otherwise ambiguous local content is retained.

**Contributor resolution and inventory.** Conversation references resolve by stable identity, never display title, and recursively reconstruct their permitted history. Atomic-note references resolve through indexed HCP chunks. Ordered deduplication occurs before fair per-source scheduling. The private inventory retains reference and unit identities plus missing/withheld/deferred status. Selected model-facing units include the provenance and source labels needed to use them; missing, withheld, and still-deferred candidate identities remain excluded from the model package, and the ordinary trace exposes no candidate inventory.

**Context assembly.** The server computes an endpoint-safe budget and packs complete units through the shared context path used by Phase A, Direct, G1–G4, and special consumers. Selection favors recent local context and fork frontier, then promoted contributor units, older history, and lower-priority global results. Dense and lexical retrieval share privacy and archive predicates. Global exclusions are source-wide, and relevance/ranking cannot resurrect an ineligible candidate.

**Conversation indexing.** `orchestrator/conversation_chunk.py` defines the exchange record used by live writes and historical replay. Chroma stores the full contextual header, user prompt, and assistant response as the document while accepting a separately bounded contextual-header-plus-user-prompt vector. Metadata remains denormalized and filterable; authoritative content remains in the envelope/raw exchange. `docs/conversation-file-format.py` is the field reference, not a claim that Pydantic validates runtime writes.

**Supplemental handling.** A supplemental request never runs a fresh query or appends a result beyond the budget. It promotes unseen, already-validated deferred contributor/global whole units and repacks the same call boundary. It stops on a repeated gap, no new eligible unit, no whole-unit fit, or resolution; there is no numeric request cap. The first three conditions yield a local `COVERAGE GAP`.

**Lifecycle and navigation.** Standard/Private Close sets `closed: true`; retained Dialogues disappear from the ordinary sidebar and remain restorable in Manage. The sidebar shows the current project's non-Stealth Dialogues plus only the active Stealth Dialogue. Exit Stealth selects the latest readable direct parent, even if that parent is Stealth, or creates a fresh Standard Dialogue; it does not close or delete. Delete Forever invokes protected Stealth purge even with descendants, which detach and retain only local turns. Ora-managed state is the purge boundary; deliberate exports and external/provider/Git/backup copies remain.

**Failure behavior.** An unresolved endpoint budget fails small. Ineligible privacy/ancestry/archive candidates fail closed. Retrieval/reranking failures may degrade the context lane without fabricating availability. Conversation writes remain atomic and process-local lock serialized; Stealth purge reports per-layer failure rather than claiming deletion it did not complete.

### Evidence & Verification

Verify this subsystem through behavior, not construction totals:

- A new fork has an empty local message list and local turn 1; effective history ends at every recorded ancestry cutoff even after ancestors receive later turns.
- Every consumer class receives the same server-authoritative history contract.
- Contributor order/deduplication, fair scheduling, full-ancestry privacy, archive exclusion, missing/withheld/deferred inventory, and source-wide global exclusions hold together.
- Packing respects the endpoint-safe budget, retains complete units, reuses unused 50/40/10 planning space, and fails small for an unknown endpoint.
- A retrieved conversation row returns Context + User + Assistant while its supplied vector is derived from bounded Context + User.
- Supplemental promotion changes selection only among already-validated deferred units and terminates without a numeric retry cap.
- Close/restore, Exit Stealth, protected purge, descendant detachment, and exact-only legacy prefix stripping produce their stated durable state.

Ordinary `context_coverage` output is deliberately numeric: `budget`, `lanes`, `source_counts`, and optional `physical_calls`, `deferred_unit_count`, and `deduplicated_unit_count`. It has no promotion or terminal-status field and reveals no unit/source identities. The sensitive supplemental forensic trace separately retains bounded gap, query-term, and why-it-matters text plus outcome fields; private mechanics retain candidate identities for resolution and diagnosis.

The historical collection is not evidence of migration completion. Before declaring the stored-document/vector split operationally materialized, replay existing rows through the live contract and validate identity, counts, content shape, privacy, exclusions, and query behavior against an isolated target.

### Expert Reading Notes

Read the boundaries in this order:

1. **Envelope before cache.** `conversation.json` local turns, ancestry cutoffs, contributors, privacy, and lifecycle are authoritative. Chunk files and index rows are rebuildable retrieval representations.
2. **Eligibility before relevance.** Privacy, complete ancestry, archive state, and source-wide exclusions determine whether a unit may compete. Similarity, lexical match, provenance, and reranking cannot restore an ineligible unit.
3. **Endpoint budget before allocation.** The 200,000-token maximum is narrowed by the actual endpoint contract. Planning shares are soft and units stay whole.
4. **Stored document before vector shorthand.** Assistant content is present in the stored document even though the vector orientation excludes it.
5. **Raw commitment before interpretation.** Never manufacture an accepted-decision memory. Attribute a decision only when the user actually committed in the authoritative exchange.

The highest drift risks are copied-parent forks, a nominal cap that ignores provider reserves, contributor-count limits, row-only instead of source-wide exclusion, privacy checks after retrieval, assistant-omitted stored documents, supplemental fresh queries, trace identity disclosure, and language that confuses Close or Exit Stealth with Delete Forever.

### Platform compatibility

The installed path is exercised on macOS/Apple Silicon. The context contract itself is provider-neutral, but these supporting surfaces retain platform dependencies:

- Local Ollama embeddings and a local reranker require their daemons and models; cloud endpoints require network access and provider credentials.
- `config/chromadb.json` binds embedder, vector dimension, and physical collection names. Every machine must keep those three consistent; dimension mismatch must fail visibly.
- ChromaDB state and the default session/conversation/vault paths are relocatable through runtime paths but remain unverified as a complete Windows-native, WSL, or Linux deployment.
- Atomic replacement is portable on the same filesystem, but the per-Dialogue lock is process-local; parallel Ora processes can still race and lose an update.
- Ora-managed Stealth purge cannot erase explicit exports or copies held by a model provider, Git remote, backup system, or other external service.

## 9. Tool Dispatch, the Capability Gate & Execution Telemetry

> **2026-08-20 G1.22 currency note.** The chapter's older composite pin remains useful for the surrounding Execution Review design, but this note supersedes its descriptions of command execution, native network reach, and MCP startup. `bash_execute` now accepts one command only, prepares one immutable argument vector, rejects shell grammar, resolves the executable against the same reduced environment used at execution, classifies Git/download/path effects semantically, and carries that prepared object through approval to `shell=False` execution. Public web and provider calls validate public destinations and redirect hops; credentialed provider requests are exact-origin and no-redirect, Jina refuses credential-bearing, signed, or sensitive-query URLs and records whether forwarding occurred, and Replicate output URLs are materialized instead of being retained as durable signed references. The vault-filesystem, Playwright, and GitHub MCP capabilities are all preserved: exact repository-local packages and Chromium are pinned, each child receives a fresh minimal environment, discovery exposes only the intersection of the child's catalog and the exact per-tool policy map, JSON-RPC requests are size-bounded and response-ID-correlated, and shutdown cleans up the children. Arbitrary Playwright code/evaluation tools are denied. The Playwright route hook is defense in depth rather than complete egress confinement: redirect hops are not delivered to the hook by the pinned browser, Chrome performs DNS independently after validation, and WebRTC and WebTransport are outside the hook.

### Problem

An orchestrator that lets a model choose what to do is only as safe as the boundary between its decisions and the world. Ora's pipeline reaches reality through many seams: it calls external models, reads RAG corpora, writes files, launches MCP subprocesses, invokes paid image and video APIs, runs user-installed shell hooks, and fetches the open web. Without a common enforcement-and-audit layer, six failure modes are unguarded, and each is independently sufficient to cause harm.

First, **auto-approve carrying a dangerous action.** The dispatcher has a permission mode (approve-each, auto-approve). If enforcement lives inside the permission mode, then flipping to auto-approve — a convenience toggle — silently authorizes irreversible deletes, secret reads, and unknown actions. A permission mode is a UX affordance; it must not be the security boundary.

Second, **unaudited execution.** A tool that runs and leaves no record cannot be reviewed after the fact. When a session does something surprising, "what did it actually touch, in what order, with what result" must be reconstructable from a durable log, not from the model's own narration of what it believes it did. Model narration is not evidence; it is a claim by the same process under review.

Third, **secret leakage into logs.** The instrument built to prevent the first two problems becomes the third if it records a retrieved API key, a password in a command argument, or a token embedded in a git URL. An audit log that captures secrets is a secret store with worse access control.

Fourth, **silent failures dissolving into empty strings.** A RAG read that returns nothing, a Step-1 parse that fails, a capability slot every provider refuses — each can degrade to `""` and flow downstream as if the stage simply had nothing to say. The user sees a thinner answer and never learns a subsystem broke.

Fifth, **non-reproducible tool selection during evaluation.** Comparative evaluation runs the same prompt through different model configurations. If tool selection is itself a model decision, the tools fired vary per run and per model, and the comparison is contaminated — you are no longer measuring the model, you are measuring the model plus its improvised tool use.

Sixth, **ungoverned web reach.** Reaching the open web from inside an analysis raises three distinct risks: leaking the prompt to a search provider, double-charging paid fetch/image APIs on retries, and injecting unattributed web text into a grounded answer with no provenance.

This chapter's subsystem — the dispatcher seam, the capability gate, the tool-event recorder, the capability-slot registry, the MCP client, the three web-reach modules, and the per-turn trace/health surfaces — exists to close all six. Its core (`orchestrator/tool_events.py`) is Phase 1 of the Execution Review architecture: the always-on instrumentation-and-enforcement substrate.

### Design Decision & Tradeoffs

**One always-on module for manifest, recorder, and gate.** The capability manifest (the risk vocabulary), the tool-event recorder (the audit write), and the pre-execution gate (the enforcement decision) all live in `tool_events.py`, deliberately in one module because they share one lifecycle (`tool_events.py:1-34`). The alternative — three modules, wired independently — was rejected because the three must never drift apart: the gate decides using the same axes the recorder stamps, and a recorder that logged a different classification than the gate enforced would make the audit trail lie about what was enforced. Colocation is the mechanism that keeps the enforced classification and the recorded classification identical by construction.

**Three risk axes layered on the dispatcher's function axis, not replacing it.** The dispatcher already classifies every tool as read/write/execute — the *function* axis. This subsystem adds three orthogonal axes on top and leaves the function axis untouched (`tool_events.py:8-15`): *mutability* (`read` → `reversible_write` → `external_write` → `irreversible`), *sensitivity* (`public` → `private` → `sensitive` → `secret`), and *egress* (`none` → `local` → `external`). The reason for orthogonality: "can I undo it," "how secret is what it touches," and "does data leave the machine" are independent questions, and collapsing them into one severity scalar loses the distinction that lets the gate block an irreversible-but-public action and an innocuous-but-secret action for different, legible reasons. The rejected alternative — a single risk score — cannot express "reversible write to a secret file," which must be gated on sensitivity while a reversible write to a public file passes.

**The gate runs before execution and independently of the permission mode.** This is the release-blocking decision. `gate()` is called at the dispatcher seam *before* the tool runs, and it does not consult the dispatcher's approve-each/auto-approve setting at all (`tool_events.py:836-850`). Auto-approve can never carry an irreversible, unknown, or secret action, because the gate that blocks those never sees the permission mode. The permission mode governs the *routine* read/reversible tools; the gate governs the *dangerous* ones, and the two are separate code paths on purpose.

**Fail closed on the unknown.** An action absent from the manifest is classified `irreversible` + `secret` + `external` + `unknown: True` and gated (`tool_events.py:99-106`, `manifest_axes` at 194-199). The tradeoff is explicit and accepted: a newly added tool whose axes nobody declared will be blocked until someone classifies it. That is the safe failure — a false block costs a human approval; a false allow costs whatever the unclassified action did. This is the Unix `umask`-style default: deny first, grant deliberately.

**Deterministic tool selection, with a default-off model-driven escape hatch.** Tool selection splits into two lanes. The deterministic lane (`tool_selector.py`) derives parameters mechanically from the prompt — a mode declares tool needs in a `## TOOLS` section, and `web_fetch` fires on exactly the URLs present in the prompt (`tool_selector.py:70-71`, docstring at 26). This is reproducible: the same prompt fires the same tools regardless of which model runs, which is what makes it safe during comparative evaluation. The model-driven escape hatch (a model requesting read-category tools mid-analysis) exists but is gated behind `ORA_MODEL_TOOL_SELECTION`, which defaults to `"0"` (`boot.py:8376`). It is built and wired, not active. That makes it **partial**, not implemented — a flag that defaults off is a capability the installed system does not exercise.

**Pattern B web evidence over Pattern A tool-use round-trips.** All three web-reach modules inject retrieved evidence into the system prompt as a context block rather than exposing web search as a tool the model calls in a round-trip (`claim_verification.py:14-22`). Pattern B was chosen over Pattern A for provider-agnosticism (a local model with no tool-use protocol still gets the evidence), traceability (every injected chunk carries a provenance tier), and cost control (the fetch happens once, deterministically, not once per model retry). The tradeoff: the model cannot adaptively decide it needs one more search mid-generation. For Ora's grounded-answer goal, deterministic-evidence-then-generate beats adaptive-search-during-generate, because the former is auditable and the latter is not.

**Capability slots generalize the hard-coded vision-extraction routing.** Rather than hard-code each image/video provider into the dispatcher, `capability_registry.py` lifts the existing `vision_extraction` routing pattern into a named-slot registry: `capabilities.json` declares N slots with input/output contracts, and `routing-config.json` declares a preferred→fallback provider chain per slot (`capability_registry.py:13-21`). `invoke()` contract-validates inputs before any provider runs and walks the fallback chain on fallback-triggering error codes. The alternative — provider conditionals scattered through the dispatcher — was rejected because it made adding a provider a dispatcher edit and gave no uniform place to record the invocation or express a fallback chain.

**The tradeoff of the whole design:** fail-closed enforcement plus a single instrumentation seam adds friction and concentrates correctness risk. Every blocked action needs a human approval. Every action pays a record cost. And the entire enforcement boundary lives in one module — a bug in `gate()` is a bug in the security boundary. I accept this concentration deliberately: one auditable seam I can test exhaustively beats enforcement scattered across every call site, where coverage is impossible to reason about.

**Open problems.** Four are real and unresolved. First, the action manifest, the exact MCP per-tool policy maps, and the protected-path lists must still be kept in sync with new actions and protection-worthy files; an unrecognized MCP tool is not exposed and an unclassified action fails closed, but a newly protection-worthy file omitted from `_PROTECTED_FILES` remains ungated. Second, the secret-path patterns are POSIX-dotfile-shaped and will under-classify Windows credential conventions (`%APPDATA%` stores) at the gate — a correctness gap the moment anyone ports off Mac. Third, the evidence-runner that would let the gate verify a check declaration before allowing it is declared vocabulary only — the runner itself is Phase 5 and not built. Fourth, the Playwright route hook cannot see redirect hops, cannot bind Chrome's subsequent DNS resolution to Python's validation result, and does not cover WebRTC or WebTransport; complete browser egress confinement would require a separately approved proxy, firewall/network sandbox, or maintained browser interception fork.

### Implementation

**The dispatcher seam.** `dispatcher.py` is the single wiring point where axes are derived and the gate and recorder fire. For an MCP call, `tool_events.mcp_policy` resolves the exact namespaced tool plus its actual parameters into call-specific axes and authority scopes; an absent or malformed policy fails closed rather than borrowing a server-wide default. File targets still escalate through `resolve_path_sensitivity`, and protected configuration still escalates mutability. For `bash_execute`, the seam prepares the command once and binds that exact prepared object — argument vector, working directory, reduced-environment digest, executable identity, semantic selectors, and read/write scopes — to both the gate and the later execution. It calls `tool_events.gate(...)` before any effect and records the real result after execution. The credential-store channel remains the one sanctioned secret path: a matching standing allow can bypass the per-call prompt while still being logged existence-only. The gate runs *here*, before the tool, so no downstream permission mode can reach around it.

**The gate decision.** `gate()` (`tool_events.py:836-933`) computes a `block_why`: unknown → blocked; `irreversible` → blocked; `secret` → blocked; and, for model-facing surfaces only, `sensitive` → blocked (851-863). If nothing blocks, it returns `allowed` immediately. If something blocks, it tries three escalations in order: consume a matching one-shot approval token if one was previously granted (884-889); ask a live human via an `interactive_approver` callback when one is wired — the terminal approve-each prompt or a browser callback (893-912); otherwise deny immediately and write a Paused queue entry (916-933). The third path is a *record, never a wait* — the gate does not block the thread waiting for approval; it denies now and leaves an entry the user can approve later, at which point a re-issued matching call passes once. A per-process dedup set (`_queued_hashes`, 788-803) ensures an agentic retry loop enqueues one Paused card, not one per retry.

**Approval tokens.** A grant mints a one-shot token keyed by `(action, args_hash)` with a default 3600-second TTL (`tool_events.py:574`, `grant_approval` 610-634). Consumption marks it used and stamps `used_at` (`check_and_consume_approval` 738-771). Conversation-scoped tokens require an exact conversation match — a context-less call cannot consume a token minted for a specific conversation (751-757). The read-modify-write is wrapped in a cross-platform exclusive lock (`_with_approvals_lock` → `runtime_paths.locked_file`) plus atomic `os.replace`; the lock is retained rather than replaced by atomic-write alone because grant and consume both mutate the same file and atomic replace does not prevent a lost update between concurrent grant/consume (601-607, `_save_approvals` 593-598). The standing-allow variant (637-657) is the credential-store exception: revocable, scoped per service, logged existence-only.

**Risk tier (Execution Review Phase 2).** The per-turn risk tier rides in the turn context (`set_turn_context(..., risk_tier=...)`, `tool_events.py:375-390`) so the gate and every recorded event see it without a dispatcher signature change. Because the tier is known only after mode dispatch — which runs after the context is seeded at step 2 — `update_turn_risk_tier` (393-405) attaches it in place. `record()` stamps it onto every event (528-532), and `consume_token_by_fingerprint` (681-712) implements the Phase-2 task gate, consuming a token by `(action, args_hash)` alone (the fingerprint itself binds the conversation, so the mint and the resume may legitimately differ). This is verified by `test_risk_gate.py` (93 tests) and `test_risk_gate_pipeline.py` (6).

**Turn context propagation.** The context (`trace_dir`, `conversation_id`, `stealth`, `surface`, `risk_tier`) is stored in a `ContextVar` (`tool_events.py:371`). The pipeline seeds it at step 2 and the server at the `_direct_stream` head. It propagates to Gear-4 worker threads via `contextvars.copy_context` (through `boot._submit_with_context`, `boot.py:44`) and to child project-tool processes via `ORA_*` environment variables read back by `get_turn_context` when no context var is set (408-416). This is why a tool-event fired deep inside a parallel Gear-4 stream still knows which conversation it belongs to.

**Two redaction layers.** Sensitivity-based redaction (`_redact_for_record`, `tool_events.py:463-490`): a `secret` event is reduced to existence-only (`content_redacted: "secret-existence-only"`, args and reads dropped); a `sensitive` event keeps only length descriptors. Content-based scrub (`scrub_content`, 359-366) runs regardless of sensitivity, matching OpenAI/Anthropic-style keys, GitHub PATs, AWS access-key ids, Slack tokens, bearer headers, userinfo credentials in URLs, and `password=`/`token=` assignments (`_SCRUB_PATTERNS` 348-356). The path classifier (`resolve_path_sensitivity`, 324-340) matches boundary-anchored patterns — `\.env($|\.)` catches `.env` and `prod.env` but not `.venv`; patterns are anchored so `tokenizer.json` does not match "token" (289-305). Paths under known private roots (workspace, vault, conversations, `~/Documents`, `~/sites`, scratch) classify `private`; unrecognized territory classifies `sensitive` — conservative by default (317-340).

**Telemetry health.** `record()` never raises (`tool_events.py:508-569`): on failure it calls `_note_failure`, which increments a counter, records the last error, prints to stderr, and best-effort emits a `ToolEventRecordingFailure` on the oversight bus (426-439). `get_telemetry_health()` (442-446) reports `incomplete: True` once any failure has occurred, and every subsequent event is stamped `telemetry_incomplete: True` (544-546). The failure mode this prevents: a silently-dropped audit line that makes the log look complete when it is not. When telemetry breaks, the log says so, on every line thereafter. `ORA_TOOL_EVENTS=off` disables the *write* only (516) — the gate still runs and still fails closed. The dual sink routes to the active turn trace dir when a turn is live, else `data/tool-events.jsonl` (`_sink_path` 502-505). Writes use `os.open(..., O_WRONLY|O_APPEND|O_CREAT, 0o644)` with UTF-8-encoded lines capped at `MAX_LINE_BYTES` (8000), truncating to a keep-set of structural fields when over (549-567).

**Capability-slot registry.** `load_registry` (`capability_registry.py:744`) reads `capabilities.json` and `routing-config.json`'s `slots` block (runtime-overlay-aware), merges project-declared slots (`merge_project_capability_slots` 674), resolves slot inheritance (`resolve_slot_inheritance` 565), and auto-registers `local_diffusers` best-effort (879-880). Six integration modules bind real handlers via `register()` — `gemini_images`, `openai_images`, `openrouter_images`, `stability`, `replicate`, and `local_diffusers`. `invoke()` (361-524) validates required inputs before any handler runs, fills optional defaults, builds the provider chain (preferred→fallback, or a length-1 chain when an explicit `provider_id` disables fallback), and walks it: on a `CapabilityError` whose code is in the slot's fallback-triggering set and with providers remaining, it advances; otherwise it breaks and re-raises the last error with the full `attempts` chain attached, so the caller can render "Slot 1 refused for content_policy → Slot 2 unavailable → no providers left." Every invocation records one `capability_slot` tool-event with the provider tried, the attempts list, and the exit reason (`_record_tool_event` 527-558). `config/capabilities.json` defines 14 slots; the image and video families are wired via integrations, while project-specific slots (e.g. `column_authoring_paid`, `image_generates_barb_cartoon`) have their provider wiring outside `~/ora` in the project's `ora-project/`, so their runtime provider status is per-install.

**MCP client.** `mcp_client.py` accepts only the three pinned stdio declarations in `config/mcp-servers.json`: vault filesystem (`@modelcontextprotocol/server-filesystem` 2026.7.10), Playwright (`@playwright/mcp` 0.0.79), and GitHub (`@modelcontextprotocol/server-github` 2025.4.8). Startup resolves their exact Node entrypoints under `mcp-runtime/node_modules`; it never invokes `npm`, `npx`, or a runtime package download. The installer provisions the locked Playwright Chromium through the repository-local Playwright CLI. Each child starts in its designated working directory with a fresh allowlisted environment; the GitHub token is inherited only by the GitHub child, and a missing token skips that child without removing the other two capabilities. Discovery namespaces tools as `mcp_<server>_<tool>` and exposes only tools present in both the child catalog and the exact policy map. Requests are serialized and bounded, replies must carry the exact outstanding JSON-RPC ID and shape, discovery/schema/catalog and stderr retention have explicit limits, tool errors remain failures, and shutdown closes streams, terminates or kills stragglers within bounded waits, joins reader threads, and clears the catalog. A server that cannot start fails soft and does not block Ora startup. HTTP transport is unsupported; pinned stdio is the only accepted transport.

**Web reach at steps 2/5/8.** `web_consultation.py` (F-Consult) runs at Step 2: parallel intent searches, source tiering (whitelisted/corroborated/single/excluded), per-chunk provenance, optional prompt-sanity and conflict passes; dependency-injected, enabled by default via `routing-config` (`web_consultation.enabled: true`; wired at `boot.py:7459`). `web_extraction.py` escalates when high-trust snippets are too thin: it deep-fetches full pages, chunks, fit-gates, and folds a `### DEEP EXTRACTIONS` block into web context — fail-*closed* when nothing survives the fit gate (`web_extraction.py:382-388`).
