It is gated on `ORA_WEB_EXTRACTION` — code default off (`boot.py:7042`, `_env_flag`) but force-exported `=1` by the tracked launcher `start.sh:26`, so it is **active on a normally-launched installed system**. `claim_verification.py` runs a pre-flight for Step 5 (Reviser) and Step 8 (Verifier): it parses the evaluator's `## FLAGGED CLAIMS`, runs challenge queries in parallel via `web_search_structured` with a DuckDuckGo cascade, and injects `## FLAGGED CLAIM EVIDENCE` into the reviser's and verifier's prompts — Pattern B (`claim_verification.py:1-22`, wired at `boot.py:11278/12008/12012`). The shared `network_policy` now normalizes destinations, refuses credentials in URLs and non-public address resolutions, and requires explicit validation of each allowed redirect hop. Authenticated OpenRouter and Replicate calls are bound to their exact provider origin and do not forward credentials across redirects; public provider-returned assets are fetched without the provider bearer. Jina remains available for useful public pages, but refuses credential-bearing, signed, or sensitive-query source URLs and emits durable forwarding/refusal metadata. Replicate's asynchronous output walker materializes URL values and URL-shaped dictionary keys into local artifacts before job state is retained.

**Forensic trace and health banner.** `pipeline_trace.py` writes per-turn step JSON/MD, RAG-failure logs, and a step-health summary under `data/pipeline-traces/<conv>/<ts>/`, gated by `ORA_PIPELINE_TRACE` (default on, `pipeline_trace.py:58-66`). `_resolve_ora_home` derives the root from `__file__` rather than a literal `~`, avoiding the observed bug where an unset `HOME` produced a literal-tilde directory (35-56). Stealth conversations create no trace directory at all (`start_trace`, 75-101). `pipeline_health.py` is the silent-failure surface: any pipeline step calls `record(category, message)` into thread-local storage (`_tls`, 32-47), and `collect_and_clear` drains it at turn end to a chat banner (50-63). This is what turns "empty RAG dissolved into an empty string" into a warning the user actually sees.

**Lifecycle hooks.** `hooks.py::fire_hooks` runs `config/hooks/*.json` shell scripts at pre_tool/post_tool/session_*/pre_compact events via `subprocess.run(command, shell=True, ...)` (70-71) and records each execution as a tool-event with `enforcement: boundary_only` (85-116). Hooks are honest about their axes because they are `shell=True` subprocesses Ora cannot see inside; user-installed hooks are pre-authorized, and installing a hook JSON is itself a protected-config write (`config/hooks` is in `_PROTECTED_PREFIXES`, `tool_events.py:256`), which closes the "one allowed write buys arbitrary execution" escalation.

**URL import.** `url_import.py` wraps `yt-dlp` to download public media into a per-conversation library. `YTDLP_BINARY = shutil.which("yt-dlp") or "/opt/homebrew/bin/yt-dlp"` (48) — `which` finds a binary on any platform, but the fallback and the not-found guidance (`brew install yt-dlp`, 149) are Homebrew-specific and Mac-shaped.

**Config surfaces.** `config/capabilities.json` (slot contracts; protected), `config/mcp-servers.json` (the three pinned stdio declarations plus exact per-tool policies; protected), `mcp-runtime/package-lock.json` (exact MCP dependency graph), and `config/routing-config.json` (`web_consultation.enabled`, slot chains; protected). Env flags: `ORA_TOOL_EVENTS`, `ORA_PIPELINE_TRACE`, `ORA_MODEL_TOOL_SELECTION`, `ORA_WEB_EXTRACTION*`, `ORA_RISK_TIER`.

### Evidence & Verification

The 2026-08-20 G1.22 pre-channel acceptance used focused behavioral checks rather than repinning the whole chapter. The command/native-network slice passed 126 tests with 3 skips plus 186 parameterized subtests, and its execution-provenance suite passed 39 tests. The final MCP correction passed 91 tests plus 64 parameterized subtests; the wider preceding MCP run passed 251 tests plus 41 parameterized subtests. An independent MCP review returned accepted after checking that all three capabilities remained available, policy was per tool and call, arbitrary Playwright code was denied, errors stayed failures, and no child survived cleanup. These counts establish the changed boundary only; they do not close the channel work or the separate P1 resource/concurrency hardening still tracked under G1.22.

The subsystem carries roughly 400 unit tests across the target files, confirmed by `grep -c 'def test'` at 7a5e8f40: `test_tool_events` (52), `test_dispatcher_gate` (44), `test_capability_registry` (34) + `test_capability_registry_fallback` (25), `test_web_consultation` (44), `test_web_extraction` (21), `test_claim_verification` (35), `test_tool_selector` (22), `test_mcp_catalog` (15), `test_url_import` (13), `test_risk_gate` (93), `test_risk_gate_pipeline` (6) — 404 in total. (The inventory listed a `test_capability_registry_routes_rerun` file and a smaller risk-gate count; at this commit that file is absent and the risk-gate suite is larger — trust the tree.)

To confirm the gate fails closed: run `test_dispatcher_gate` and `test_risk_gate`. The gate's block logic (`tool_events.py:851-863`) and its independence from the permission mode are the properties under test; the `FAIL_CLOSED` default (99-106) is exercised by feeding an unclassified action and asserting a block.

To confirm telemetry flags its own incompleteness: force a `record()` failure and read `get_telemetry_health()` — `incomplete` flips to `True` and every subsequent event carries `telemetry_incomplete: True` (544-546). This is the check that distinguishes a complete log from a log that merely looks complete.

To confirm slot fallback records an attempts chain: `test_capability_registry_fallback` drives a Slot-1 refusal into a Slot-2 attempt and asserts the re-raised error's `.attempts` captures the full chain (`capability_registry.py:361-524`).

**Explicitly untested surfaces.** There is no dedicated test file for `pipeline_trace.py`, `pipeline_health.py`, or `hooks.py` at this commit (confirmed by directory listing) — the trace atomic-write, the stealth-skip path, and the hook telemetry-recording path are exercised only indirectly through pipeline tests, not in isolation. `ORA_MODEL_TOOL_SELECTION` is default-off and not exported by any launcher, so the installed system does not exercise it; `ORA_WEB_EXTRACTION` is code-default-off but force-exported `=1` by `start.sh:26`, so a normally-launched installed system does run web extraction. The evidence-runner has a validated check vocabulary (`validate_check_declaration`, `EVIDENCE_RUNNER_DEFAULTS`, `tool_events.py:983-1005`) but no runner — an undeclared check is un-runnable by construction, and the runner is Phase 5 (980-981). No reliability budget is instrumented anywhere in this subsystem: there is no per-step success-rate metric, no gate-block-rate counter beyond the raw event log, and no measured coverage percentage. The failure modes above are prevented qualitatively — the gate blocks the class, the recorder logs the class — and the only quantitative instrument present is the telemetry-failure counter, which counts recorder failures, not action success rates.

**Post-pin trace verification.** The dedicated tests absent at `7a5e8f40` now exist in `orchestrator/tests/test_trace_manifest.py` and `test_trace_walk_ui.py`. The PR #269 acceptance run exercised both import orders and the full suite with exact baseline failure/error signature parity. Its behavioral cases invoke real single-analyst, external-consolidation, fallback/retry, server/CLI direct-entry, lifecycle/disconnect, flagged-claim evidence, supplemental-RAG, web-consultation, terminal-routing, private-projection/export, and writer-failure paths. This statement is limited to the trace-completeness landing; it does not revise the other untested surfaces named in this chapter.

A note on Execution Review status, because it is easy to overstate. Phases 1–4 are landed in `~/ora` main at 7a5e8f40 (the commit is titled "Execution Review Phase 1-4: macOS+Windows portability retrofit"). Phase 5 — the evidence-runner — is not. It sits in an unmerged worktree (`ora-worktrees/phase5-impl` on branch `execution-review-phase5-impl`) at the code-review gate; it is not merged to `~/ora` main, not installed, not landed. The installed system's Execution-Review coverage ends at Phase 4. The evidence-runner vocabulary you see in `tool_events.py` is the Phase-4 declaration of what Phase 5 will validate — declared now precisely so an undeclared check cannot later slip past a runner that does not yet exist.

### Expert Reading Notes

Read these five things first, in this order. First, `tool_events.gate()` and the `FAIL_CLOSED` default — this is the release-blocking security boundary, and everything else is scaffolding around it. Second, `_redact_for_record`, `_SCRUB_PATTERNS`, and `resolve_path_sensitivity` — the leak-prevention layer that keeps the audit log from becoming a secret store. Third, the dispatcher seam — the single place policy is resolved and the gate and recorder fire; its `PreparedCommand` and `mcp_policy` paths are the current command/MCP authority boundary. Fourth, `capability_registry.invoke`, `resolve_provider_chain`, and `load_registry`'s routing-config overlay resolution — the slot dispatch and fallback machinery. Fifth, `mcp_client`'s pinned-registry validation, fresh-environment construction, exact-ID request handling, discovery intersection, and cleanup — together these are the load-bearing MCP boundary; the reader thread is only one part of it.

**Where future changes most likely cause drift.** First, the `capability_registry.py` module docstring (lines 30-33) still declares "Stub-only in WP-7.0.1 ... ships with the registry, the dispatcher, and an empty provider table." That is stale: six integration modules now register real handlers. Trust the code — the provider table is populated at load. Second, `CLAUDE.md` gives this subsystem essentially no coverage: the security/telemetry substrate (the gate, the recorder, the dispatcher wiring, the MCP client, the web modules, the trace/health surfaces, hooks) is absent from the architecture map, which mentions only `capability_slots` and only in the plugin-convention and Visual-tab sections. The doc-code gap here is a whole-subsystem omission, not a line-level lag — do not read `CLAUDE.md`'s silence as absence of the subsystem. Third, the `ACTION_MANIFEST`, the exact MCP tool maps in `mcp-servers.json`, the package lock, and the `_PROTECTED_*` path lists must be kept in sync with new actions, MCP package versions, tools, and protection-worthy files; a discovered MCP tool absent from policy is not exposed and a new unclassified action fails closed, but a newly protection-worthy file left out of `_PROTECTED_FILES` remains ungated. Fourth, the secret-path patterns are POSIX-dotfile-shaped and will under-cover a Windows port. Fifth, the two default-off flags are partial-by-policy, not unbuilt — do not delete them as dead code; they are wired and tested, waiting on a deliberate operator enable.

For the framework contracts these modules implement, see [[Reference — Capability Invocation Contracts]] (slot input/output contracts), [[Reference — AI Agent Security Architecture]] (the gate's threat model), and the F-stage specs [[Specification — F-Consult]], [[Specification — F-Revise]], and [[Specification — F-Verify]] for the web-reach injection points at Step 2 (consultation), Step 5 (claim-verification reviser pre-flight), and Step 8 (claim-verification verifier pre-flight).

### Platform compatibility

The commit at 7a5e8f40 is titled a "macOS+Windows portability retrofit," and the code reflects genuine portability *intent* — but intent is not evidence. No test at this commit simulates Windows or Linux path semantics — no test exercises `sys.platform`, `normcase`, or backslash-separated paths against the classifiers — so every non-macOS surface below is labeled **untested**, never "works." macOS Apple Silicon is the sole verified target.

- **Path handling — `_matchable` / `_cmp_key` / protected-path and secret-path matching** (`tool_events.py:61-77`, 254-282): macOS — works. Windows-native / WSL / Linux — untested. The code forward-slashes and normcase-folds keys explicitly so that `/`-anchored patterns and `startswith` prefix checks hold on backslash-separated, case-insensitive Windows paths; this is written for portability but has no off-Mac test evidence.
- **Secret/sensitive path patterns** (`tool_events.py:289-313`): macOS / WSL / Linux — works (POSIX dotfile conventions: `.ssh`, `.aws`, `.env`, `id_rsa`, `.pem`). Windows-native — untested and known to under-cover: Windows credential conventions (`%APPDATA%` credential stores, DPAPI blobs) are not in the pattern set, so a Windows port would under-classify sensitive paths at the gate. This is a correctness gap, not just an untested one.
- **Tool-event sink write — `os.open(..., O_WRONLY|O_APPEND|O_CREAT, 0o644)`** (`tool_events.py:563`): macOS / WSL / Linux — works. Windows-native — untested; `os.open` ignores most mode bits on Windows but the append write still functions.
- **Approvals file locking — `runtime_paths.locked_file` + atomic `os.replace`** (`tool_events.py:601-607`): macOS — works. Windows-native / WSL / Linux — untested. Portability depends on the `runtime_paths.locked_file` cross-platform lock abstraction, which is out of scope for this chapter and unverified off-Mac.
- **MCP stdio reader threads + binary UTF-8 protocol bounds** (`mcp_client.py`): macOS — works. Windows-native / WSL / Linux — untested. The pump avoids `select.select` on Windows pipes, while byte limits are enforced before decoding; this is design-intent for Windows correctness, not verified behavior.
- **Pinned repository-local MCP packages and Chromium** (`mcp-runtime/package-lock.json`, `config/mcp-servers.json`; vault-fs/playwright/github): macOS — works. Windows-native / WSL / Linux — untested. Startup uses exact local Node entrypoints rather than `npx`; installation requires a compatible Node runtime and provisions the locked Chromium, but neither install nor launch has been exercised off-Mac. Individual servers fail soft when their pinned local runtime or required credential is unavailable.
- **`local_diffusers` capability provider (MLX/Metal)** (auto-registered in `capability_registry.load_registry`): macOS Apple Silicon — works. Windows-native / WSL / Linux — n/a: MLX/Metal is Apple-Silicon-only; on other platforms the import fails and the registry silently falls through to API providers.
- **`web_fetch` browser tier (local Playwright/Chrome) used by web_consultation/web_extraction**: macOS — works. Windows-native / WSL / Linux — untested; depends on a browser install and was not exercised off-Mac.
- **Pipeline-trace / tool-event sink directory under `ORA_HOME`** (`pipeline_trace.py:35-56`): macOS / WSL / Linux — works; `_resolve_ora_home` derives from `__file__` to avoid a literal-`~` directory when `HOME` is unset and refuses `~` paths. Windows-native — untested but filesystem-agnostic in principle.
- **`yt-dlp` URL import binary resolution** (`url_import.py:48`, 149): macOS — works. Windows-native — needs porting: the fallback path `/opt/homebrew/bin/yt-dlp` and the `brew install yt-dlp` guidance are Homebrew-specific. WSL / Linux — untested: `shutil.which` would find a system-installed binary, but the fallback and error message are Mac-shaped.

## 10. Visual Intelligence

### Problem

Analytical model output is prose. That is the right default for most reasoning, but for a class of problems — systems dynamics, decision structure, causal attribution, argument mapping — the honest and legible form is a diagram, not a paragraph. A causal loop with three reinforcing and two balancing links is a five-line drawing and an unreadable page of English. So the pipeline has to turn reasoning into diagrams. Three failure modes stand between "the model reasoned well" and "the user saw a correct, readable, accessible diagram," and each one, left unaddressed, silently degrades the product.

First, **producer unreliability.** The system's contract for a renderable diagram is a strict, typed JSON envelope (`ora-visual`). Analytical models are good at *drawing* — they emit well-formed Mermaid, DAGitty `dag { … }`, and Structurizr `workspace { … }` fluently — and bad at emitting a schema-valid envelope. The `visual_recovery.py` module docstring states the measured motivation plainly: model-emitted envelopes validate "at ~0%" while the Mermaid/DSL the same model draws is well-formed (`orchestrator/visual_recovery.py:7-14`). If the envelope is the only accepted input, the diagram almost never appears, or appears as raw JSON in the transcript. A concrete instance of the latter is documented in the V3 dispatch module: after V3 retired the classic fence-extraction path without a replacement, envelopes "rendered as raw JSON code blocks in the transcript" (`server/static/js/v3-visual-dispatch.js:5-9`).

Second, **dishonest charts.** An LLM asked to draw a chart defaults to the dishonest form: truncated y-axes that exaggerate a trend, chartjunk, low-contrast palettes, unlabeled series. These are not rendering bugs; they are the model's learned priors. A chart that renders cleanly and lies is worse than no chart, because it launders a distortion through an authoritative-looking artifact.

Third, **inaccessibility.** A rendered diagram is, by default, a pixel blob to a screen-reader user and a dead end to a keyboard user. Without a deliberate accessibility layer, every visual the system produces is invisible to that audience.

Without this subsystem, visuals either never appear, appear as raw JSON, or appear misleading and inaccessible. The Visual Intelligence subsystem exists to prevent all three.

### Design Decision & Tradeoffs

The central move is to **split the producer from the schema.** The envelope schema stays strict — that strictness is what buys deterministic rendering and lets an honesty gate reason about the diagram mechanically — but the analytical model is relieved of the burden of hitting that schema. A three-tier fallback stands between the model and a rendered visual:

1. **Model emits** a valid `ora-visual` envelope (rare, per the ~0% figure, but free when it happens).
2. **Deterministic recovery** of the model's *own* diagram: `visual_recovery.py` converts a model-drawn Mermaid/DAGitty/Structurizr block — or a malformed `ora-visual` envelope — into a schema-valid envelope of the correct kind, with **zero model calls** (`orchestrator/visual_recovery.py`). This is preferred over re-synthesis because it renders the diagram the model *actually drew* rather than re-deriving one.
3. **Prose→envelope synthesis**: `visual_synthesis.py` builds a fresh envelope from the analytical prose via one narrow, gated model call with a validate→repair loop (`MAX_REPAIR_ROUNDS = 2`, so at most three model calls total; `orchestrator/visual_synthesis.py:32`).

The alternative I rejected is **making the analytical model emit render-ready SVG directly.** It is tempting: one round-trip, no schema, no compiler. I rejected it because SVG from a model is non-deterministic (the same reasoning yields a different drawing each time), unauditable (there is no typed structure for an honesty gate to inspect — you would be reduced to regexing pixels), and it forecloses the accessibility layer (alt-text and keyboard navigation need to know that *this* mark is a stock and *that* one is a flow). The strict-envelope-plus-swappable-producer design keeps a single auditable contract at the center and lets the messy, model-dependent part live at the edges where it can fail open.

Honesty is enforced by an **adversarial Tufte gate**, `visual_adversarial.py`, which runs 15 `_t*` rules (verified: `grep -c 'def _t[0-9]'` = 15) plus LLM-prior-inversion and WCAG/overlap checks. It blocks on Critical findings and warns on Major. A Critical finding can force a fallback all the way to a table or prose. This is the component that prevents the truncated-axis and chartjunk failure mode: the same gate runs both server-side over the envelope and client-side over the rendered artifact.

Rendering is **client-side and vendored** — local-first, no CDN, CSP-safe. Every library ships in `server/static/ora-visual-compiler/vendor/` with a pinned version and a LICENSE file. A server-side headless-browser path exists for export (envelope→SVG without a live browser client).

The tradeoff is real and worth naming. This design pays for model-independence, honesty, and accessibility with (a) multiple model/render round-trips in the worst case, and (b) a large footprint — a vendored library set (Vega, Vega-Lite, Mermaid, D3, dagre, Ajv, viz-js, a hand-written Structurizr parser) plus a Playwright/Chromium dependency and a Node CLI fallback. A single-round direct-SVG design would be lighter and faster. I traded latency and disk for auditability and correctness, because for analytical diagrams a wrong-but-fast answer is a liability, not a feature.

**Open problems.** The recovery/synthesis kind-routing is subtle: when a mode accepts sibling kinds (a decision-under-uncertainty mode that lists both `tornado` and `decision_tree`), recovery of a sibling can mark the turn "rendered" and short-circuit synthesis of the kind actually requested. The current code restricts recovery to an explicitly-threaded `preferred_kind`, with the old accept-any-sibling behavior gated behind `ORA_VISUAL_RECOVER_SIBLINGS` (`orchestrator/boot.py:564-567`). This is a policy patch over a genuine ambiguity, not a closed problem. Separately, the true end-to-end envelope valid-rate is *observed* (Phase 0 emission telemetry sweeps every pipeline step) but there is no instrumented budget or success-rate threshold that gates behavior — the ~0% figure is a documented measurement of the problem, not a live SLO. Reliability here is qualitative; the failure-mode-prevented framing below is not backed by an instrumented percentage.

### Implementation

**Runtime entry.** Every response passes through `_run_visual_hook(response, context_pkg)` at `orchestrator/boot.py:286`, wired at the single call site `response = _run_visual_hook(response, context_pkg)` (`orchestrator/boot.py:9410`). The hook is available only if `visual_adversarial` imports cleanly; `VISUAL_HOOK_AVAILABLE` guards it (`orchestrator/boot.py:278-283`), and when a response contains no `ora-visual` blocks and no visual is expected, the hook is a no-op — zero impact on text-only pipelines.

**Data flow inside the hook** (braiding why with how):

- **Phase 0 — emission telemetry.** `_log_visual_emissions_for_turn` sweeps *all* pipeline steps, not just the final response, because an intermediate analyst may emit an envelope the step-8 formatter later rewrites into prose. This is observe-only, never mutates state, and is how the true valid-rate becomes measurable. Fully fail-open.
- **Validate / suppress.** If the response contains `ora-visual`, `_visual_process_response` (from `visual_adversarial.py`) validates each block and suppresses any with Critical findings, replacing it with a `[visual … suppressed: …]` marker so prose still flows. This prevents the dishonest-chart failure mode at the emission point. Any exception here returns the prose unchanged — the hook never blocks legitimate prose on its own bug.
- **Phase 1a — recovery.** If the mode expected a visual and the turn rendered zero valid envelopes (none emitted, or all suppressed), `_maybe_recover_visual` calls `visual_recovery.recover_envelope` over the final response *and* the earlier Gear-4 step texts (analyst / reviser / verifier), because the step-8 formatter routinely strips the diagram from the final text (`orchestrator/boot.py`, `_visual_recovery_texts`). Zero model calls. The recovered envelope still passes the same validator + adversarial gate.
- **Phase 1b — synthesis.** If recovery finds nothing recoverable, `_maybe_synthesize_visual` builds an envelope from cleaned prose via `synthesize_envelope`. This is gated to **interactive** execution contexts only — synthesis adds a model call, and unattended/autonomous runs get no surprise cost (matching the image-gen stance). The synthesis endpoint resolves through `_resolve_synthesis_endpoint` (`orchestrator/boot.py:489`), which respects a pinned per-request configuration so a repair call cannot silently execute on the wrong model set (the campaign-fidelity gate).
- **Client-facing diagnostics.** If a visual was recovered, synthesized, or repaired in place, the superseded failures are hidden from the user (the trace keeps the full record); otherwise the diagnostics surface. Everything is stashed to `step-visual-hook.json` in the per-turn trace so a wrong suppression is auditable after the fact.

**Server render.** `_render_visual_svg(env)` (`orchestrator/boot.py:897`) is the envelope→SVG entrypoint. The **primary** path is `_render_visual_svg_browser` (`orchestrator/boot.py:730`): it launches Playwright Chromium, serves the compiler scripts, the vendored libraries, and the schemas through an in-process route handler (with a path-traversal guard — requests outside the static/schema roots get 403, `orchestrator/boot.py:821`), boots Ajv via `OraVisualCompiler.bootstrapAjv({ schemaRoot: '/static/visual-schemas/' })` (`orchestrator/boot.py:844-848`), then calls `OraVisualCompiler.compileWithNav(env)` and returns the SVG. The **fallback** is `_render_visual_svg_cli` (`orchestrator/boot.py:876`): `subprocess.run(["node", render-envelope.js], …)` boots the same compiler under jsdom and pipes stdin→stdout. `_render_visual_svg` tries browser first, then CLI, then returns a combined error string — three-tier fail-through to prose (browser → CLI → nothing).

Note a drift risk here that a reader must internalize: the script list inside `_render_visual_svg_browser` (starting `orchestrator/boot.py:747`) is **hand-maintained**. I verified it loads all 15 named renderer files (`vega-lite.js`, `mermaid.js`, `causal-dag.js`, `c4.js`, `causal-loop-diagram.js`, `stock-and-flow.js`, `fishbone.js`, `decision-tree.js`, `influence-diagram.js`, `ach-matrix.js`, `quadrant-matrix.js`, `bow-tie.js`, `ibis.js`, `pro-con.js`, `concept-map.js`) plus `stub.js`, the vendored libraries, and the accessibility and adversarial scripts. Adding a renderer to the client `renderers/` directory without adding it to this list silently breaks server render only — the client keeps working, so the bug hides until an export.

**Render-critique-refine loop.** `_maybe_review_and_refine_visual` (`orchestrator/boot.py:1090`) renders the SVG, rasterizes it to PNG via `_rasterize_svg_light` (Playwright `set_content` + screenshot, `orchestrator/boot.py:909`), sends the PNG to a vision endpoint for critique, and revises the envelope. The critique endpoint resolves through the `image_critique` then `vision_input` slots and requires a vision-capable model (`_resolve_visual_critique_endpoint`, `orchestrator/boot.py:991-993`). If no vision endpoint is configured, this loop does not run (`"no image_critique vision endpoint configured"`, `orchestrator/boot.py:1019`). Whether it is enabled in the live production routing was not traced end-to-end.

**Client compiler.** `server/static/ora-visual-compiler/index.js` exposes `compileWithNav`: validate against the Ajv 2020 schema → dispatch through `dispatcher.js` to one of the 15 renderers → apply the accessibility layer (`alt-text-generator.js` produces Lundgard-Satyanarayan four-level descriptions, `aria-annotator.js` adds element-level ARIA, `keyboard-nav.js` adds Olli-style keyboard navigation) → run `artifact-adversarial.js`, which does the rendered-artifact honesty pass (overlap, text truncation, WCAG 2.1 contrast) and can force a fallback to table/prose on a Critical finding. The 15 renderer *files* cover 22 diagram *kinds* because two renderers are multi-kind: `mermaid.js` handles flowchart/sequence/state, and `vega-lite.js` handles comparison/time_series/distribution/scatter/heatmap. The synthesis-targetable kinds are enumerated in `_KNOWN_VISUAL_TYPES` (22 types; `orchestrator/boot.py:425-430`); `annotated_image` is deliberately excluded because it needs a user-uploaded backdrop and cannot be synthesized from prose alone.

**Canvas and V3 dispatch.** `server/static/visual-panel.js` (the largest client file) is a Konva-based dual-pane canvas with a four-layer stage — background, annotation, user-input, selection — supporting pan/zoom/select/keyboard-nav, shape tools with undo-redo, and annotation authoring. `server/static/js/v3-visual-dispatch.js` is the V3 replacement for the retired classic fence-extraction: `dispatch(text, key)` pulls the last `ora-visual` envelope from an assistant turn, hands it to `OraPanels.visual.onBridgeUpdate` (a `canvas_action` state machine — replace/update/annotate/clear, where user drawings survive everything except an explicit replace/clear), and swaps the raw JSON fence for a one-line marker. The `key` argument deduplicates so a header refresh does not re-fire the same envelope.

**Canvas file format.** `orchestrator/canvas_file_format.py` is the server-side reader/writer for `.ora-canvas` files (gzip-wrapped canonical JSON), a byte-identical mirror of the client `canvas-serializer.js`, used for vault export/autosave and server previews.

**Vision extraction.** `orchestrator/visual_extraction.py` (WP-4.3) invokes a vision-capable model on a user image, parses the result into `spatial_representation` JSON, validates with a single repair pass, and scores confidence — the inbound direction (image → structure) complementing the outbound envelope→SVG.

**Media adjuncts** (a related, Mac-bound cluster, not the diagram path):
- `orchestrator/render.py` is the **FFmpeg video** render engine — timeline→ffmpeg command builder, `drawtext`/`drawbox` lower-thirds and title cards, an optional two-pass encode on the `high` preset, and a `RenderManager` with progress SSE. This is *video*, not envelope→SVG; the name invites confusion. `_resolve_font_file` (`orchestrator/render.py:247`) picks from a candidate list of macOS system fonts with a hardcoded Mac fallback.
- `orchestrator/media_capture.py` wraps FFmpeg for screen + microphone capture. The installed system implements **macOS only** via `avfoundation`; the Windows (`gdigrab`/`ddagrab` + `dshow`) branch is present in the docstring but explicitly stubbed — the code raises `NotImplementedError("Windows capture lands in Phase 10")` (`orchestrator/media_capture.py:159, 259`).
- `orchestrator/transcription.py` wraps `whisper-cli` (whisper.cpp) — ffmpeg 16 kHz mono extract → whisper-cli JSON → hallucination filter.
- `orchestrator/waveform.py` shells `ffmpeg showwavespic` to a small PNG.

**Library inventory with licenses** (versions and license text read from each `vendor/*/VERSION` and `LICENSE`): Vega 6.2.0 (BSD-3-Clause), Vega-Lite 6.4.2 (BSD-3-Clause), Mermaid 11.14.0 (MIT), D3 7.9.0 (ISC), dagre 0.8.5 (MIT — header "Permission is hereby granted, free of charge…", Copyright 2012-2014 Chris Pettitt), Ajv 8.18.0 2020-12 bundle (MIT), viz-js 3.26.0 (ships a Third-Party Notices file covering its Graphviz/Emscripten sublicenses — confirm before redistribution), structurizr-mini (hand-written parser + renderer, Context/Container only), and Konva (MIT, Anton Lavrenov) for the canvas. All vendored locally; no CDN; CSP-safe.

**Config surfaces.** `config/visual-schemas/specs/` holds the 23 JSON-Schema-2020-12 specs (verified count) plus example envelopes and guides. `config/mode-to-visual.json` (`_schema_version` 0.2, `_last_updated` 2026-06-13) sets per-mode `visual_types`, `relation_to_prose_default`, and `adversarial_strictness`; its `_comment` states it is synced manually to the vault reference "until generation is automated" (`config/mode-to-visual.json:2`). Environment flags: `ORA_VISUAL_RECOVER_SIBLINGS` (restore accept-any-sibling recovery), `ORA_VISUAL_RECOVER_ANY` (recover an unrequested diagram in interactive contexts; default off).

**Failure behavior is fail-open everywhere.** A visual-hook exception returns prose unchanged. A render failure falls browser → CLI → prose. A synthesis or recovery exception is caught and skipped. No path in this subsystem can turn a good text answer into an error.

### Evidence & Verification

**Python suites** (`orchestrator/tests/`): `test_visual_validator`, `test_visual_adversarial`, `test_visual_phase2_rules`, `test_visual_synthesis`, `test_visual_fallback`, `test_visual_extraction`, `test_visual_routing`, `test_visual_e2e`, `test_visual_merged_input`, `test_canvas_file_format`, `test_media_library_transcript`, `test_render_two_pass`, `test_waveform`. Recovery is exercised through the fallback and e2e suites.

**JS harness**: `server/static/ora-visual-compiler/tests/` with 39 case directories (verified: `ls tests/cases/` = 39), run via `node run.js`; plus a V3-dispatch case (`tests/cases/test-v3-visual-dispatch.js`, cited at 12 assertions in the module's audit note). Exact aggregate pass counts were not re-run in this pass — CLAUDE.md cites figures at various dates; treat those as stale until re-run.

**Live-fire**: a Tier A run across the visual-bearing modes was executed during development on 2026-04-18.

**How to confirm it works.** Emit an `ora-visual` block in an interactive turn and check `step-visual-hook.json` in the per-turn trace for `visuals_seen`/`visuals_suppressed`/`synthesized`. To confirm server render, call `_render_visual_svg(env)` on a known-good envelope and verify the SVG opens; a `browser render failed … cli fallback failed` string tells you which tier broke. To confirm recovery, feed a raw Mermaid block through a mode configured for a matching kind and check for `recovered: true` in the diagnostics.

**Explicitly untested surfaces.** `orchestrator/video_suggestions.py` and `orchestrator/preview.py` are not in the listed suites — behavior unverified. The render-critique-refine loop runs only when a vision endpoint is configured. Whether server-side Playwright render fires in the live cloud pipeline (versus only for vault export) was not traced to its call sites. **All non-macOS platforms are untested** (see below). Reliability here is qualitative: there is no instrumented reliability budget, success-rate metric, or per-step percentage — the ~0%-valid-envelope figure is a documented measurement of the problem, not a live gate.

### Expert Reading Notes

Read `orchestrator/boot.py:286-1200` first — the hook, the three-tier fallback, both render paths, the rasterizer, and the critique loop all live here, and this is where every design decision lands and where drift concentrates. Then read `orchestrator/visual_recovery.py`'s module docstring for the ~0%-valid-rate motivation and the DSL-native-versus-malformed-envelope split, and `orchestrator/visual_adversarial.py`'s 15 `_t*` rules for the honesty policy.

**Drift hotspots, in priority order:**

1. **The hand-maintained script list in `_render_visual_svg_browser` (`orchestrator/boot.py:747`)** must stay in sync with `renderers/` and `vendor/`. Adding a renderer to the client without adding it here breaks server render silently — the failure surfaces only on export. This is the single most likely place a future change introduces a hidden bug.
2. **`config/mode-to-visual.json` is manually synced** to the vault reference (its own `_comment` says so). Canonical-versus-operational drift risk; auto-generation is planned, not built.
3. **The 23 schemas ↔ 16 renderer files ↔ 22 kinds mapping is non-obvious** because `mermaid.js` and `vega-lite.js` are multi-kind. Anyone counting files against schemas will be confused; the mapping is the thing to hold in mind, not the raw counts.
4. **CLAUDE.md §101 describes the client boot sequence and can be misread** as claiming the server render path "mirrors `render-envelope.js`" (jsdom). The code is unambiguous: Playwright/Chromium is **primary** (`_render_visual_svg_browser`) and node/jsdom is only the **fallback** (`_render_visual_svg_cli`). Trust the code.
5. **A stale `mode_success_criterion` hook** remains as a placeholder comment in `visual_adversarial.review_envelope`; the backing `mode_success_criteria.py` was deleted 2026-05-10 (CLAUDE.md §240). Do not credit per-mode structural success checks that no longer run.
6. **`render.py` is video, not diagrams.** Grouping it with the visual subsystem invites treating it as the envelope renderer. The envelope renderer is `boot.py:_render_visual_svg` plus `render-envelope.js`.

### Platform compatibility

The client compiler and Konva canvas are pure browser JavaScript with vendored, CSP-safe libraries; they run in any modern browser regardless of server OS. Everything server-side was tested only on macOS at 7a5e8f40. I label each surface by evidence, not assumption.

| Subsystem | macOS | Windows-native | WSL | Linux | Evidence / note |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Client compiler + accessibility + artifact-adversarial | works | works | n/a | works | Pure browser JS, vendored libs, no CDN. `server/static/ora-visual-compiler/`. OS-independent by construction. |
| Konva canvas (`visual-panel.js`, V3 dispatch) | works | works | n/a | works | Browser JS; same rationale. |
| Server envelope→SVG render, **primary** (Playwright/Chromium) | works | untested | untested | untested | `_render_visual_svg_browser` (`boot.py:730`) uses `p.chromium.launch()`. Playwright supports all OSes but requires a `playwright install` browser; Ora run only on macOS. Path handling assumes the `~/ora` `server/static` layout. |
| Server envelope→SVG render, **fallback** (node + jsdom CLI) | works | untested | untested | untested | `subprocess.run(["node", render-envelope.js])` (`boot.py:876`). Relies on `node` on PATH with **no absolute fallback** (unlike ffmpeg); jsdom vendored under `tests/node_modules`. |
| SVG rasterization (critique loop) | works | untested | untested | untested | `_rasterize_svg_light` (`boot.py:909`), same Playwright dependency; only runs when a vision endpoint is configured. |
| FFmpeg video render (`render.py`) | works | untested | untested | untested | `FFMPEG_BINARY = shutil.which("ffmpeg") or "/opt/homebrew/bin/ffmpeg"` (`render.py:71`). PATH lookup is portable; the fallback is a Homebrew-on-Apple-Silicon path. `drawtext` requires the homebrew-ffmpeg tap on Mac (per project memory). |
| Video-render font resolution (`_resolve_font_file`) | works | needs-porting | needs-porting | needs-porting | `render.py:247` candidates are all `/System/Library/Fonts` and `/Library/Fonts`; the final fallback is a hardcoded Mac path that will not exist elsewhere. macOS-only. |
| Screen/mic capture (`media_capture.py`) | works | not-implemented | n/a | not-implemented | macOS `avfoundation` only; the Windows branch raises `NotImplementedError("Windows capture lands in Phase 10")` (`media_capture.py:159, 259`). No Linux `x11grab` path exists. |
| Transcription (`transcription.py`, whisper.cpp) | works | untested | untested | untested | `whisper-cli` via PATH with `/opt/homebrew/bin/whisper-cli` fallback. whisper.cpp builds cross-platform, but binary path and models layout assume Mac. |
| Waveform (`waveform.py`) | works | untested | untested | untested | ffmpeg `showwavespic`; same ffmpeg-binary assumptions as above. |
| `WORKSPACE` / path form (`~/ora`, vault `~/Documents/vault`) | works | needs-porting | untested | untested | `os.path.expanduser("~/ora")` — tilde expands cross-platform, but a `%USERPROFILE%\ora` layout is untested and the vault path is Mac-specific. |
| JS test harness (`tests/run.js`, jsdom) | works | untested | untested | untested | `cd tests && node run.js`; node/jsdom are cross-platform but run only on macOS. |

No non-macOS server surface is claimed to work without evidence. The one surface with a *definite* off-Mac status is `media_capture.py`: its Windows path is coded-but-stubbed (raises `NotImplementedError`) and Linux is absent, so it is "not-implemented," not merely "untested." Cross-reference the Audio/Video Media & Timeline Editing chapter for the adjacent Mac-bound media-capture and render/export tooling.

## 11. Audio/Video Media & Timeline Editing

### Problem

A user drops a screen recording into a conversation, wants to trim the dead air off the front, stack a title card over the first three seconds, watermark the corner, and export an MP4 — without opening a non-linear editor and without ever mutating the original file. Separately, they want the clip transcribed, the transcript filed as a searchable vault note, and the system to propose where the cuts should go.

Without a dedicated subsystem, two failure modes dominate. The first is destructive editing: any pipeline that trims or re-encodes the source in place makes every edit irreversible and every "undo" a re-import. The second is the wrong tool. Ora already ships the Visual Intelligence diagram pipeline (see the Visual Intelligence chapter), which compiles typed JSON envelopes into SVG. Media editing is a categorically different problem — the artifacts are real audio/video files that only FFmpeg can produce, not vector graphics — and forcing it through the diagram compiler would produce nothing playable. This subsystem exists to give the conversation a non-destructive A/V editing layer that terminates in a real rendered file.

### Design Decision & Tradeoffs

Two load-bearing decisions define the layer.

**Decision one: the media library holds references, not files.** `orchestrator/media_library.py` stores a per-conversation JSON list of entries, each recording a `source_path` (absolute, resolved), classification, ffprobe metadata, and an optional generated thumbnail path (`media_library.py:240-256`). `remove()` deletes the library entry and its thumbnail file but never touches the source (`media_library.py:270-294`, docstring "Does NOT delete the source file"). This is the Unix symlink discipline applied to media: the library is a directory of pointers, cheap to mutate and structurally incapable of destroying user data. The failure mode it prevents is data loss on edit — a source cannot be lost by editing the timeline, because the timeline never holds the source. The tradeoff is referential fragility: if the user moves or renames the underlying file, the entry dangles, and nothing in the library repairs the pointer. `add_entry` validates existence at add time (`media_library.py:231-232`), but nothing re-validates later.

**Decision two: the timeline is a normalize-on-I/O JSON document compiled to a single FFmpeg pass at render time.** `orchestrator/timeline.py` defines a schema-version-1 state — tracks (video / audio / pip / music / overlay), clips carrying `track_position_ms` / `in_point_ms` / `out_point_ms` / transition / volume / fade, Phase-6 overlay clips (lower-third / title-card / watermark), and a global watermark. Every `load()` and `save()` runs the state through `_normalize_state`, which clamps numeric fields, fills defaults, enforces track-id uniqueness, and recomputes `duration_ms` as the maximum clip end across all tracks (`timeline.py:337-361`). Editing operations mutate this JSON only. Nothing decodes a frame until render. At render, `orchestrator/render.py` reads the timeline plus a library snapshot and emits one FFmpeg filtergraph — trims become `trim`/`atrim`, gaps become black/silence pad, overlays become `drawbox`+`drawtext`, the watermark becomes a `drawtext` ◎ glyph or an image overlay (`render.py:19-24`, `render.py:302-500`).

The main alternative rejected was a persistent frame-accurate edit engine — a resident decode/composite pipeline that maintains rendered state per edit, the architecture a desktop NLE uses. It does not fit a conversation-embedded tool: it demands a long-lived media process per conversation, frame caches, and re-encode-on-edit, none of which suit a Flask request/response server where conversations are cheap and numerous. Deferring all compositing to a single terminal FFmpeg pass keeps edit operations to JSON writes and makes render a stateless subprocess.

The tradeoff is that render is an all-or-nothing black box whose correctness rides entirely on FFmpeg's filter behavior, and `drawtext` is the fragile part of that surface — a filter known for quoting and font-resolution failures (`render.py:224-240` is a dedicated escaping helper written because of it). The Phase-7 MVP is deliberately narrow: it renders the **first** video-kind track and the **first** audio-kind track, cut transitions only (`render.py:8-18`).

**Open problems**, stated plainly and traced to the code that defers them:
- Only the `dissolve` transition is deferred — it needs clip-overlap timing (xfade) the MVP does not compute (`render.py:555,787-788`). Fade-from-black and fade-to-black ARE rendered, via `fade=t=in`/`fade=t=out` on individual clips (`render.py:561-570`). The schema accepts `dissolve` (`timeline.py:71-72`), so a state describing a dissolve is silently treated as a cut — a live schema-vs-renderer gap for that one transition.
- Multi-video composition (including picture-in-picture) and multi-audio mixing via `amix` are IMPLEMENTED in `_assemble_command` (`render.py:772-946`): pip-kind tracks scale to 320px and layer, and more than one audio (audio+music) track mixes via `amix`. Both `pip` and `music` track kinds reach the render output.
- The LLM suggestion path is built but unreached (see Implementation).

### Implementation

The subsystem is nine orchestrator modules behind a uniform Flask surface in `server/app.py`, each import guarded by a `_HAS_*` flag so a missing module or a missing FFmpeg degrades to an HTTP 503 rather than crashing the server.

**Module map.** `media_library.py` (reference library), `timeline.py` (edit state), `render.py` (FFmpeg compilation), `video_suggestions.py` (edit-suggestion generator), `transcription.py` (whisper.cpp + cloud), `vault_transcript.py` (transcript note writer), `waveform.py` (audio waveform PNGs), `media_capture.py` (screen/mic capture), `preview.py` (frame extraction + low-res proxy).

**Endpoint surface.** `server.py` wires `/api/media-library/*` (list / add / remove / rename / thumbnail / waveform / transcript / import-url / suggest-edits), `/api/timeline/<id>` (GET/PUT), `/api/transcribe/*`, `/api/render/*` (presets / start / state / cancel), and `/api/preview/*`. Every handler opens with its `_HAS_*` guard — for example `render_start` returns 503 unless render, timeline, and media library are all available.

**Two event hooks braid the modules together.** A capture-complete hook auto-adds a finished capture into the conversation's media library; a transcription-complete hook (`server.py:343-379`) writes the canonical vault note on the `complete` event and stashes the returned path so the polling `/state` endpoint can surface it. The hook is fail-soft: a vault-write exception is caught and logged, the transcription itself stays `complete`, and the user can re-export manually (`server.py:373-379`). Note one drift to trust the code over the docstring: the hook passes `transcription_model="whisper-large-v3-local"` as a fixed string (`server.py:372`) regardless of which engine actually ran, so the vault note's model attribution is not authoritative.

**Render data flow.** `render_start` (`server.py:2153-2156`) calls `timeline.load()` and `media_library.list_entries()`, then passes both snapshots as arguments into `RenderManager.start(conversation_id, preset_key, timeline, library, export_dir=...)`. This argument-passing is deliberate: `render.py` imports neither `timeline` nor `media_library`, so the renderer is a pure function of the two snapshots it is handed — testable in isolation, and incapable of racing a concurrent edit because it operates on a captured snapshot. Inside, `_collect_clip_sources` builds a `media_entry_id → source_path` map (`render.py:274-280`), a position sort orders clips, and the command builder assembles trim/concat/pad plus overlay and watermark filters. The export directory resolves from the user setting `export.default_directory`, defaulting to `~/ora/exports/` (`render.py:70`, `server.py:2144-2149`).

**Presets and two-pass.** `PRESETS` (`render.py:83-186`) defines `standard` / `high` / `web` / `mov` / `webm` / `audio_only` and an internal `preview_proxy` (360p, `ultrafast`, not in the user-facing menu — selected programmatically by `preview.py`). Codecs are `libx264` / `aac` / `libvpx-vp9` / `libopus` — portable software codecs, no VideoToolbox dependency, so encoding itself is cross-platform once FFmpeg is present. The `high` preset sets `v_two_pass: True` (`render.py:108`); `_split_for_two_pass` (`render.py:710-754`) builds a pass-1 analysis argv writing stats to a per-render `.x264-pass-<render_id>` passlog and a pass-2 encode argv reading them, with the passlog path returned for cleanup. Render runs as a `subprocess.Popen` (`_Render.process`, `render.py:203`) with progress parsing and cancel via terminate; the passlog sidecars are cleaned after both passes.

**Transcription.** `transcription.py` shells `whisper-cli` (whisper.cpp) after extracting 16 kHz mono WAV with FFmpeg, parses `--print-progress`, and persists `<source>.whisper.json` next to the source — the file both `/transcript` and `/suggest-edits` read later. OpenRouter cloud transcription and audio-model paths exist as network alternatives.

**Suggestions.** `video_suggestions.py` ships two paths. `generate_suggestions_heuristic` is deterministic pure Python — filler-word cuts, silence-gap cuts, false-start detection, chapter markers from discourse cues, a title-card topic guess — validated against `config/framework-schemas/video-editing-suggestions.schema.json` (present on disk). `generate_suggestions_llm` assembles the framework prompt from `frameworks/book/video-editing-suggestions.md` (also present on disk), calls an injected `model_callable`, and extracts/validates JSON with heuristic fallback. **The LLM path is partial: it is built and unit-tested but no endpoint invokes it.** The `/suggest-edits` handler calls only the heuristic generator (`server.py:1116`), and both the module docstring and the endpoint docstring state the live wiring is "left for the user's gating." At 7a5e8f40 the installed suggestion behavior is heuristic-only.

**Failure behavior.** Corrupt library or timeline JSON is quarantined: `load()`/`_load()` catch the parse exception, `replace()` the file to a `.json.broken` sidecar for forensics, and return defaults (`timeline.py:384-391`, `media_library.py:191-198`). Thumbnail generation failure is non-fatal — `_generate_thumbnail` returns `False`, the entry carries `thumbnail_path: None`, and the UI falls back to a glyph (`media_library.py:139-155,251`). Waveform generation is lazy and 404s on failure rather than erroring the entry.

### Evidence & Verification

**Tested surfaces.** The subsystem carries seven test files, and the mechanisms they cover are the ones most likely to break silently: `test_video_suggestions.py` (16 tests, heuristic generator), `test_waveform.py` (12), `test_render_two_pass.py` (13, two-pass **command construction**), `test_media_library_transcript.py` (5, transcript-normalization slice), `test_watermark_upload.py` (15), `test_url_import.py` (13), and `test_multi_stop_gradient.py` (14, gradient stop parsing, 2–8 stops).

**How to confirm it works end to end.** Start the server, POST a real media file to `/api/media-library/<conversation_id>`, PUT a timeline with one trimmed clip to `/api/timeline/<id>`, POST to `/api/render/<id>`, then poll `/api/render/<render_id>/state` until `complete` and confirm the output file exists in the export directory. This is the only way to exercise the actual FFmpeg execution, because —

**Explicitly untested surfaces**, stated so a maintainer does not over-trust the green suite:
- There is **no `test_timeline.py`**. The entire `_normalize_state` clamp/default/duration-compute path — the schema contract that both the UI and `render.py` depend on — is exercised only indirectly. This is the highest-value coverage gap in the subsystem.
- There is **no full `test_media_library.py`**. Only the transcript-normalization slice is covered; `add_entry` / `remove` / `rename` / thumbnail generation are untested in isolation.
- `test_render_two_pass.py` asserts the **command that would be run**, not the output of running it. No test executes FFmpeg against real media and inspects the result, so `drawtext` correctness, filtergraph validity, and overlay timing are unverified by the suite.
- The LLM suggestion path has unit tests but no live wiring, so its end-to-end behavior against a real model is unverified.
- **No runtime render was performed for this documentation pass.** Every "works on macOS" claim below rests on code inspection plus the existing unit tests, not on a live export.

### Expert Reading Notes

Read in this order: `timeline.py` first (the schema is the contract for both the UI and the renderer — understand `_normalize_state` before anything else), then `render.py` (how that schema becomes an FFmpeg command), then `media_library.py` (what the renderer resolves clip sources against).

Where future changes most likely cause drift:

- **The schema accepts more than the renderer honors.** `VALID_TRANSITIONS_IN` includes `dissolve`/`fade-from-black` and `VALID_TRANSITIONS_OUT` includes `dissolve`/`fade-to-black` (`timeline.py:71-72`); `render.py` honors fade-from-black/fade-to-black and treats only `dissolve` as a cut (a silent no-op). Anyone adding dissolve support must close this at both ends, and anyone adding a transition to the schema without the renderer produces a silent no-op.

- **The deferrals comment in `render.py` is partly stale.** `render.py:30` lists "Live preview (low-res proxy generated on demand)" as deferred, but `orchestrator/preview.py` exists, is wired at `/api/preview/*`, and is functional: it extracts a frame via FFmpeg, renders a 360p proxy through `RenderManager` using the `preview_proxy` preset, and invalidates the proxy on timeline mutation via a content signature (`preview.py:75,104-201`). Trust the module, not the comment — preview is implemented, not deferred. Treat the `render.py` header comment as documenting Phase-7 intent, not current wiring.

- **The docstrings describe intent by phase label; wiring is what ships.** `video_suggestions.py` advertises an LLM path that no endpoint reaches. `media_capture.py:3-4` claims macOS + Windows; the Windows branch raises `NotImplementedError`. When a docstring and the call graph disagree, the call graph is the installed system.

- **`_resolve_font_file` fails silently at import time on non-Mac.** It enumerates only `/System/Library/Fonts` and `/Library/Fonts` and, on a total miss, unconditionally returns `/System/Library/Fonts/Supplemental/Courier New Bold.ttf` (`render.py:247-266`); the result is cached in the module-level `_FONT_FILE` at import (`render.py:269`). On Linux or Windows every candidate misses and the function still returns a Mac path that does not exist, so `drawtext` receives a nonexistent `fontfile=` and the failure surfaces only as an FFmpeg render error, never at startup. This is the first thing to fix in any off-Mac port.

- **The portability retrofit skipped this subsystem entirely.** HEAD is `7a5e8f40` ("Execution Review Phase 1-4: macOS+Windows portability retrofit"); `git diff 82553162..7a5e8f40` for the five core A/V files (`media_library.py`, `timeline.py`, `render.py`, `video_suggestions.py`, `media_capture.py`) is empty. The retrofit hardened other subsystems and did not touch the A/V layer, so its Mac-only assumptions persist unmodified. Do not assume any "portability" claim elsewhere in the tree covers these files.

### Platform compatibility

Every platform-sensitive surface in this subsystem, labeled by evidence. No off-Mac surface is labeled "works" — the pass was read-only on macOS, so non-Mac rows are "untested" or "needs-porting" per what the code structurally requires.

- **FFmpeg / ffprobe binary discovery** — macOS: **works** (on PATH, or `/opt/homebrew/bin` fallback). Windows-native / WSL / Linux: **untested**. Resolution is `shutil.which("ffmpeg") or "/opt/homebrew/bin/ffmpeg"` (`media_library.py:59-60`, `render.py:71-72`, plus `waveform.py`, `transcription.py`, `media_capture.py`). If FFmpeg is on PATH it resolves anywhere; only the fallback is Mac-specific. Software codecs (`libx264`/`aac`/`libvpx-vp9`/`libopus`) are portable, so encoding is cross-platform *once FFmpeg is present* — but this was not run off-Mac.

- **`drawtext` overlays (title-cards / lower-thirds / watermark glyph)** — macOS: **works, conditionally** — stock Homebrew FFmpeg lacks `drawtext`; the `homebrew-ffmpeg/ffmpeg/ffmpeg` tap is required (a known install prerequisite). Windows-native / WSL / Linux: **needs-porting**. Two independent blockers off-Mac: (1) whether apt/choco FFmpeg ships `drawtext` compiled in is **untested**; (2) `_resolve_font_file` returns a nonexistent Mac font path on every non-Mac host (`render.py:247-266`), so overlays and watermark fail at render regardless of the FFmpeg build. The failure mode is a nonexistent `fontfile=` surfacing as an FFmpeg error; there is no instrumented success rate.

- **Screen + microphone capture** — macOS: **mac-only, works** (avfoundation). Windows-native: **needs-porting** — the branch raises `NotImplementedError("Windows capture lands in Phase 10")` (`media_capture.py:158-159`). WSL: **n/a** (no display/audio capture surface). Linux: **needs-porting** — would require x11grab/pulse; `_build_ffmpeg_command` raises `RuntimeError` on any non-Darwin, non-Windows platform (`media_capture.py:157-160`), and device discovery early-returns empty off Darwin.

- **Whisper transcription (local engine)** — macOS: **works** (`whisper-cli`, models under `~/ora/models/whisper`). Windows-native / WSL / Linux: **untested** — whisper.cpp builds on those platforms but was not run here, and the `/opt/homebrew` fallback in the binary resolver is Mac-only (`transcription.py:46-48`). The OpenRouter cloud transcription path is a network call and therefore platform-agnostic (untested off-Mac but carries no platform-specific code).

- **Atomic JSON writes (tmp + `replace`) for library/timeline state** — macOS / WSL / Linux: **works** (POSIX `Path.replace` is atomic; `media_library.py:209`, `timeline.py:399`). Windows-native: **untested** — `os.replace` is atomic same-volume on Windows but can raise if the target is held open by another handle; concurrent-access behavior was not exercised.

- **`subprocess.Popen` for ffmpeg/ffprobe/whisper-cli/render-cancel** — macOS / WSL / Linux: **works** (argv lists, no `shell=True`). Windows-native: **untested** — process termination and cancel use generic APIs, but signal/terminate semantics on Windows were not verified.

- **Hardcoded home/workspace paths (`~/ora`, `~/Documents/vault`, `/opt/homebrew`)** — macOS: **works**. WSL / Linux: **works** for `~/ora` and `~/Documents/vault` (`expanduser` resolves); `/opt/homebrew` fallbacks are inert but only reached if FFmpeg is off PATH. Windows-native: **untested** — `~` maps to `%USERPROFILE%` and forward-slash paths mostly work in Python, but the `/opt/homebrew` fallbacks are meaningless. The vault transcript root `~/Documents/vault` is hardcoded (`vault_transcript.py:37`).

- **Localhost Flask endpoint layer** — macOS: **works**. Windows-native / WSL / Linux: **untested but platform-neutral** — the endpoint code is pure Python/Flask; all platform risk lives in the media modules it dispatches to, not the routing.

- **Portability-retrofit coverage of this subsystem** — macOS: **works**. Windows-native / WSL / Linux: **needs-porting**. Commit `7a5e8f40` did not modify any A/V file (`git diff 82553162..7a5e8f40` for the five core files is empty), so the Mac-only assumptions above were never addressed by the retrofit that hardened other subsystems.


---

# Part IV — Supervision & Governance

## 12. Meta-Layer Observation and Explicit Project Oversight

### Problem

Long-lived projects still need reality-contact signals: a milestone checkbox can change without evidence, a corpus reference can go stale, and a child project's progress may matter to its parent. Those facts justify observation and durable event records. They do not justify an autonomous judgment or transition engine.

### Design Decision & Tradeoffs

The installed oversight runtime is observational. File events and exact time-caused deadlines feed the existing event bus. `oversight_router.process_event` records an event, suppresses Stealth persistence, prevents fan-out recursion, and may surface a child event to its parent. It never invokes Process Coherence, parses a framework verdict, mutates project state, or dispatches Programming work.

Process Coherence v5.0 is instead an explicitly invoked project/framework judgment. When a caller chooses it at a boundary configured by Oversight Configuration v3.0, it returns exactly `PROCEED`, `REVISE`, or `ESCALATE`. The caller retains responsibility for any permitted next action. These labels are unrelated to Programming's `CONTINUE`, `FIX`, `DONE`, and `ASK USER` outcomes.

This removes the former `ORA_OVERSIGHT_LIVE` fork, automatic context-to-model route, seven-directive parser, REVISE counter, and verdict-action dispatcher. There is no dormant live mode to enable and no simulated-supervision warning.

### Implementation

- **Event/deadline daemon.** `oversight_daemon.py` runs the OS file-event lane, persisted exact-deadline lane, and bounded watchdog introduced by G1.10. Time is used only where time is the actual cause; file-caused work runs from its event.
- **Event bus.** `oversight_events.emit` normalizes and records non-Stealth events, then invokes registered observational handlers without allowing one handler failure to break the bus.
- **Router.** `oversight_router.py` produces `logged_only`, `fan_out_audit_only`, or `stealth_suppressed`. Fan-out records carry the existing recursion sentinel and cannot fan out again.
- **Parent visibility.** `oversight_relationships.py` may append one managed child-update derivative to a parent's Decision Log. The parent is informed; no parent-side judgment is invoked.
- **Durable utilities.** `oversight_actions.py` retains lock-protected managed Decision Log derivatives, reversible privacy/delete support, the existing human queue, and action-log utilities used by active callers. It contains no Process Coherence verdict parser or dispatcher.
- **Health.** `oversight_health.py` reports actual daemon/thread or telemetry degradation. It no longer reports a configuration-disabled judgment path.
- **Explicit frameworks.** The canonical behavior of project-level judgment lives in `Framework — Oversight Configuration.md` v3.0 and `Framework — Process Coherence.md` v5.0, not in the event router.

### Evidence & Verification

The active tests exercise Stealth suppression, observational routing, fan-out recursion prevention, managed Decision Log derivative preservation/removal, event/deadline liveness, and system-protection boundaries. Runtime behavior—not old routing tables or historical papers—is authoritative.

### Expert Reading Notes

Read `oversight_router.py::process_event` for the complete router authority boundary, `oversight_events.py` and `oversight_daemon.py` for event/deadline observation, and `oversight_actions.py` only for its remaining durable utilities. Historical documents that describe automatic Process Coherence invocation, seven directives, Process Runs, or `ORA_OVERSIGHT_LIVE` do not describe the installed system.
## 13. Execution-Review Architecture

### Problem

An orchestrator that can touch reality — spawn a shell, `git push`, fetch a URL, call an MCP server, run a project tool, write a file — has two failure modes that no amount of model self-narration can catch, because the model is the thing being trusted.

The first is the auto-approve escape hatch. Ora's dispatcher has a permission mode. When a user (or a background configuration) sets it to auto-approve, every tool call the model emits runs without a human in the loop. That is the intended ergonomics for a long analytical turn — you do not want to approve forty file reads by hand. But the same setting will carry an irreversible action (`rm -rf`, a force-push, a `curl | sh`) or a secret-sensitivity read (a credential file, a private key) straight through to execution. The permission mode is a single dial, and turning it to "fast" turns off the brake on the one action class where the brake matters most. This is a privilege-escalation-by-convenience race: the dangerous call and the harmless call travel the same path, gated by the same dial.

The second is the reviewer grading the wrong artifact. When a downstream verifier — human or model — reads a turn's output and sees "I verified the tests pass and the migration is safe," it is reading a *producer claim*, a sentence the producing model wrote about its own work. A fluent claim out-argues a silent failure. If the model never actually ran the tests, the claim is indistinguishable in the text from the case where it did. The reviewer grades the model's homework using the model's own answer key. Without an independent record of what the turn *mechanically did* — what it mutated, what sources it read, what left the machine, how sensitive any of it was — there is no ground truth to check the narration against. The model grades itself, and confident prose wins.

Execution Review is the always-on instrumentation-and-gating layer that makes what a turn did to reality mechanically observable, so that neither failure mode depends on trusting the narration. I will define the load-bearing terms as they arrive; the first is *turn*, meaning one user-input-to-final-output cycle of the pipeline, which is the unit everything here is scoped to.

### Design Decision & Tradeoffs

The architectural move is to split what-was-declared from what-was-observed onto two clocks, and to make the before-clock deterministic and fail-closed.

**Two clocks.** The *before-clock* runs before the turn executes: a deterministic risk classifier assigns a *risk tier* (one of `light` / `standard` / `high-risk` / `irreversible`, an ordered severity estimate computed with no model call), and a fail-closed capability gate stands at the single point where the model's tool calls enter execution. The *after-clock* runs after the turn: it folds the mechanical tool-event log — one JSON line recorded per action at the invocation boundary, never from narration — into a small set of observed signals, and compares them against what the before-clock declared. Declaration and observation never share a code path, so a divergence between "I said this was a read-only turn" and "the log shows an external write" is structurally recordable rather than something the model can paper over.

**Fail-closed at the single chokepoint, independent of permission mode.** The gate lives at `dispatcher.py:735`, the one model-facing call site through which every dispatched tool passes. It runs *before* the existing permission check and *independently of the permission mode*. An action classified as irreversible, secret-sensitivity, or — critically — *unknown* is blocked regardless of whether the dispatcher is set to auto-approve. The rationale is the Unix principle of a chokepoint: you enforce an invariant at the one place all traffic crosses, not at every caller. The manifest's default for an unclassified action is fail-closed: unknown means irreversible-and-secret means blocked (`FAIL_CLOSED`, `tool_events.py:101-106`). The tradeoff is real and I will not soften it — a novel tool with no declared axes is refused until someone classifies it, which trades convenience for the guarantee that auto-approve can never carry an unaudited capability. That is the correct trade for this class of risk; a system that fails open on the unknown case has no gate at all.

**Structural separation of evidence from judgment.** The after-clock's output is a packet whose lanes come in two kinds, and the separation is enforced by the type system rather than by discipline. An `EvidenceLane` is a mechanical, verifiable check that yields a real verdict. A `JudgmentLane` is interpretive — a taste call — and *by construction has no `verdict` or `sufficient` field to hold a verdict* (`execution_packet.py:81-88`). Taste cannot masquerade as a passing check because there is no slot for it to occupy. The renderer then orders the packet evidence-first and producer-claim-last, and labels the producer claim as unverified text (`render_for_review`, `execution_packet.py:236`). The reviewer sees what the turn mechanically did before it sees what the turn said about itself.

**The rejected alternative: a parallel review pipeline.** The obvious design is a second Gear-3/Gear-4 adversarial pass that reviews the first turn's work — a separate reviewer stage with its own model calls. I rejected it. It doubles the pipeline surface, it invites the reviewer to re-derive the producer's claims from the same text (grading the homework again, one layer up), and it is expensive. Instead the packet is a *richer form of the existing terminal deliverable*, built at the same `run_pipeline` terminal the deliverable already exits through — same machinery, richer output. There is one construction site per gear surface, not a parallel apparatus.

**The rejected alternative: model-driven risk classification.** I also rejected asking a model to rate the turn's risk. A model-scored tier reintroduces the original problem — the thing being gated writes its own gate input — and adds latency and nondeterminism to the before-clock, which must run on every turn. The before-clock is therefore a deterministic pattern classifier: turn-head regex floors (Stage A) plus an optional mode-declared `## RISK TIER` heading (Stage B), no model call (`classify_tier`, `risk_gate.py:293`). The tradeoff is that a regex table is coarse and will misclassify novel phrasings. I accept that and mitigate it two ways: the tier is a *floor* that fails safe upward (the failsafe tier on any classifier error is `high-risk`, `risk_gate.py:67,337`), and the whole Stage-A pattern table is flagged in-code as PROVISIONAL and un-calibrated (`risk_gate.py:20,211`). It is retunable, not settled.

**Catalog versus contract — the distinction that keeps a green run honest.** The deepest design commitment sits at the boundary between what is landed and what is not. A *catalog* (`.ora/evidence.yaml`, Phase 5) says what checks *exist* in a repo. A task-specific *Evidence Contract*, produced at planning time, says what checks *this task* must pass. The distinction is load-bearing: a green evidence run means nothing you knew to check broke — it does not mean the right problem was solved. Conflating "all declared checks passed" with "the task succeeded" is the same category error as the fluent producer claim, one level up. The architecture keeps them separate so that an empty evidence-lane list is treated as *not sufficient* — evidence owed — rather than vacuously passed (`all_evidence_sufficient`, `execution_packet.py:97-105`). This is the honest-by-construction stance; it is also, as the next section makes plain, the part that is designed and implemented-in-a-worktree but not merged and not installed.

**Open problems.** The `enforcement_model` label — which says whether a gate actually constrained the action or merely observed it — is hardcoded `in_harness` in the packet builder with an explicit TODO (`execution_packet.py:352-360`). It is honest for a core in-harness gear turn and wrong for any orchestrated, opaque-shell, or MSI-custom path that later produces a packet; correct enumeration is owned by a future phase. The declared-versus-observed output-type consistency note is wired end-to-end but dormant, because no mode declares an `output_type` — it stays `unknown` on every live turn (`consistency_note`, `execution_packet.py:155-174`). And the risk-tier floors are provisional. None of these are latent bugs; they are named incompletions.

### Implementation

The subsystem is three modules plus two shared substrates, wired at two lockstep seams. I will braid the why into the how.

**Phase 1 — recorder, manifest, gate (`orchestrator/tool_events.py`, gate wired at `dispatcher.py:735`).** Three things share this module deliberately, because they share one lifecycle — the always-on instrumentation substrate. First, the *capability manifest*: three orthogonal axes layered on top of the dispatcher's existing function axis without touching it — `mutability` (`read` / `reversible_write` / `external_write` / `irreversible`), `sensitivity` (`public` / `private` / `sensitive` / `secret`), `egress` (`none` / `local` / `external`) — plus an `enforcement_model` label and the fail-closed default for unknown actions (`tool_events.py:87-106`). Second, the *recorder*: `record()` writes one JSON line per action invocation, mechanically, at the boundary — never from narration. It writes to the active pipeline-trace turn directory when a turn is live, else to the global sink (`data/tool-events.jsonl`, resolved via `runtime_paths`). The write uses `os.open` with `O_APPEND` for atomic concurrent appends (`tool_events.py:563`). Recording is two-layer redacted: a per-path sensitivity check plus a content scrub, and a secret-sensitivity event is reduced to existence-only (`_redact_for_record`, `tool_events.py:463`). Third, the *gate*: `gate()` (`tool_events.py:836`) runs before execution and independently of the permission mode. A blocked action is denied immediately and queued to the oversight Paused queue; approval mints a one-shot token that lets exactly one re-issued matching call through (`grant_approval` / `check_and_consume_approval`, `tool_events.py:610,738`). At `dispatcher.py:735` the call site invokes `tool_events.gate(...)` and, when `decision.allowed` is false, returns `decision.message` — the model gets a gate refusal string, not the tool's effect.

The failure contract here is the reliability spine: recording is best-effort and never raises, but is never silent — a failed write sets a telemetry-incomplete flag that is stamped onto every subsequent event and surfaced via `get_telemetry_health()` (`tool_events.py:422-454`); failures also ride the oversight bus through `_note_failure`. Gate decisions fail closed regardless of whether their recording or queueing succeeded. `ORA_TOOL_EVENTS=off` disables the *write* for test suites — but the docstring is explicit and the code enforces it: the gate still runs and still fails closed even with recording off (`record` docstring and guard, `tool_events.py:508-520`). This is the component that prevents the auto-approve-carries-a-dangerous-action failure mode. The failure surface is qualitative and only partly instrumented: a write failure is counted and flagged (telemetry-incomplete), but there is no success-rate or per-step reliability figure — none is instrumented, and the guarantee is the named contract (never-raises, never-silent, fail-closed), not a measured number.

**Phase 2/3 — tier, hold, criteria, after-clock fold (`orchestrator/risk_gate.py`).** The before-clock is `classify_tier` / `assign_tier` (`risk_gate.py:293,710`): Stage A matches turn-head regex floors seeded from the gate vocabulary (bash irreversible flags, `curl`/`wget` egress), Stage B reads an optional mode `## RISK TIER` heading; the higher floor wins, irreversible over external-write, with no model call. An `irreversible`-tier turn is *held before the executor runs* unless a valid task token is present — `evaluate_hold` (`risk_gate.py:814`) returns a plain-text pause reply and writes a durable Paused card, and it fails closed to a hold on any error. This is the hard boundary: post-hoc routing is permitted only in reversible tiers, so an irreversible action is stopped before it happens, not audited after. `apply_criteria` (`risk_gate.py:935`) runs an acceptance-criteria pass for standard-and-above tiers, failing closed to `WARN` (standard) or `HOLD` (high-risk and above) rather than silently dropping to light execution.

The after-clock is `fold_route_observed` (`risk_gate.py:548`), which reads the tool-event log scoped by `conversation_id` and turn timestamp and produces the observed signals: `any_mutation` and `max_mutability` (Phase 2's signal 1), and `source_read_suspected` with its channels and public-safe candidate reads (Phase 3's signal 2 — did the output make claims that imply it read a source it did not disclose). `record_route_observed` (`risk_gate.py:983`) is the terminal recorder that writes the divergence. Note one subtle threshold: `source_read_suspected` is driven partly by whether the output is *substantive*, gated on `_MIN_SUBSTANTIVE_OUTPUT_CHARS = 24`, and the candidate list is capped at `_MAX_SOURCE_CANDIDATES = 64` — both flagged PROVISIONAL in-code (`risk_gate.py:114-128`).

**The single-fold contract.** `record_route_observed` folds the log *once* and returns the signals; the terminal then builds the packet *separately* from those returned signals. There is no second fold. Any refactor that folds again double-counts mutations — this is called out as a drift class and it is worth internalizing before touching either seam.

**Phase 4 — lanes, packet, renderer (`orchestrator/execution_packet.py`).** `route_lanes` (`execution_packet.py:174`) takes the already-folded signals and routes them into `EvidenceLane` / `JudgmentLane` instances. `EvidenceLane.sufficient` is tri-state — `None` means unfilled, which means evidence-owed, which means *not* sufficient; `True`/`False` only once a runner judges it (`execution_packet.py:65-77`). `JudgmentLane` structurally omits the verdict field. `build_execution_packet` (`execution_packet.py:322`) consumes the folded signals plus the in-gear text-review verdict threaded on `context_pkg['execution_review']` and assembles a polymorphic `ExecutionPacket` with reserved blocks for fields a later phase fills. `construct_and_write` (`execution_packet.py:436`) writes `execution-packet.json` trace-local.

**The two wiring seams, in lockstep.** The before-clock and after-clock are invoked at both primary gear terminals. In `boot.py` (foreground/terminal): `assign_tier` (~9279), `evaluate_hold` (~9289), `apply_criteria` (~9304), `record_route_observed` (~9296, ~9416), `construct_and_write` (~9429), plus the task-gate reply handler (~9123). In `server/app.py` (background/server): the same sequence (~2837 / 2845 / 2857 / 2852 / 2968 / 2980, task-gate reply ~3113). The two seams must stay in lockstep — a change to one that is not mirrored in the other silently splits foreground and background behavior. (`server.py` carries additional worker and dirty-state entrypoints, ~3264 and ~3737, that wire the same functions; when auditing coverage, grep the function names, not the offsets — the line numbers here are at 7a5e8f40 and drift with edits.)

**Config surfaces.** `ORA_TOOL_EVENTS=off` (disable recording, gate still fires); `ORA_RISK_TIER` and the `/risk <tier>` sticky-plus-override and `## RISK TIER` mode heading (tier control); `ORA_STEALTH_CONTEXT` (stealth suppression at write, with gate decisions exempted so a blocked action is still logged existence-only, `tool_events.py:524-541`); `config/mcp-servers.json` axes and `config/capabilities.json` slots (the manifest source). The manifest fail-closed default and the PROVISIONAL tuning constants are the surfaces a tuner will touch first.

**Normal-operation vs failure behavior, summarized.** On a normal turn: tier assigned, no hold, tools gated per-call, log written, after-clock folds, packet written trace-local.
