On a gate block: the tool never runs, the refusal string returns, the block is queued and logged. On an irreversible turn: held before execution with a Paused card. On any recording failure: work proceeds, telemetry-incomplete flag set, health surface reflects it. On any classifier error: failsafe to `high-risk`. Nothing in this subsystem raises into the user's turn; everything degrades to safe-and-noted. These are contract guarantees, not measured reliability rates — no success-rate is instrumented.

### Evidence & Verification

The subsystem carries 206 dedicated unit tests: `test_tool_events.py` (52), `test_risk_gate.py` (93), `test_risk_gate_pipeline.py` (6), `test_execution_packet.py` (55), counts confirmed at 7a5e8f40. The portability retrofit added `test_portability.py`, now 65 tests, of which PR #181 contributed +17 Windows-simulation cases.

The landing gate is a full-suite parity diff, not a green-checkmark. Each phase ran the entire test suite and compared a sorted list of FAIL/ERROR names against a recorded environmental baseline of 22 failures and 5 errors (`baseline-failures.txt`, 27 lines), requiring *zero new* failures — the same 22F/5E set, name-for-name. PR #181's own commit message records the result at 7a5e8f40: 3738 tests, 22F/5E identical to baseline, zero new. This is the right discipline for landing instrumentation into a large tree: you prove you broke nothing, against a known-imperfect starting point, rather than pretending the tree was green.

**How to confirm the gate is live.** `dispatcher.py:735` calls `tool_events.gate` and returns `decision.message` when the decision is not allowed — a blocked call visibly returns a gate string instead of a tool result. The fail-closed default is confirmable at `tool_events.py:101-106`. The single-fold contract is confirmable by reading `record_route_observed` (returns signals) against the terminal's separate `construct_and_write` call.

**Explicitly untested surfaces — stated plainly.** `render_for_review` and `_format_artifact_for_review` are TESTED-BUT-UNWIRED: the docstring says so, and it is true — no live verifier consumes a packet. They are proven only by a test that feeds their output into the evaluator prompt shape (`execution_packet.py:14-30,236-243`). The evidence-first review the architecture exists *for* does not run in production; only the packet is built and written trace-local. The §6 consistency note is dormant on every live turn because `output_type` is `unknown` — a doc must not imply misroute detection is active; it is exercised only in tests. The evidence lanes are never sufficient, because the runner that fills them is not merged (below). And every Windows and Linux behavior is *simulation*-tested via `test_portability.py` — `ntpath`, `PureWindowsPath`, `os.name` monkeypatching, subprocess env assertions — and has never been run on a native Windows or Linux host. Simulation coverage is real coverage of the branching logic; it is not evidence the code runs off-Mac.

**The Phase-5 boundary, stated exactly.** Phases 1–4 are landed on `~/ora` main (current tip 7a5e8f40, PR #181). Phase 5 — `evidence_runner.py`, the `.ora/evidence.yaml` catalog reader, live sandboxed check execution, the planning-stage Evidence Contract, the dirty-state modes — is **implemented in an unmerged worktree at the code-review gate; it is not committed, not merged to `~/ora` main, and not installed.** The HANDOFF's top block records it as "DESIGN APPROVED + IMPLEMENTED 2026-07-04," implemented in `~/ora-worktrees/phase5-impl` on branch `execution-review-phase5-impl` off `82553162`, with its own `test_evidence_runner.py` (58 tests); the HANDOFF's explicit standing instruction is "NO commit until [the code-review re-review] approves." Its Evidence-Contract wiring at the planning seam is described in the worktree's own material as flag-gated (`ORA_EVIDENCE_CONTRACT`, default OFF) in one revision and as running unconditionally in a later revision — an unresolved internal detail of unmerged code, not installed behavior either way. I verified that `orchestrator/evidence_runner.py` is absent from the pinned tree at 7a5e8f40 (`git ls-files` at that commit returns nothing). (Phase 5 has since merged post-pin as PR #185, one commit past 7a5e8f40, which adds `evidence_runner.py`; this document describes the system AT 7a5e8f40, where it is not present.) So on the *installed* system, Execution-Review coverage ends at Phase 4. The Phase-5 runner vocabulary is pre-seeded into the landed tree — `EVIDENCE_RUNNER_DEFAULTS` (`tool_events.py:983`), `validate_check_declaration` (`tool_events.py:994`), and `evidence.yaml` in `_PROTECTED_BASENAMES` (`tool_events.py:267`) — but I confirmed these have *zero non-test consumers* in the landed code. They are a landed vocabulary with no landed caller. Do not read `phase5-impl.diff` or the `phase5-impl/` worktree as installed behavior.

### Expert Reading Notes

Inspect these first, in order, because this is where a confident reader goes wrong.

**The implemented-versus-landed line.** Before believing any Phase-5 claim, grep the landed tree for consumers of `EVIDENCE_RUNNER_DEFAULTS` and `validate_check_declaration` — there are none outside tests — and confirm `orchestrator/evidence_runner.py` is absent from git history. The runner exists only in an unmerged, uncommitted worktree at the code-review gate. Internalize the catalog-versus-contract distinction: a green evidence run means nothing you knew to check broke, not that the right problem was solved. A reviewer who forgets this will over-trust the very packet the architecture built to be honest about its own limits.

**The two lockstep seams.** `boot.py` (~9123–9432) and `server.py` (~2837–3113, plus the ~3264 and ~3737 worker/dirty-state entrypoints) both wire the before-clock/after-clock/packet sequence. They must move together. A change to one seam that is not mirrored in the other silently diverges foreground (terminal) from background (server) turns — the exact race the chokepoint design was meant to eliminate, reintroduced at the wiring layer. Grep function names, not line offsets; the offsets moved between 82553162 and 7a5e8f40 and will move again.

**The MSI-custom-gear bypass.** MSI's custom gear paths (`msi_run_gear4`, `article_generator`, `backfill`, `gear3_orchestrator`) build their own prompts and do not flow through the `run_pipeline` terminals. Risk tiers and packets therefore do not reach MSI-authored turns. This is a known drift class documented in the project's own convention notes; do not document Execution-Review coverage as universal without checking whether a given surface actually crosses one of the wired seams.

**The reversible-flag gotcha — do not "fix" it.** The `reversible=(tier != 'irreversible')` flag is the post-hoc-routing gate, not a consequence measure. Post-hoc routing is permitted in reversible tiers; irreversible is held before the executor. So `high-risk == reversible` is *correct* — a high-risk turn is dangerous but routable-after, while only irreversible is held-before. A reader who "corrects" high-risk to non-reversible breaks the routing model.

**The enforcement_model hardcode.** `build_execution_packet` hardcodes `enforcement_model: "in_harness"` with a TODO (`execution_packet.py:352-360`). It is honest for a core in-harness gear turn. The moment an orchestrated, opaque-shell, or MSI path produces a packet, that label is wrong until the future-phase enumeration lands. This is where the next change most likely introduces a false claim.

**The PROVISIONAL surfaces.** The Stage-A pattern table, `DEFAULT_TIER='standard'`, `FAILSAFE_TIER='high-risk'`, the token TTLs, `_MIN_SUBSTANTIVE_OUTPUT_CHARS=24`, and `_MAX_SOURCE_CANDIDATES=64` are all flagged in-code as un-calibrated. Treat them as retunable defaults, not settled thresholds, and retune from data rather than trusting the current values.

**The namespace collision.** `server/review.py` is *not* this subsystem. It is an unrelated Phase-10 document-extraction human-review web app (localhost:5005, `data/review-queue/`) that imports none of `tool_events` / `risk_gate` / `execution_packet`. The name collides with "Execution Review"; the code has nothing to do with it. Do not wire the two together, and do not cite `server/review.py` as evidence of anything here.

Finally, the primary architecture map lags the code: `CLAUDE.md` has no dedicated Execution-Review section. The authoritative sources are the landed code and the `~/ora-worktrees` spec and HANDOFF. Where those disagree with `CLAUDE.md`, trust the code.

### Platform compatibility

The primary tested target is macOS on Apple Silicon (M4 Max, macOS 26.3). Every non-macOS cell below is either simulation-tested or untested; none is "works" off-Mac without the qualification stated.

- **Check-execution sandbox backend (`code_execute._sandbox_backend`, `code_execute.py:40-48`).** macOS: works — `sandbox-exec` is the only implemented backend and returns `enforcement_model: orchestrated`. Windows-native: needs-porting — `_sandbox_backend` returns `None` off-darwin, so `code_execute` fail-closes/gates. WSL: needs-porting (same off-darwin path). Linux: needs-porting (same). Phase 5's Rev-2 design proposes an `ORA_EVIDENCE_SANDBOX` wrapper plus native Linux `unshare -rn`, but that is in the unmerged worktree, not installed.
- **Direct `bash_execute` argument-vector execution (`bash_execute.py`, `PreparedCommand`).** macOS: works. Windows-native / WSL / Linux: untested. The command is never passed to a shell: platform-appropriate narrow quoting is parsed into one argument vector, shell operators and expansion are refused, and the same reduced environment and executable identity used for classification are rechecked before `shell=False` spawn. Windows parsing is covered by simulation tests but has not been run on native Windows; WSL/Linux use direct process execution and do not require a POSIX shell for this path.
- **Advisory file locking on approvals and sticky JSON (`runtime_paths.locked_file`, `runtime_paths.py:100`).** macOS: works — POSIX `fcntl.flock` branch. Windows-native: untested — the `msvcrt.locking` branch exists and is guarded, but is simulation-tested only, not run native. WSL: untested. Linux: works — POSIX branch.
- **Protected-path and secret/sensitive regex matching (`tool_events._matchable` / `_cmp_key`; `runtime_paths.within_base` / `within_any_base` hardened in PR #181).** macOS: works. Windows-native: untested — forward-slash-plus-normcase folding and the boundary-anchored path helper are coded and Windows-simulation-tested (`ntpath`/`PureWindowsPath`), not run on native Windows. PR #181 fixed a real prefix-sibling boundary bug in the live file read/write gates (`dispatcher.validate_path` and `file_ops._validate_path` treated a mere-prefix sibling directory as "inside base") that was latent on POSIX and active on Windows, replacing raw `startswith(base)` with the shared boundary-anchored helper. WSL: works. Linux: works.
- **Trace-local sinks and packet paths (`record()` `os.open(O_WRONLY|O_APPEND|O_CREAT, 0o644)`, `tool_events.py:563`).** macOS: works. Windows-native: untested — `O_APPEND` is cross-platform, but the `0o644` mode argument is a no-op on Windows; not run native. WSL: works. Linux: works. All roots resolve via `runtime_paths` (no hardcoded `/Users/...` remains in the subsystem after PR #181's slash-command and server.py cleanups).
- **SCRATCH_DIR containment for sandboxed check execution (Phase 5, unmerged).** macOS: the per-run read-side re-allow is macOS-`sandbox-exec`-specific and is the only mechanism that works, and it is not landed. Windows-native: needs-porting. WSL: untested. Linux: untested. Off-Mac containment is entirely Phase-5 design-and-worktree, not installed.
- **MLX/Metal local inference** is Apple-only and sits upstream of this subsystem; Execution Review itself is pure Python and does not touch MLX, but the enclosing orchestrator is Apple-Silicon-tested and untested elsewhere.

The net statement: on the installed system at 7a5e8f40, Execution Review's enforcement (the `code_execute` sandbox) is macOS-only; its recording, gating, tiering, and packet logic are pure-Python with cross-platform *code* whose non-macOS branches are simulation-tested only. No non-macOS surface has been run native. Cross-reference the reference spec *Ora Execution Review Architecture* for the full build spec, and the platform substrate is shared with the roots-and-locking layer described in the runtime-paths portability discussion.

## 14. Operation-Matrix (Mission / Objectives / Milestones)

### Problem

A project needs a durable strategic hierarchy: a Mission that states why the work exists, Objectives that name the focal areas the work organizes around, and Milestones that are verifiable, cadence-specific checkpoints a third party can confirm. The project-management modal's "Mission & Goals" tab has to read and write that hierarchy. Three failure modes threaten it.

First, drift. If the strategic content lived in two places — a copy in the project's JSON record and the "real" one somewhere else — the two copies would diverge the moment either side is edited out of band, and the system would then have to decide which copy is authoritative. That reconciliation is a standing source of bugs: the same class as a cache that outlives the data it caches.

Second, clobbering. A project's Operation-Matrix is a hand-authored Obsidian markdown file. Beyond the three MOM sections it carries Obsidian Bases queries, a `## Problem Solving` section, a `## Spawned Activity Registry` table, and whatever else the user put there. A naive "read the fields, rewrite the file" edit destroys every one of those sibling sections. The write must touch only the bytes it owns.

Third, the vault-less environment. The canonical vault lives on the user's Mac. Cloud-ora — the headless Linux server that runs the news pipeline — has no vault at all. Any code path that reaches for the matrix on the server must degrade to "nothing here" rather than raise, or every server-side call site that touches a project inherits a crash.

This subsystem — `orchestrator/operation_matrix.py` and the `server.py` MOM endpoints — exists to give the modal a drift-free, non-clobbering, best-effort-degrading read/write layer over the MOM sections of a project's matrix file.

One boundary must be stated up front, because it is the most-confused fact about this subsystem. This is *not* the Project Plugin subsystem. The plugin/fork extensibility boundary is `project_registry.py` plus `ora-project.json` manifests — the mechanism documented in the [[Reference — Ora Project Plugin Convention]]. Operation-Matrix is strategy content only: Mission, Objectives, Milestones. The two subsystems key off the same pointer files (see `project_meta.py` below), which is exactly why they get conflated. Keep them apart.

### Design Decision & Tradeoffs

The decision: the vault markdown file is the single source of truth for MOM, and the project JSON record stores no copy of it. There is no second copy, so there is nothing to reconcile.

The matrix lives at `<vault>/Matrix/Project Matrix <Name>.md`, with Mission, Objectives, and Milestones under `## Mission`, `## Objectives`, and `## Milestones` headings (`operation_matrix.py:1-26` docstring, `MOM_HEADINGS` at line 41). The modal reads live from the file on every open and writes straight back to it. The project's pointer record (`orchestrator/project_meta.py`) carries `name`, `status`, `last_accessed_at`, and a set of inert profile slots — and no mission, objectives, or milestones field anywhere. Verify this against the code, not the docstring: `_DEFAULT_SLOTS` (`project_meta.py:42-49`) contains only `default_model_profile`, `interaction_style`, `output_style`, `persona`, `model_locks`, `private`; and `_UPDATABLE_FIELDS` (`project_meta.py:200-203`) whitelists only `name`, `status`, those same profile slots, and `last_accessed_at`. No MOM field can be written to the pointer even by accident, because the patch function filters unknown keys against that whitelist (`project_meta.py:211`). The "no drift" property rests entirely on this whitelist; if a future change adds a `mission` key to it, the property is void.

The alternative rejected: cache the MOM in the project JSON for read speed. It is the obvious optimization — the pointer file is already loaded to resolve the display name, so folding the MOM in would save a filesystem hit. It reintroduces exactly the drift the design exists to prevent, and it violates the vault-canonical rule the repository is built on: the vault is the source of truth for markdown content, and `~/ora` copies are operational, never authoritative (repo `CLAUDE.md`, "Vault Canonical Rule"). A JSON cache of vault prose is a second authority for the same bytes. That is the bug, not the feature.

Writes are section-targeted splices, not rewrites. `_replace_section` (`operation_matrix.py:195-206`) finds the region between a target `## H` heading and the next `## ` heading (or end of file) via `_section_bounds` (lines 143-158), and replaces only that region. Every other byte — frontmatter, Bases queries, sibling sections — is carried through untouched by string slicing: `text[:head_start] + block + text[section_end:]`. A missing MOM section is *inserted*, not appended blindly: `_insert_index` (lines 177-192) places it to keep Mission → Objectives → Milestones order and puts the whole MOM block ahead of any non-MOM section. This is the "leave every other byte untouched" guarantee, and it is the direct answer to the clobbering failure mode.

The tradeoff accepted: no read cache. Every `mom` GET re-reads and re-parses the file from disk. When the `Project Matrix <Name>.md` naming convention does not match, resolution falls back to a scan of `Matrix/*.md`, reading the first 2 KB of each file and parsing its frontmatter for a `nexus` match (`resolve_matrix_path`, `operation_matrix.py:108-133`). At project-modal interaction rates — a human clicking a tab — this is free. At large project counts it is a linear scan on every unmatched lookup. There is no instrumentation on the scan, so there is no measured project-count ceiling, only the shape of the cost: fine at the rates it runs at today, unverified at scale.

Open problems, stated plainly:

- **`_safe_name` does not sanitize Windows-illegal characters.** It strips only `\` and `/` (`operation_matrix.py:59-62`). A project display name containing `:`, `*`, `?`, `"`, `<`, `>`, or `|` produces an invalid `Project Matrix <Name>.md` filename on NTFS. Latent porting bug; harmless on macOS/Linux/WSL, where only `/` is illegal.
- **The map lags the code.** Neither the repo `CLAUDE.md` nor the user's global instructions mention `operation_matrix.py`, the MOM endpoints, or the mom-assist feature. The only provenance is the `G1.33 sub-step 5` tags in the docstrings, which point at an install-overhaul working doc, not the canonical map. A completeness pass flags this subsystem as missing for that reason: it is real, tested, and wired, but undocumented in the richest map. That is a documentation-parity gap, not a code gap.

### Implementation

Five endpoints in `server.py` expose the engine; all of them are thin, and the engine holds the logic.

**Read path.** `GET /api/projects/<nexus>/mom` (`server.py:4540-4565`) resolves a display name — from the `?name=` query param, else by reading the project pointer via `project_meta.read_project_meta` — then calls `operation_matrix.read_mom(nexus, name)`. `read_mom` (`operation_matrix.py:274-297`) resolves the matrix path, reads the file, and extracts each section. It returns both a parsed `milestones` list and the raw `milestones_raw` markdown. The parse (`_parse_tasks`, lines 216-228) turns `- [ ]` / `- [x]` task lines into `{text, done, indent}` dicts; the raw string is returned alongside so the modal can offer checkbox editing when the section is clean task lines and fall back to raw-text editing when it is not. This is a deliberate dual return: structure where structure exists, raw where it does not.

**Resolution.** `resolve_matrix_path` (`operation_matrix.py:108-133`) tries the naming convention first (`Project Matrix <safe-name>.md`), and only if that misses does it scan `Matrix/*.md` for a file whose frontmatter `nexus` field contains the nexus. The nexus `general` is explicitly rejected before the scan (line 124) — the Commons is synthetic and has no matrix file. Frontmatter parsing uses PyYAML with a graceful `ImportError` fallback to `{}` (`operation_matrix.py:36-39`, `_split_frontmatter` lines 69-89), so a runtime missing PyYAML degrades to "no frontmatter matched" rather than crashing.

**Write path.** `POST /api/projects/<nexus>/mom` (`server.py:4568-4603`) accepts any of `mission`, `objectives`, `milestones` (structured list), `milestones_raw`, and `name`, and calls `write_mom` (`operation_matrix.py:353-401`). The write logic:

1. Guard: if nexus is empty or `general`, return `None` — the Commons is synthetic, no file to write (line 373). The endpoint turns that `None` into a 404.
2. Resolve the path; if missing and `create_if_missing` is true and a name is present, create the file from a template (`_create_matrix` / `_new_matrix_text`, lines 300-327) — frontmatter with `type: matrix` and today's dates, an H1 title, and the three empty MOM sections.
3. Splice each provided field via `_replace_section`. `None` leaves a section untouched. `milestones_raw` wins over structured `milestones` when both are supplied (lines 391-394).
4. Bump the `date modified` frontmatter line (`_bump_modified`, lines 330-344).
5. Write atomically and re-read, returning the fresh MOM.

**Atomic write.** `_atomic_write` (lines 347-350) writes to a `.tmp` sibling and calls `os.replace`. This prevents the torn-write failure mode: a crash mid-write leaves either the old file or the new file intact, never a half-written matrix. No advisory lock is taken here — unlike `oversight_actions.py`, which uses `fcntl`. The matrix is edited by one human through one modal at a time; `os.replace` gives atomicity of the swap, but two concurrent writers would still race on the read-modify-write. That race is untested and, at modal rates, unlikely; it is named here rather than claimed impossible.

**Best-effort degradation.** Every failure returns empty or `None`, never raises. Missing vault directory → `resolve_matrix_path` returns `None` → `read_mom` returns `_empty_mom()` (`exists: False`, empty fields, lines 263-271, path-None branch at 282-284). `OSError` on read → same empty result (lines 287-288). This is what lets cloud-ora, with no vault, call these endpoints without inheriting a crash.

**The mom-assist endpoint** — `POST /api/projects/<nexus>/mom-assist` (`server.py:4715-4787`) — is the one path that touches a model, and it is strictly read-only: it drafts suggestions into the modal's editable fields and never writes the matrix. It calls the cheap `sidebar` slot through the standard model seam (`_call_small_model_with_system`, lines 4660-4685) with a doctrine prompt (`MOM_ASSIST_SYSTEM`, lines 4616-4640) that encodes the going-concern rules from the [[Framework — Mission, Objectives, Milestones Clarification]]: Mission as a cycle-shaped Service Statement, every recurring milestone carrying a specific cadence, at least one maturity gate. The response is parsed into MISSION / OBJECTIVES / MILESTONES blocks by `_parse_mom_blocks` (lines 4688-4712), tolerant of code fences and case. The failure taxonomy is explicit and never 500: the Commons → 400 before any model call (lines 4729-4731), model unavailable → 503 (lines 4767-4770), unparseable draft after a one-shot retry → 502 (lines 4774-4777). The retry exists because small models occasionally wrap output in fences or skip a label; one retry, then give up cleanly.

**The files-index endpoint** — `GET /api/projects/<nexus>/files` (`server.py:4805-4846`) — returns a read-only index of the project's vault output folder (via `project_meta.list_project_files`), pins the Operation-Matrix file as a separate `matrix` entry, and attaches an `obsidian://open` URI to each entry via `_obsidian_uri_for` (lines 4790-4802). File management defers to Obsidian by design decision (Q2 LOCKED in the docstrings): Ora indexes and links, Obsidian edits. The sibling reveal action — `POST /api/fs/reveal` (lines 4914-4954) — is macOS-gated: it first sandboxes the path to the vault root or the Ora Exports/Resources folders (403 on escape, lines 4934-4941) and checks existence (404, line 4944), then returns 501 `reveal-in-Finder is macOS-only` on any non-Darwin platform (lines 4946-4948) before shelling out to `open -R` (line 4951).

**Config surface.** One knob: `ORA_VAULT_PATH` (or the alias `ORA_VAULT`) overrides the vault root, which otherwise defaults to `~/Documents/vault` (`vault_root`, `operation_matrix.py:44-52`). `vault_root()` is also imported by `export.py` and `nexus_rename.py`, so it is a shared primitive, not a private helper — a change to its resolution logic reaches beyond this subsystem.

### Evidence & Verification

Twenty-four tests pass across three suites, run at HEAD `7a5e8f40`:

- `test_operation_matrix.py` — 13 tests. Resolution by name and by frontmatter-nexus scan; `read_mom` on an existing file and on a missing one (returns empty); the splice-preserves-siblings invariant (`test_write_mission_preserves_rest`); milestone round-trip both structured and raw, with `milestones_raw` winning; the Commons-is-synthetic no-op guard; matrix creation from template; missing-section insertion. Tests inject a `vault=` temp directory via `tempfile.TemporaryDirectory`, so no real vault is touched.
- `test_mom_assist_endpoint.py` — 7 tests. The degradation taxonomy: unparseable → 502, model-unavailable → 503, the Commons → 400; and block parsing of MISSION/OBJECTIVES/MILESTONES. The model is mocked.
- `test_projects_mom_endpoints.py` — 4 tests. The mom GET/POST HTTP endpoints end to end.

The "no drift" property is verifiable without running anything: read `project_meta.py:42-49` and `:200-203` and confirm no MOM field appears in either `_DEFAULT_SLOTS` or `_UPDATABLE_FIELDS`. If a MOM field is ever present in either, the design's central invariant is broken.

Explicitly untested surfaces:

- **The `_bump_modified` insert-before-fence branch.** `test_bump_modified` (`test_operation_matrix.py:162-166`) uses a fixture whose frontmatter already carries a `date modified:` line (line 27, `date modified: 2025-09-12`), so it exercises the `re.sub` *replace* branch (`operation_matrix.py:336-340`). The *insert* branch — when no such line exists, inserting before the closing `---` fence (lines 342-343) — is a live code path with no test hitting it.
- **The frontmatter-nexus fallback scan at scale.** `test_resolve_by_frontmatter_nexus` covers correctness against a small fixture; behavior and cost against many `Matrix/*.md` files are not exercised.
- **All non-macOS filesystem behavior.** Every test runs on macOS against a `tempfile` directory. `os.replace` atomicity, the `~/Documents/vault` default, and `_safe_name` sanitization are unverified off-Mac.
- **Real model draft quality.** The mom-assist tests mock the model. Whether a real `sidebar`-slot draft adheres to the strict three-block OUTPUT CONTRACT is not measured — only the parse and degradation paths are tested.

### Expert Reading Notes

Read `operation_matrix.py` end to end first. It is 412 lines, self-contained, and its docstring (lines 1-26) states the three design commitments — matrix-canonical, section-targeted edit, best-effort/vault-sandboxed — that the rest of the chapter defends. Then read the endpoints at `server.py:4540-4846` plus the reveal sibling at `4914-4954`.

Before trusting the "no drift" property, open `project_meta.py` and confirm that `_DEFAULT_SLOTS` and `_UPDATABLE_FIELDS` carry no MOM field. This is the single most important architectural fact in the subsystem, and it is also the fact most likely to be silently broken by a future change: the natural instinct, when someone wants "faster MOM reads," is to add a cached field to the pointer record. That one edit voids the invariant. Watch that whitelist.

The second drift vector is the naming-convention-versus-scan split in `resolve_matrix_path`. The convention (`Project Matrix <Name>.md`) and the frontmatter-nexus scan are two resolution strategies for the same file; a project rename that updates the pointer name but not the matrix filename shifts resolution from the fast convention path to the slow scan path. It still resolves — the scan matches on frontmatter nexus, which does not change on rename — but the performance character changes with no error. When debugging "why is this project's MOM slow to load," check whether its filename still matches its display name.

Where changes most likely cause drift: `_replace_section` and `_section_bounds` are the byte-preservation core. Any edit to the heading-matching regex (`_H2_RE`, line 140) or the section-boundary logic changes what counts as "the section" and therefore what gets clobbered. The splice guarantee is a regex away from breaking. Add a preserves-siblings assertion to any test that touches those functions.

Finally, note the `_safe_name` gap (lines 59-62) if this is ever ported off macOS — it is the first thing that breaks on Windows.

### Platform compatibility

Every test in this subsystem ran on macOS against `tempfile` directories. The per-subsystem platform posture:

- **`vault_root()` default `~/Documents/vault`** (`operation_matrix.py:44-52`): macOS — works (exercised). Windows-native / WSL / Linux — untested. `os.path.expanduser` resolves `%USERPROFILE%` on Windows and `$HOME` on Linux, so the mechanism is portable in principle, but the `~/Documents/vault` default assumes a macOS-style Documents directory and has been exercised only on macOS. Overridable via `ORA_VAULT_PATH` / `ORA_VAULT`.
- **Atomic write via tmp file + `os.replace`** (`operation_matrix.py:347-350`): macOS — works (exercised). Windows-native / WSL / Linux — untested. `os.replace` is atomic within a single filesystem on all three, and no OS-specific primitive is used (no `fcntl`/`flock` here), so there is no known blocker — but it was not run off Mac.
- **`_safe_name` filename sanitization** (`operation_matrix.py:59-62`): macOS — works (exercised). WSL / Linux — works in principle (only `/` is illegal there, and it is stripped; untested). Windows-native — needs porting: strips only `\` and `/`, not the NTFS-illegal `: * ? " < > |`; a display name containing any of those yields an invalid matrix filename.
- **`obsidian://open` URI construction** (`server.py:4790-4802`): macOS — works (exercised). Windows-native / WSL / Linux — untested. The URI scheme is cross-platform (Obsidian registers it on all desktop OSes) and the code uses only `relative_to` + `urllib.quote` with no OS-specific branch, but it was verified only on macOS.
- **PyYAML frontmatter parse** (`operation_matrix.py:36-39`, `82-88`): macOS / Windows-native / WSL / Linux — no platform concern. Pure-Python with a graceful `ImportError` fallback; no OS-specific behavior.
- **Reveal-in-Finder sibling endpoint** (`server.py:4914-4954`): macOS — mac-only by design (shells out to `open -R`). Windows-native / WSL / Linux — n/a: explicitly gated, returns 501 `reveal-in-Finder is macOS-only` on any non-Darwin platform. This is not part of the core MOM engine, but it is the reveal action wired to the files-index entries this subsystem produces; a Windows or Linux port would need `explorer /select` or `xdg-open` respectively.

## 15. Documentation-Code Parity & Vault-Repo Mirror Governance

### Problem

Ora keeps its authoritative prose in two places at once, and the two copies drift the moment either is edited alone. The vault at `/Users/oracle/Documents/vault/` holds the canonical markdown — YAML frontmatter plus body. The orchestrator loads operational copies from `~/ora/` at runtime — body only, no YAML. That split is deliberate (the runtime does not want to parse Obsidian frontmatter on every mode load), but it creates a classic replication hazard: two writable replicas, no primary, no reconciliation protocol. A developer fixes a mode file in `~/ora/modes/` for fast iteration, forgets the paired vault file, and the next vault→ora sync silently overwrites the ora-side edit. Or the reverse: someone edits the vault, the runtime never re-syncs, and Ora executes a stale copy of a spec the documentation swears was updated. `~/ora/CLAUDE.md`'s first section — "Vault Canonical Rule (READ FIRST)" — exists precisely because this failure mode had already happened enough times to warrant a standing warning: "otherwise drift accumulates and the next vault → ora sync will silently overwrite your ora-side edits."

There is a second, worse failure mode than lag: documentation that describes behavior the code never implemented. A doc claims a RAG provenance score, or a verifier that edits the consolidated output, or a runtime-configuration file that was retired months ago — and the reader trusts it. This is the missing-feature class, and it is more corrosive than staleness because a stale doc is merely old while a fictional doc is actively wrong about what the system does. For a technical documentation project specifically — the deepest source of truth an engineer consults — a fictional claim is a defect that propagates into every downstream decision.

This chapter's own deliverable is subject to exactly this hazard. The technical documentation is meant to live as a vault-canonical file paired with a `~/ora/docs/` mirror. The instant that pairing exists, both copies become writable replicas of each other, and without a governing mechanism they will diverge the first time either side is edited in isolation. So the parity apparatus is not incidental to this document — it is the thing that keeps this document honest over time.

### Design Decision & Tradeoffs

The apparatus is two-tier, and the split is the central design decision. Some parity questions are mechanically checkable; most are not.

**Tier 1 — an AI-executed framework (Documentation-Code Parity, DCP).** DCP is a specification the model reads and executes, not a compiled subsystem. At this chapter's pinned implementation it could be pasted into a session or invoked by a scheduled agent. G1.10's current architectural disposition is narrower: internally caused parity work belongs to exact commit, deployment, or specification-handoff events, while a full-state semantic audit is an explicit release/audit campaign. G1.24 owns verification of any external DCP routine and the delivered full-state audit, so this chapter does not claim that an external schedule has been removed, installed, or successfully delivered. The canonical spec is [[Framework — Documentation-Code Parity]]. It has four modes: DCP-Sweep (the default pass — audit, reconcile, backfill), DCP-Audit (read-only drift report, no writes), DCP-Specify (find code with no paired spec, propose one), and DCP-Deprecate (find code that appears to serve no function). A purely mechanical differ remains insufficient for semantic parity: a byte-diff cannot determine whether documentation describes behavior the code never computes. The tradeoff remains model judgment plus reflexive Git discipline rather than a deterministic completeness proof.

**Tier 2 — a handful of narrow, deterministic Python linters** for the few parities that *are* mechanically checkable. These live in `~/ora/scripts/`: `check-capability-sync` (slot-set equality across three config/doc sources), `verify-implementation.py`'s drift-parity check (byte-equality modulo YAML for architecture files), `verify-configuration-equivalence.py` (router endpoint equivalence), `check-model-refresh-drift.py` (seed-file dirtiness guard), and `check-portability.py` (cross-platform regression guard). Each is a deterministic exit-code contract — the opposite of Tier 1's judgment-bearing sweep. Where a parity reduces to set-equality or byte-equality, a script is cheaper, faster, and more trustworthy than a model, so those parities were pulled out of DCP's semantic scope and handed to code.

**Direction-of-truth is fixed: vault→ora/site.** The vault is canonical; the `~/ora/` and site copies are downstream mirrors. This removes merge ambiguity entirely. Drift always means the copy lags the canonical, never that the two disagree and a human must adjudicate which wins. A two-way-authoritative design was not chosen because it reintroduces exactly the merge-conflict resolution problem the fixed direction eliminates.

**Historical event-driven disposition, superseded by G1.10.** The pinned implementation deferred commit/specification events in favor of manual or scheduled invocation. G1.10 reverses that architecture for internally observable causes: parity verification follows the exact commit, deployment, or specification-handoff event, and full-state work requires an explicit campaign. This is a disposition and local runtime contract, not an overclaim about external DCP delivery; G1.24 retains that verification.

**Open problems, stated honestly.** Three are load-bearing. First, the pairing convention is duplicated across three documents that must be kept consistent by hand — `~/ora/CLAUDE.md`'s Vault Canonical Rule, the DCP framework's prefix list, and the DCP Configuration's prefix list — with no automated cross-check enforcing the triangle. Second, `verify-implementation.py`'s architecture-pair table is stale (detailed below) and false-fails, so a reader who treats it as green is misled. Third, only one of the five linters has an automated call site; the rest are manual pre-commit tools, and DCP itself is a model run whose thoroughness is unmeasured. Nothing measures how much drift DCP misses — there is no instrumented drift-catch rate, and this chapter will not invent one. The named failure mode is undetected semantic drift; it is not instrumented.

### Implementation

**DCP entrypoints and data flow.** At the pinned implementation, DCP was invoked manually via `/framework documentation-code-parity` or described as a time-boxed `claude-opus-4-8` routine across five repositories. G1.10's local inventory found no active Mac DCP cron or LaunchAgent and does not treat the described external routine as verified. The current local contract is exact event handoff or explicit campaign; G1.24 must verify the external routine and delivered full-state result. A run proceeds through layers. Layer 0 is reflexive and runs *before* the parity check: it commits and pushes any uncommitted Ora-related changes the framework itself produced, and updates the registry, so the check operates against the most current state rather than a dirty tree. Layers 1 through 8 then run scope/config load (from [[Reference — Documentation-Code Parity Configuration]]), inventory and pairing, semantic drift detection, classification, auto-fix application with Decision Log entries, escalation-queue construction, registry description backfill, and post-flight commit-push-report.

**Historical schedule shape.** The pinned design distinguished a diff-driven daily run from a Monday full-state run as a cost governor. That description is retained as provenance, not evidence of current delivery. Under G1.10 the diff path follows exact events and the full-state path is an explicit campaign; G1.24 must establish the external as-built state.

**Outputs** land in `~/Documents/vault/Administration/DCP/` (relocated there from the vault root on 2026-06-11): dated Drift Reports, a Decision Log, an Escalation Queue, and a Run History. A delivery invariant added 2026-06-17 defines success precisely: a run is complete only when its records land on the vault's *active* branch. A run that commits only to an anonymous Claude branch is **delivery-blocked**, not complete — this is the failure mode the invariant prevents, where a scheduled agent does all the work but strands it on a branch nobody merges. The routine must either push to the active branch's tracking remote or open a clearly named PR the user can merge intentionally.

**The pairing convention** is prefix-driven. `~/ora/CLAUDE.md`'s Vault Canonical Rule enumerates it per subdirectory: `~/ora/frameworks/book/<name>.md` pairs with `Framework — <Title>.md` (vault has YAML, ora does not); `~/ora/modes/<name>.md` with `Modes/<name>.md` (both have YAML); `~/ora/lenses/<name>.md` with `Lenses/<name>.md`; and critically `~/ora/architecture/<name>.md` with `Reference — <Title>.md`. That last subdirectory (Decision K) holds the eight pre-routing-pipeline files the orchestrator reads at runtime, and it carries a load-bearing exception: `signal-vocabulary-registry.md` pairs with `Registry — Signal Vocabulary Registry.md` — the `Registry —` prefix, not `Reference —` — because it functions as a registry, not a reference document. The same section records that `runtime-configuration.md` was retired on 2026-05-12 when Decision C was reversed; its runtime fields moved into each mode file's `## DEFAULT GEAR` and `## RAG PROFILE` sections. These two facts — the Registry-prefix exception and the retired runtime-configuration pair — are exactly where the stale linter goes wrong, so hold them in mind.

**The five Tier-2 linters, each named by the drift it catches:**

*`check-capability-sync`* (372 lines) prevents capability-slot drift — the failure where the code's slot set, the router's slot set, and the vault's contract doc disagree about which capabilities exist. It verifies that `~/ora/config/capabilities.json`, `~/ora/config/routing-config.json`'s `slots` block, and `Reference — Capability Invocation Contracts.md` agree on the slot set, each slot's `execution_pattern`, its required inputs, and its error codes. Exit 0 clean, 1 on drift (output lists every disagreement), 2 on missing/malformed input. It is the only linter with an automated call site.

*`verify-implementation.py`* (drift-parity check, `--check drift`) prevents architecture-file drift. `check_drift_parity` (line 415) walks `ARCHITECTURE_PAIRS` (defined line 106), strips the vault YAML via a frontmatter parser, normalizes trailing/leading whitespace, and byte-compares the vault body against the ora file — reporting the first divergent line number for actionable feedback. The comparator logic is sound. Its data is stale (see Evidence).

*`verify-configuration-equivalence.py`* (104 lines) prevents router-config divergence — the failure where a migrated configuration file would route a slot to a different endpoint than the live `Router` does. It imports `orchestrator.router.Router`, and for each of 15 `(context, config-file, slot, gear, cell-path)` checks across the `interactive` (`user-pipeline.json`) and `agent` (`background-default.json`) pipelines, compares `Router.resolve_endpoint`'s returned endpoint id against the `primary` in the corresponding configuration cell. Exit 0 on full equivalence, 1 on any mismatch. Manual invocation only — no wired call site.

*`check-model-refresh-drift.py`* (47 lines) prevents seed-file contamination — the failure where a live model-refresh run dirties tracked seed config in the working tree instead of writing under `data/runtime/`. It shells out to `git status --porcelain` over eight watched paths (`config/model-catalog.json`, `config/model-registry.json`, `config/routing-config.json`, the three preset configs `free`/`budget`/`premium`, and two data JSONL changelogs) and returns 1 if any are dirty, pointing the operator at `scripts/promote-runtime-model-state.py --apply` for intentional seed updates. Manual only.

*`check-portability.py`* (336 lines) is the porting-regression guard — the safety net for the Windows/Linux effort, preventing macOS-only constructs from silently landing. It walks the repo (skipping binaries, `vendor/`, `.git`, and friends) and applies 15 regex rules across severity bands: eight CRITICAL (`pkill`, `/opt/homebrew/bin/python3`, `osascript`, `lsregister`, `iconutil`, `pmset`, `launchctl`, `pbcopy`/`pbpaste`), four HIGH (`pgrep`, `/tmp/`, `~/Library`, `/Applications/`), two MEDIUM (`/Users/oracle/` hardcodes, bare `subprocess` calls to `open`), and one LOW (a `sys.platform == 'darwin'` check reported for review, not as a bug). A separate `check_missing_files` pass adds a CRITICAL `missing-windows-launcher` finding when `start.sh`/`stop.sh` exist with no `.bat` counterpart — at 7a5e8f40 both `start.bat` and `stop.bat` ship, so this rule does not fire. An `ALLOWED_MAC_ONLY_FILES` allowlist exempts genuinely Mac-only code (the `.app` launchers, `start.sh`, `stop.sh`, `swap-icon.sh`, `publish.sh`). A `PLATFORM_GUARDED_RULES` set plus `has_platform_branching` heuristic downgrades a finding when the file already contains a `sys.platform`/`platform.system()` branch. Exit 1 on any CRITICAL. The `users-oracle-hardcode` rule exists precisely to catch the class of hardcoded absolute paths that `verify-implementation.py` itself commits.

**Normal operation** is a clean Drift Report and clean linter exits. **Failure behavior** has two named states from the Configuration's delivery contract: *delivery-blocked* (the run's records committed only to an anonymous branch, never landing on the active branch) and *coverage-degraded* (a repo in scope was unreadable, so part of the sweep is missing). Neither is a crash; both are honest partial-completion states the report must surface.

### Evidence & Verification

**DCP runs — proven live.** `~/Documents/vault/Administration/DCP/` contains 26 dated Drift Reports spanning 2026-05-18 through 2026-06-17, plus `Working — DCP Decision Log.md`, `Working — Documentation-Code Parity Escalation Queue.md`, and `Working — DCP Run History.md`. The dated cadence is direct evidence the framework runs on schedule.

**`check-capability-sync` — the one wired linter.** `orchestrator/tests/test_capability_registry.py` (line 131) runs the script as a subprocess against the shipped configs and fails the test if it exits non-zero; the same suite also injects drift — dropping the `image_generates` slot from a temp `capabilities.json` (`test_detects_missing_slot_in_capabilities`) and adding an orphan slot to a temp `routing-config.json` (`test_detects_unknown_slot_in_routing_config`) — and asserts exit 1 with the offending slot named in output. These are true negative tests, not smoke tests. This is the only parity linter with an automated call site — meaning it is the only one that cannot silently rot, because a real drift breaks CI-equivalent test runs. One caveat: the test hardcodes `/opt/homebrew/bin/python3` as the subprocess interpreter (line 132), so the test itself is Apple-Silicon-coupled even though the script under test derives its paths from `Path.home()`.

**`check-model-refresh-drift.py` — exit 1 on the author's live machine, and that is expected.** A clean checkout of 7a5e8f40 is clean, so the script exits 0 on the committed tree. On the author's running machine, four of the eight watched seed files are dirty in the working tree (`config/model-catalog.json`, `config/model-registry.json`, `config/routing-config.json`, and `data/model-catalog-changes.jsonl`) from an in-progress model refresh, so the script exits 1 there. This is not a code bug — it is the script correctly reporting live working-tree drift, which is machine-local and not part of the commit. A reader must not mistake this exit-1 for a defect.

**The stale linter — `verify-implementation.py:106`.** This is the single most important thing to inspect, and it is genuinely broken. `ARCHITECTURE_PAIRS` still lists nine pairs, two of which no longer match reality:

- It includes `("Reference — Mode Runtime Configuration.md", "runtime-configuration.md")` — but both files were retired on 2026-05-12 when Decision C was reversed, as documented in `~/ora/CLAUDE.md`. `check_drift_parity` hits the `if not vault_path.exists()` branch (line 423) and false-fails with "Vault file missing."
- It lists `("Reference — Signal Vocabulary Registry.md", "signal-vocabulary-registry.md")` — but the real vault file carries the `Registry —` prefix per the CLAUDE.md exception, so this too false-fails on "Vault file missing."

Anyone running `verify-implementation.py --check drift` and treating a non-zero exit as real drift is being misled by two false positives against a comparator whose logic is otherwise correct. The `check_drift_parity` docstring and the `ARCHITECTURE_PAIRS` comment both still say "9 pairs," compounding the mismatch. The fix is to reconcile the table to the eight live pairs CLAUDE.md enumerates — drop the retired `runtime-configuration` pair, and correct the signal-vocabulary entry to `Registry — Signal Vocabulary Registry.md`. Until then, this script's drift check is stale, not trustworthy.

**Untested surfaces — stated plainly.** There is no CI: `.github/workflows/` does not exist. There are no git hooks: `.git/hooks/` holds only the default `.sample` files. Four of the five linters are therefore manual-only — they run when a human remembers to run them. I did not execute the linters live in this pass; their exit-code behavior is asserted from reading the code plus the one observed dirty-tree state, not from a run. DCP's semantic-comparison depth is unverifiable except by re-reading each Drift Report. And DCP-Specify and DCP-Deprecate appear unexercised in production — the `Administration/DCP/` directory holds only Sweep/Audit-shaped Drift Reports plus the Decision Log, Run History, and Escalation Queue; no Deprecation Candidates artifact is present. Those two modes are specified but partial.

**The technical-documentation pairing now exists, and this document is half of it.** The vault canonical `Reference — Ora Technical Documentation.md` is committed to the vault, and its repo mirror `~/ora/docs/technical-documentation.md` was landed on `ora-commons/ora` main (PR #183) with a body byte-identical to the vault file. The parity rule is stated in this document's Appendix C (the vault is the source of truth; the mirror is regenerated from it). Registration followed at Gate 7 (post-pin): the pair is declared in the three places DCP actually reads to build its pairing map — the pairing-convention block in `~/ora/CLAUDE.md`, the DCP framework's Layer 2 pairing rules, and the DCP Configuration's pairing rules. (The `Reference —` prefix was already in the canonical-prefix lists; what Gate 7 added is the per-file vault↔mirror pairing a prefix list cannot express.) A DCP sweep now knows to check the pair.

### Expert Reading Notes

**Inspect first: `verify-implementation.py:106`.** The `ARCHITECTURE_PAIRS` table is the highest-value target in this whole subsystem because it is broken *and* silently trusted. Reconcile it against `~/ora/CLAUDE.md`'s enumerated eight-file list: drop the retired `runtime-configuration` pair, and correct the signal-vocabulary entry to `Registry — Signal Vocabulary Registry.md`. Update the "9 pairs" comment and docstring to eight while you are there. A green treatment of this script today is a false green.

**The three-place pairing triangle is the top drift zone.** The pairing/prefix convention is stated in three documents that must stay consistent by hand: `~/ora/CLAUDE.md`'s Vault Canonical Rule, the DCP framework's prefix list ([[Framework — Documentation-Code Parity]]), and the DCP Configuration's prefix list ([[Reference — Documentation-Code Parity Configuration]]). No script enforces their mutual consistency — the parity apparatus does not check its own governing convention. Any new prefix, any new pairing, must be added to all three or the triangle skews. This is the meta-drift the system cannot self-detect.

**Registering the technical-documentation pairing — done at Gate 7.** The pair `Reference — Ora Technical Documentation.md` ↔ `~/ora/docs/technical-documentation.md` is registered in all three pairing locations (the `~/ora/CLAUDE.md` pairing block, the DCP framework's Layer 2 pairing rules, and the DCP Configuration's pairing rules), so a DCP sweep includes it in scope; the two derived installed-system docs (accessible overview, how-to guide) were registered the same way in the same pass. The pairs were deliberately NOT added to `verify-implementation.py`'s `ARCHITECTURE_PAIRS` table, which is itself stale (see above) — feeding a new pair to a broken comparator only inherits the breakage; mechanical byte-parity is instead confirmed directly (strip the vault YAML plus the leading blank line, then `diff` against the mirror) at each sync.

**`check-model-refresh-drift.py` exit-1 under active refresh is expected.** Do not file a bug when this exits 1 mid-refresh; watched files dirty is the normal signature of an in-progress model-catalog refresh. The script is working as designed — it fires so a refresh does not accidentally commit seed changes.

**Where future changes most likely cause drift.** Adding a site (the Configuration is the single edit-point by design — do it there), changing a prefix (must touch the triangle), or introducing the technical-doc pairing without registering it in all three lists. The scheduled routine's 2026-08-01 disable date is itself a governance cliff: after that date, parity reverts to on-demand unless someone re-schedules, and drift that accumulated silently will only surface at the next manual sweep.

### Platform compatibility

Every parity script was written and run on macOS (Apple M4 Max, macOS 26.3). The apparatus is Mac-native in several places, and the off-Mac surfaces are untested — I say so where I did not run them. I did not execute any linter off-Mac at 7a5e8f40; every "works" below that is not macOS is downgraded to "untested" accordingly.

- **`verify-implementation.py` — hardcoded absolute paths.** Lines 47–48 set `VAULT_ROOT = Path("/Users/oracle/Documents/vault")` and `ORA_ROOT = Path("/Users/oracle/ora")`. **macOS: works.** **Windows-native: needs-porting** (hardcoded POSIX absolute paths, user-specific). **WSL / Linux: untested** — the paths would only resolve on a machine with that exact user layout. `check-portability.py`'s own `users-oracle-hardcode` MEDIUM rule is designed to flag exactly this file.
- **`check-capability-sync` and `check-model-refresh-drift.py` — `Path.home()`/repo-relative.** Both derive paths from the home directory or the repo root plus `git status` rather than hardcoded `/Users/oracle/`. **macOS: works.** **WSL / Linux: untested but likely portable** — pure stdlib plus a `git` subprocess with no `shell=True`, forward-slash paths git normalizes. **Windows-native: untested** — `git` on PATH would satisfy the subprocess, but this was never run there.
- **Homebrew Python interpreter hardcode.** `verify-configuration-equivalence.py` and `check-capability-sync` usage docstrings invoke `/opt/homebrew/bin/python3`, and `test_capability_registry.py:132` hardcodes it as the subprocess interpreter. **macOS (Apple Silicon): works.** **Windows-native / WSL / Linux: needs-porting** — that prefix is the Apple-Silicon Homebrew path; other platforms need `py -3` or `python3` from PATH. `check-portability.py` classifies this exact string as a CRITICAL `homebrew-python-hardcode` finding elsewhere in the tree.
- **`check-portability.py` itself — the porting safety net.** Pure Python stdlib (`os.walk`, `re`, `pathlib`), no shell-out. **macOS: works.** **WSL / Linux: untested** — its dependency surface is platform-neutral stdlib, so it should run, but I did not run it off-Mac at 7a5e8f40. **Windows-native: untested.** It is designed to be run *on* the porting host, so this is the one script whose off-Mac execution is the point.
- **`verify-configuration-equivalence.py` — imports the live Router.** Beyond the Homebrew interpreter note above, the script imports `orchestrator.router.Router`, so its portability is bounded by the orchestrator's. **macOS: works.** **WSL / Linux / Windows-native: untested** — inherits whatever platform constraints the router module carries.
- **DCP framework git operations (5 repos).** The framework is model-executed and environment-agnostic as a specification; git is invoked through whatever session harness runs it. **macOS: works** (the live scheduled routine runs there). **Windows-native / WSL / Linux: untested** — the spec makes no shell-specific assumption, but the scheduled routine's repo-clone auth is environment-bound (all five remotes must be authorized in the routine's environment per the Configuration's 2026-06-17 git-remotes note; prompt-level clone instructions are insufficient if the routine lacks repo access).
- **DCP output paths and vault filename case-sensitivity.** Outputs write under `~/Documents/vault/Administration/DCP/`. **macOS: works** (APFS is case-insensitive by default). **Linux / WSL: untested and case-sensitive-risky** — vault filenames use a spaced em-dash (` — `) and mixed-case prefixes (`Reference —`, `Registry —`, `Framework —`); these survive on case-insensitive APFS but must match exactly on case-sensitive ext4, where a prefix-case mismatch (the very `Reference —`/`Registry —` confusion that stales `verify-implementation.py`) becomes a hard file-not-found rather than a soft lookup. **Windows-native: needs-porting** (tilde-home path plus reserved-character concerns).
